\HUGE Classifying Types
Egbert Rijke

Chapter 0
Introduction

The study of homotopy theoretic phenomena in the language of type theory
[
33
]
is
sometimes loosely called ‘synthetic homotopy theory’
[
8
]
.
Homotopy theory in type theory
[
4
]
is only one of the
many aspects of homotopy type theory, which also includes the study of the
set theoretic semantics (models of homotopy type theory and univalence in a
meta-theory of sets or categories
[
3
,
5
,
7
,
19
,
32
,
35
]
), type theoretic semantics (internal models of homotopy type
theory), and computational semantics
[
2
]
, as well as the study of various questions
in the internal language of homotopy type theory which are not necessarily
motivated by homotopy theory, or questions related to the development of
formalized libraries of mathematics based on homotopy type theory.
This thesis concerns the development of synthetic homotopy theory.

Homotopy type theory is based on Martin-Löf’s theory of dependent types
[
25
]
, which
was developed during the 1970’s and 1980’s.
The novel additions of homotopy type theory are Voevodsky’s univalence axiom
[
36
,
37
]
,
and higher inductive types
[
21
,
31
,
33
]
. The univalence axiom characterizes the identity
type on the universe, and establishes the universe as an object classifier
[
29
]
.
Higher inductive types are a generalization of inductive types, in which both
point constructors (generators) and path constructors (relations) may be specified.
A simple class of higher inductive types, which includes most known higher inductive
types, are the homotopy pushouts. When the universe is assumed to be closed
under homotopy pushouts, it is also closed under homotopy coequalizers
[
33
]
,
sequential colimits
[
33
]
, and propositional truncation
[
15
]
.
For instance, one way of obtaining the
n
-spheres
[
22
]
using homotopy pushouts is by setting
\mathbb{S}^{-1}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{0}
and
by inductively defining the
(n+1)
-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n+1}
to be the pushout
of the span
\mathbf{1}\leftarrow\mathbb{S}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{1}
.
Then we can attach
(n+1)
-cells to a type
X
.
Let
f:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}B(a))\to X
be a family of attaching maps
for some
\mathbb{S}^{n}
-bundle
B:A\to\mathrm{BAut}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, where
\mathrm{BAut}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}\|\mathbb{S}^{n}\simeq X\|
of types merely equivalent to the
n
-sphere. We
attach the
(n+1)
-disks indexed by
A
to
X
by taking the homotopy pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}B(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

In this thesis we study dependent type theory with univalent universes that are closed under homotopy pushouts, further developing the program set out in
[
33
]
on synthetic homotopy theory. We will assume in this dissertation that every type family is classified by a univalent universe, and that all universes are closed under homotopy pushouts, as well as under the usual type constructors including identity types,
\Pi
- and
\Sigma
-types, and a natural numbers object. The model in cubical sets by Coquand et al.
[
7
]
is a constructive model for this setup, although the fact that it is closed under homotopy pushouts is currently unpublished. Furthermore, we will exclusively work with objects that can be described either as (dependent) types, or as their terms. In other words, the objects of our study are all ‘formalizable’ in the sense that they can be encoded in computer implementations of our setup of dependent type theory. Existing implementations of dependent type theory, supporting univalent universes and homotopy pushouts, with libraries developing synthetic homotopy theory, include the proof assistants Agda
[
26
]
, Coq
[
13
,
6
]
, and Lean
[
16
]
, and parts of the material in this theses are formalized in each of them.

1
Overview per chapter

In
Chapter

1
we will first establish notation, although we will mostly follow the notation from
[
33
]
1
1

1

A difference of practice between
[
33
]
and this thesis is that
[
33
]
doesn’t use the label ‘Proposition’ for any results at all, whereas we use propositions for statements that might be of independent interest, but are not our main theorem. In this thesis, the label ‘Theorem’ is reserved for the main results that were originally established in my thesis research, and is therefore used much more sparingly.
. Furthermore, we recall some of the most basic facts of homotopy type theory, and then we will show that the univalence axiom establishes any universe as an object classifier.

In
Chapter

2
we will first recall the most basic properties of homotopy pushouts, and then we will proceed to prove the descent property for pushouts, using the univalence axiom. Note that the coherence problem of type theory plays a role in this chapter: we would much rather have shown that the descent property holds for any homotopy colimit, but this task requires the definition of an internal
\infty
-category. Some solace will be offered in
Chapter

6
, where we will prove a descent property for any modality.

In
Chapter

3
we will study reflexive graphs and, most importantly, reflexive coequalizers in type theory. We take interest in reflexive graphs, because the topos of reflexive graphs (over sets) is cohesive over the topos of sets. The left-most adjunction is the reflexive coequalizer which is left adjoint to the discrete functor. We will construct the reflexive coequalizer as a pushout, so it exists under our assumptions, and we know in some cases how to compute them into previously known operations. Here we run again into the limitation of homotopy type theory, because we cannot establish the type of reflexive graphs as the type of objects of an
\infty
-category, and neither can we establish the reflexive coequalizer as an
\infty
-functor. However, to state and prove that the reflexive coequalizer satisfies the universal property of the left adjoint of the discrete functor, we only need composition of reflexive graph morphisms, and the action on morphisms of the operation equipping a type with the structure of a discrete graph. The universal property is indeed sufficient for many purposes. We then introduce the notion of fibrations for reflexive graphs, and show that the fibrations are right orthogonal to the same maps as the discrete reflexive graphs: morphisms between representables. It follows that a morphism is a fibration if and only if it is cartesian. Then we proceed to prove the descent property of reflexive coequalizers. We also note that diagrams over reflexive graphs are just left fibrations of reflexive graphs, so we also obtain a descent theorem for diagrams over graphs. Of particular interest in the present work is the descent property for sequential colimits. The contents of this chapter are joint work with Bas Spitters.

In
Chapter

4
we will show that any function factors as a surjective function followed by an embedding, even though we only assume that the universe is closed under pushouts and the basic type constructors. Of course, it is of essential importance here that the universe contains a natural numbers object. Our construction of the image of a map proceeds by iteratively taking the fiberwise join of a map with itself, so we call it the join construction. It follows from our construction that the image of a map from an essentially small type into a locally small type (notions that are explained in
Chapter

1
), is again essentially small. The join construction can be used to construct the quotient of a type by a
\mathsf{Prop}
-valued equivalence relation (i.e. an equivalence relation in the usual sense), and it can be used to construct the Rezk completion of a pre-category. The construction of set-quotients includes the construction of the set truncation, and the construction of the Rezk completion includes the construction of the
1
-truncation, and of Eilenberg-Mac Lane spaces of the form
K(G,1)

[
23
]
. Following
[
23
]
, the Eilenberg Mac-Lane spaces
K(G,n)
for
G
abelian and
n\geq 2
can be constructed once we have constructed the
k
-truncation for any
k\geq-2
. We note that Eilenberg-Mac Lane spaces are important classifying spaces in higher group theory, and so are the connected components of the universe (which we show to be essentially small).

In
Chapter

5
we consider general reflective subuniverses. Examples that we have at our disposal at this point are the
(-2)
-,
(-1)
-,
0
-, and
1
-truncations. Most examples of reflective subuniverses are obtained by localization at a family of maps. However, since the only assumed homotopy colimits are pushouts, we will construct localizations only in
Chapter

7
, and only of families between compact types, because we need more theory in order to establish the necessary basic results. Thus, in
Chapter

5
we focus on general reflective subuniverses. We show that for any reflective subuniverse
L
, the subuniverse
L^{\prime}
of
L
-separated types (i.e. types whose identity types are
L
-types) is again reflective. It follows at once that the subuniverse of
k
-truncated types is reflective, for any
k\geq-2
. Furthermore, we will study several classes of maps related to a reflective subuniverse. First of all, we study the
L
-equivalences, i.e. the maps that become an equivalence by the functorial action of
L
, and second of all we study the
L
-connected maps, i.e. the maps with fibers that become trivial after applying
L
. Clearly, any
L
-connected map is also an
L
-equivalence, but the converse is one of the many characterizations of
L
being lex given in
[
28
]
.
Furthermore, we study modalities. One of the characterizations of modalities is as reflective subuniverses that are
\Sigma
-closed, but we provide three more equivalent definitions of modalities. One particularly important alternative definition is that of a stable orthogonal factorization system, i.e. a pair
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
of two classes of maps such that every map factors as an
\mathcal{L}
-map followed by a
\mathcal{R}
-map; the class
\mathcal{L}
is left orthogonal to the class
\mathcal{R}
; and the pullback of an
\mathcal{L}
-map is again a
\mathcal{L}
-map. For any modality
{\ocircle}
, the stable orthogonal factorization system associated to it consists of the
{\ocircle}
-connected maps as the
\mathcal{L}
-maps, and the
{\ocircle}
-modal maps as the
\mathcal{R}
-maps. A final topic for this chapter is the notion of accessibility for reflective subuniverses and accessible modalities.

The contents from this chapter are selected from
[
28
]
and
[
12
]
. I began to study reflective subuniverses with Mike Shulman and Bas Spitters, and continued to study them with my MRC teammates Morgan Opie and Luis Scoccola, under the lead of Dan Christensen.

In
Chapter

6
we recall from
[
38
]
the notion of
{\ocircle}
-étale map for an arbitrary modality
{\ocircle}
. We prove a modal version of the descent theorem, which asserts that a maps into
{\ocircle}X
from a
{\ocircle}
-modal type are equivalently described as étale maps into
X
. The
{\ocircle}
-étale maps form the right class of a second orthogonal factorization system associated to any modality: the
reflective
factorization system. The left class of this factorization system is the class of
{\ocircle}
-equivalences. Using this factorization system we obtain that the universal cover of a type
X
at a point
x_{0}
is the left-right-factorization of the map
\mathbf{1}\to X
.
We then proceed to study the étale maps for the modality of discrete reflexive graphs. Note that the type of all reflexive graphs isn’t exactly a universe, so it is not possible to directly apply our previous observations about
{\ocircle}
-étale maps. Nevertheless, most arguments are practically the same. Thus, we treat our section on
{\ocircle}
-étale maps as a blue-print for our section of
\Delta
-étale maps, and do most arguments a second time. Such is the current state of homotopy type theory. What we get out is a generalized flattening lemma, which states that for any morphism
f:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
factors uniquely as an
\Delta
-equivalence followed by a fibration of graphs. We use this to show that the loop space of the suspension of a pointed type
X
is the free H-space
G
with a base-point preserving map
X\to_{\ast}G
. The generalized flattening lemma also applies to diagrams over reflexive graphs, and in particular to sequential colimits. Moreover, the equifibrant replacement can be constructed by a telescope construction. We show in this chapter that sequential colimits commute with
\Sigma
- and identity types, and therefore also with pullbacks. In particular, the sequential colimit operation sends sequences of fiber sequences to fiber sequences. Moreover, we show that sequential colimits commute with
k
-truncation for all
k\geq-2
, from which it follows that sequential colimits commute with
\pi_{k}
for any
k\geq 0
. We expect to be able to use these results also in showing that the spectrification of a pre-spectrum is indeed a spectrum, but we haven’t done that yet.

The idea of a modal version of the descent theorem first arose in unpublished work on reflexive graphs with Bas Spitters, in the spring of 2016. However, I only learned about
{\ocircle}
-étale maps much later from Felix Wellen, and many of the results presented in
Section

1
came out of a discussion I had with Felix Wellen and Mike Shulman, who also brought my attention to the reflective factorization system of a modality, of which the classical case is due to
[
11
]
.
The material in
Section

3
on the equifibrant replacement operation on reflexive graphs is joint work with Bas Spitters.
The material in
Section

4
on sequential colimits is joint work with Floris van Doorn and Kristina Sojakova
[
14
]
, and all the results concerning sequential colimits are formalized in the proof assistant Lean.

In
Chapter

7
we introduce the notion of (sequentially) compact types, in order to provide an application for the results in
Chapter

6
. The most important basic result about compact types is that they are closed under pushouts, and in proving this fact we use that sequential colimits commute with pullbacks.
Our main purpose here, and the final main result of this dissertation, is to show that for any family
f
of maps between compact types, the subuniverse of
f
-local types is reflective, thus providing a fairly large class of reflective subuniverses including types localized away from a prime
[
12
]
. It should be noted that all subuniverses of
f
-local types are reflective if enough higher inductive types are assumed. Furthermore, our result about
\omega
-compact types should in principle hold for
\kappa
-compact types for cardinals larger than
\omega
. However, we restrict to the case of
\omega
-compact types since we do not have a good theory of such larger cardinals available in homotopy type theory, while the natural numbers object is right there (by assumption).

Chapter 1
The object classifier

In this chapter we establish notation, and we highlight the basic results concerning fiberwise transformations and fiberwise equivalences, which we will use for the descent theorems
2.9
and
4.11
. Of particular importance are the following theorems:

(i)

The Fundamental Theorem of Identity Types (
2.5
), which establishes that a type family
B
over
A
with
b:B(a)
for a given
a:A
is fiberwise equivalent to the identity type
a=x
if and only if its total space is contractible. This result appears in
[
33
]
as Theorem 5.8.2, which contains other equivalent conditions as well.

(ii)

Section

3
, in which we establish that for any fiberwise map

g:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}P(x)\to Q(f(x)),

the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}Q(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}_{f}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
\mathsf{tot}_{f}(g)
is defined as
{\lambda}(x,p).\,(f(x),g(x,y))
, is a pullback square if and only if
g
is a fiberwise equivalence. As a consequence, we obtain that a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is a pullback square if and only if the induced fiberwise transformation

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(h(x))

is a fiberwise equivalence. Our main reference
[
33
]
does not present many results of homotopy pullbacks, although the material we present about homotopy pullbacks is surely well-known. The connection between pullbacks and fiberwise equivalences has an important role in the descent theorem in
Chapter

2
, which is why we devote a section to this result.

(iii)

6.5
, in which we establish that the universe is an object classifier. This result appears in
[
29
]
as Theorem 2.31, and in
[
33
]
as Theorem 4.8.4

1
Notation and preliminary results

We work in Martin-Löf dependent type theory with
\Pi
-types,
\Sigma
-types and cartesian products, coproducts
A+B
equipped with
{\mathsf{inl}}:A\to A+B
and
{\mathsf{inr}}:B\to A+B
for any two types
A
and
B
, an empty type
\mathbf{0}
, a unit type
\mathbf{1}
equipped with
\star:\mathbf{1}
, a type
\mathbf{2}
of booleans equipped with
{1_{\mathbf{2}}},{0_{\mathbf{2}}}:\mathbf{2}
, a type
\mathbb{N}
of natural numbers equipped with
0:\mathbb{N}
and
\mathsf{succ}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, and identity types.

Remark 1.1
.

As usual, we write
\mathsf{id}_{A}:A\to A
for the
identity function

{\lambda}x.\,x
on
A
, and we write
g\circ f:A\to C
for the
composite function

{\lambda}x.\,g(f(x))
of
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to C
. For any two types
A
and
B
, and any
b:B
, we write

\mathsf{const}_{b}:A\to B

for the
constant function

{\lambda}x.\,b
. Sometimes we also write
{\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,b
for the constant function.

In the case of
\Sigma
-types, the empty type
\mathbf{0}
, and the unit type, we use the following notation to define functions by pattern-matching:

\displaystyle{\lambda}(x,y).\,f(x,y)

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{t:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)}P(t)

\displaystyle{\lambda}\star.\,y

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{t:\mathbf{1}}P(t).

For instance, the first and second projection maps

\displaystyle\mathsf{pr}_{1}

\displaystyle:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))\to A

\displaystyle\mathsf{pr}_{2}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{p:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)}B(\mathsf{pr}_{1})

are defined as
\mathsf{pr}_{1}\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}(x,y).\,x
and
\mathsf{pr}_{2}\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}(x,y).\,y
.
We use similar notation for definitions by iterated pattern-matching. For instance, given a dependent function
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}{y:B(x)}{z:C(x,y)}P((x,y),z)
we obtain the function

{\lambda}((x,y),z).\,f(x,y,z):{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{t:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))}}}{\sum_{(s:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))}}{\sum_{(s:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))}}{\sum_{(s:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))}}C(s)}P(t).

Given a type
A
in context
\Gamma
, the
identity type
of
A
at
a:A
is the inductive type family

\Gamma,x:A\vdash a=_{A}x\penalty\ \mathrm{type}

with constructor

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{refl}_{a}:a=_{A}a.

The induction principle for the identity type of
A
at
a
asserts that for any type family

\Gamma,x:A,\alpha:a=_{A}x\vdash P(x,\alpha)\penalty\ \mathrm{type}

there is a term

\mathsf{ind}_{a=}:P(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a})\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}{\alpha:a=_{A}x}P(x,\alpha)

in context
\Gamma
, satisfying the computation rule

\mathsf{ind}_{a=}(p,a,\mathsf{refl}_{a})\equiv p.

A term of type
a=_{A}x
is also called an
identification
of
a
with
x
, or a
path
from
a
to
x
.
The induction principle for identity types is sometimes called
identification elimination
or
path induction
. Occasionally, we also write
\mathsf{Id}_{A}
for the identity type on
A
.

Moreover, we assume that there is a universe
\mathcal{U}
with a universal family
\mathrm{El}
over
\mathcal{U}
, that is closed under the type forming operations. For example, there is a map

\check{\mathsf{Id}}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathrm{El}(A)\to\mathrm{El}(A)\to\mathcal{U}

satisfying

\mathrm{El}(\check{\mathsf{Id}}(A,x,y))\equiv(x=_{\mathrm{El}(A)}y),

establishing that the universe is closed under identity types.

Given a type
A
the
concatenation
operation

\mathsf{concat}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y,z:A}(x\mathbin{{=}}y)\to(y\mathbin{{=}}z)\to(x\mathbin{{=}}z)

is defined by
\mathsf{concat}(\mathsf{refl}_{x},q)\vcentcolon\equiv q
. We will usually write
p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q
for
\mathsf{concat}(p,q)
.
The concatenation operation satisfies the unit laws

\displaystyle\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}(p)

\displaystyle:\mathsf{refl}_{x}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p=p

\displaystyle\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}unit}(p)

\displaystyle:p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{refl}_{y}=p.

The
inverse operation

\mathsf{inv}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}(x=y)\to(y=x)

is defined by
\mathsf{inv}(\mathsf{refl}_{x})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{x}
. We will usually write
p^{-1}
for
\mathsf{inv}(p)
.
The inverse operation satisfies the inverse laws

\displaystyle\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}inv}(p)

\displaystyle:p^{-1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p=\mathsf{refl}_{y}

\displaystyle\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}inv}(p)

\displaystyle:p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p^{-1}=\mathsf{refl}_{x}.

The
associativity operation
, which assigns to each
p:x=y
,
q:y=z
, and
r:z=w
the
associator

\mathsf{assoc}(p,q,r):(p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}r=p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}r)

is defined by
\mathsf{assoc}(\mathsf{refl}_{x},q,r)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.3014pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}r}
.

Given a map
f:A\to B
, the
action on paths
of
f
is an operation

\mathsf{ap}_{f}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}(x\mathbin{{=}}y)\to(f(x)\mathbin{{=}}f(y))

defined by
\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({\mathsf{refl}_{x}}\right)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{f(x)}
.
Moreover, there are operations

\displaystyle\mathsf{ap\underline{\ }{}id}_{A}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}{p:x\mathbin{{=}}y}p\mathbin{{=}}\mathsf{ap}_{\mathsf{id}_{A}}\left({p}\right)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ap\underline{\ }{}comp}(f,g)

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}{p:x\mathbin{{=}}y}\mathsf{ap}_{g}\left({\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({p}\right)}\right)\mathbin{{=}}\mathsf{ap}_{g\circ f}\left({p}\right)

defined by
\mathsf{ap\underline{\ }{}id}_{A}(\mathsf{refl}_{x})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{\mathsf{refl}_{x}}
and
\mathsf{ap\underline{\ }{}comp}(f,g,\mathsf{refl}_{x})\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{\mathsf{refl}_{g(f(x))}}
, respectively.
It can be shown easily that the action on paths of a map preserves the groupoid operations, and that the groupoid laws are also preserved.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
A
be a type, and let
B
be a type family over
A
. The
transport
operation

\mathsf{tr}_{B}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}(x\mathbin{{=}}y)\to(B(x)\to B(y))

is defined by
\mathsf{tr}_{B}(\mathsf{refl}_{x})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{id}_{B(x)}
.

Definition 1.3
.

Given a dependent function
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}B(a)
and a path
p:x\mathbin{{=}}y
in
A
, the
dependent action on paths

\mathsf{apd}_{f}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}{p:x=y}\mathsf{tr}_{B}(p,f(x))\mathbin{{=}}f(y)

is defined by
\mathsf{apd}_{f}\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{refl}_{x}\right)\mathclose{}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{f(x)}
.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
f,g:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}P(x)
be two dependent functions. The type
f\sim g
of
homotopies
from
f
to
g
is defined as

f\sim g\vcentcolon\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}f(x)=g(x).

Commutativity of diagrams is stated using homotopies. For instance, a triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is said to commute if it comes equipped with a homotopy
H:f\sim g\circ h
, and a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is said to commute if it comes equipped with a homotopy
H:h\circ i\sim f\circ g
.

The reflexivity, inverse, and concatenation operations on homotopies are defined pointwise.
We will write
H^{-1}
for
{\lambda}x.\,H(x)^{-1}
, and
H\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}K
for
{\lambda}x.\,H(x)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}K(x)
.
These operations satisfy the groupoid laws (phrased appropriately as homotopies). Apart from the groupoid operations and their laws, we will occasionally need
whiskering
operations and the naturality of homotopies.

Definition 1.5
.

We define the following
whiskering
operations on homotopies:

(i)

Suppose
H:f\sim g
for two functions
f,g:A\to B
, and let
h:B\to C
. We define

h\cdot H\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{ap}_{h}\left({H(x)}\right):h\circ f\sim h\circ g.

(ii)

Suppose
f:A\to B
and
H:g\sim h
for two functions
g,h:B\to C
. We define

H\cdot f\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}x.\,H(f(x)):h\circ f\sim g\circ f.

We will frequently make use of commuting cubes. The commutativity of a cube is stated using the whiskering operations on homotopies.

Definition 1.6
.

A
commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{111}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{110}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{101}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{011}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{100}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{010}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{001}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{000},}

consists of

(i)

types

A_{111},A_{110},A_{101},A_{011},A_{100},A_{010},A_{001},A_{000},

(ii)

maps

\displaystyle f_{11\check{1}}

\displaystyle:A_{111}\to A_{110}

\displaystyle f_{\check{1}01}

\displaystyle:A_{101}\to A_{001}

\displaystyle f_{1\check{1}1}

\displaystyle:A_{111}\to A_{101}

\displaystyle f_{01\check{1}}

\displaystyle:A_{011}\to A_{010}

\displaystyle f_{\check{1}11}

\displaystyle:A_{111}\to A_{011}

\displaystyle f_{0\check{1}1}

\displaystyle:A_{011}\to A_{001}

\displaystyle f_{1\check{1}0}

\displaystyle:A_{110}\to A_{100}

\displaystyle f_{\check{1}00}

\displaystyle:A_{100}\to A_{000}

\displaystyle f_{\check{1}10}

\displaystyle:A_{110}\to A_{010}

\displaystyle f_{0\check{1}0}

\displaystyle:A_{010}\to A_{000}

\displaystyle f_{10\check{1}}

\displaystyle:A_{101}\to A_{100}

\displaystyle f_{00\check{1}}

\displaystyle:A_{001}\to A_{000},

(iii)

homotopies

\displaystyle H_{1\check{1}\check{1}}

\displaystyle:f_{1\check{1}0}\circ f_{11\check{1}}\sim f_{10\check{1}}\circ f_{1\check{1}1}

\displaystyle H_{0\check{1}\check{1}}

\displaystyle:f_{0\check{1}0}\circ f_{01\check{1}}\sim f_{00\check{1}}\circ f_{0\check{1}1}

\displaystyle H_{\check{1}1\check{1}}

\displaystyle:f_{\check{1}10}\circ f_{11\check{1}}\sim f_{01\check{1}}\circ f_{\check{1}11}

\displaystyle H_{\check{1}0\check{1}}

\displaystyle:f_{\check{1}00}\circ f_{10\check{1}}\sim f_{00\check{1}}\circ f_{\check{1}01}

\displaystyle H_{\check{1}\check{1}1}

\displaystyle:f_{\check{1}01}\circ f_{1\check{1}1}\sim f_{0\check{1}1}\circ f_{\check{1}11}

\displaystyle H_{\check{1}\check{1}0}

\displaystyle:f_{\check{1}00}\circ f_{1\check{1}0}\sim f_{0\check{1}0}\circ f_{\check{1}10},

(iv)

and a homotopy

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle:(f_{\check{1}00}\cdot H_{1\check{1}\check{1}})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}((H_{\check{1}0\check{1}}\cdot f_{1\check{1}1})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(f_{00\check{1}}\cdot H_{\check{1}\check{1}1}))

\displaystyle\qquad\sim(H_{\check{1}\check{1}0}\cdot f_{11\check{1}})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}((f_{0\check{1}0}\cdot H_{\check{1}1\check{1}})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{0\check{1}\check{1}}\cdot f_{\check{1}11}))

filling the cube.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a function. We say that
f
has a
section
if there is a term of type

\mathsf{sec}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}}{\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}f\circ g\sim\mathsf{id}_{B}.

Dually, we say that
f
has a
retraction
if there is a term of type

\mathsf{retr}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}}{\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}h\circ f\sim\mathsf{id}_{A}.

If
f
has a retraction, we also say that
A
is a
retract
of
B
.

Definition 1.8
.

We say that a function
f:A\to B
is an
equivalence
if it has both a section and a retraction, i.e. if it comes equipped with a term of type

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{sec}(f)\times\mathsf{retr}(f).

We will write
A\simeq B
for the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}}{\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}{\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}{\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
.

Clearly, if
f
is
invertible
in the sense that it comes equipped with a function
g:B\to A
such that
f\circ g\sim\mathsf{id}_{B}
and
g\circ f\sim\mathsf{id}_{A}
, then
f
is an equivalence. We write

\mathsf{has\underline{\ }{}inverse}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}}{\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(g:B\to A)}}(f\circ g\sim\mathsf{id}_{B})\times(g\circ f\sim\mathsf{id}_{A}).

The section of an equivalence is also a retraction (and vice versa), so we define the
inverse
of an equivalence to be its section. It follows immediately that the inverse of any equivalence is again an equivalence. The identity function
\mathsf{id}_{A}
on a type
A
is an equivalence since it is its own section and its own retraction.

It is straightforward to show that for any two functions
f,g:A\to B
, we have

(f\sim g)\to(\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)\leftrightarrow\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(g)).

Given a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with
H:f\sim g\circ h
, we have:

(i)

If the map
h
has a section, then
f
has a section if and only if
g
has a section.

(ii)

If the map
g
has a retraction, then
f
has a retraction if and only if
h
has a retraction.

(iii)

(The
3-for-2 property
for equivalences.) If any two of the functions

f,\qquad g,\qquad h

are equivalences, then so is the third.

In the following theorem we characterize the identity type of a
\Sigma
-type as a
\Sigma
-type of identity types.

Proposition 1.9

(Theorem 2.7.2 of
[
33
]
)
.

Let
B
be a type family over
A
, let
s:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)
, and consider the dependent function

\mathsf{pair\underline{\ }{}eq}_{s}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{t:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)}(s=t)\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\alpha:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}}{\sum_{(\alpha:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}{\sum_{(\alpha:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}{\sum_{(\alpha:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}\mathsf{tr}_{B}(\alpha,\mathsf{pr}_{2}(s))=\mathsf{pr}_{2}(t)

defined by
\mathsf{pair\underline{\ }{}eq}_{s}(\mathsf{refl}_{s})\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{refl}_{\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)},\mathsf{refl}_{\mathsf{pr}_{2}(s)})
. Then
\mathsf{pair\underline{\ }{}eq}_{s,t}
is an equivalence for every
t:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)
.

We include the proof mainly to introduce some more notation.

Proof.

The maps in the converse direction

\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}pair}_{s,t}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(t))}}\mathsf{tr}_{B}(p,\mathsf{pr}_{2}(s))\mathbin{{=}}\mathsf{pr}_{2}(t)\Big)\to(s\mathbin{{=}}t)

is defined by

\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}pair}_{(x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}(\mathsf{refl}_{x},\mathsf{refl}_{y})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{(x,y)}.

The proofs that the function
\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}pair}_{s,t}
is indeed an inverse of
\mathsf{pair\underline{\ }{}eq}_{s,t}
are also by induction.
∎

Definition 1.10
.

We say that a type
A
is
contractible
if there is a term of type

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(c:A)}}}{\sum_{(c:A)}}{\sum_{(c:A)}}{\sum_{(c:A)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}c=x.

Given a term
(c,C):\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)
, we call
c:A
the
center of contraction
of
A
, and we call
C:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}a=x
the
contraction
of
A
.

Suppose
A
is a contractible type with center of contraction
c
and contraction
C
. Then the type of
C
is (judgmentally) equal to the type

\mathsf{const}_{c}\sim\mathsf{id}_{A}.

In other words, the contraction
C
is a
homotopy
from the constant function to the identity function.

Definition 1.11
.

Consider a type
A
with a base point
a:A
. We say that
A
satisfies
singleton induction
if for every type family
B
over
A
, the map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pt}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\Big)\to B(a)

given by
f\mapsto f(a)
has a section.

Proposition 1.12
.

A type
A
is contractible if and only if it satisfies singleton induction.

Example 1.13
.

By definition the unit type
\mathbf{1}
satisfies singleton induction, so it is contractible.

Remark 1.14
.

For any family
P:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)\to\mathcal{U}
there is a map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{t:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)}P(t)\Big)\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}{y:B(x)}P(x,y)

that evaluates
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{t:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)}P(t)
at pairs
(x,y)
. In other words,
\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}
is defined by
{\lambda}f.\,{\lambda}x.\,{\lambda}y.\,f(x,y)
. By the induction principle for
\Sigma
-types, this map has a section. It is easy to show that
\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}
is in fact an equivalence.

Similarly, there is a map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}{p:a=x}B(x,p)\Big)\to B(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a})

given by
{\lambda}f.\,f(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a})
, for any type family
B:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to\mathcal{U}
. By path induction, this map has a section, and again it is easy to show that this map is in fact an equivalence.

Proposition 1.15

(Lemma 3.11.8 in
[
33
]
)
.

For any
x:A
, the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:A)}}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}x=y

is contractible.

Proof.

We have the term
(x,\mathsf{refl}_{x}):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:A)}}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}x=y
, and both maps in the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{t:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:A)}}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}x=y}B(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:A}{p:x=y}B((y,p))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B((x,\mathsf{refl}_{x}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}}

have sections, so the composite has a section. The composite is
\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pt}
, so we see that the asserted type satisfies singleton induction.
∎

Definition 1.16
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a function, and let
b:B
. The
fiber
of
f
at
b
is defined to be the type

\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}f(a)=b.

Example 1.17

(Lemma 4.8.1 of
[
33
]
)
.

Consider a type family
B
over
A
. Then the map

B(a)\to\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(a)

given by
b\mapsto((a,b),\mathsf{refl}_{a})
is an equivalence. In other words, the fibers of the projection function
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\big)\to A
are just the fibers of the family
B
.

Definition 1.18
.

We say that a function
f:A\to B
is
contractible
if there is a term of type

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)).

We cite Chapter 4 of
[
33
]
for the following result, although it is well-known that it can be proven directly and without the use of function extensionality.

Proposition 1.19

(Chapter 4 in
[
33
]
)
.

A function is an equivalence if and only if it is contractible.

2
The Fundamental Theorem of Identity Types

Consider a family

f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\to C(x)

of maps. Such
f
is also called a
fiberwise map
or
fiberwise transformation
.

Definition 2.1

(Definition 4.7.5 of
[
33
]
)
.

We define the map

\mathsf{tot}(f):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}C(x).

by
{\lambda}(x,y).\,(x,f(x,y))
.

Lemma 2.2

(Theorem 4.7.6 of
[
33
]
)
.

For any fiberwise transformation
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\to C(x)
, and any
a:A
and
c:C(a)
, there is an equivalence

\mathsf{fib}_{f(a)}(c)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{tot}(f)}({\mathopen{}(a,c)\mathclose{}}).

Example 2.3
.

There are equivalences

\mathsf{fib}_{(\mathsf{ap}_{f})_{x,y}}(q)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\delta_{f}}((x,y,q))

for any
q:f(x)=f(y)
, because the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:A)}}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}x=y}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:A)}}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}f(x)=f(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}x.\,(x,x,\mathsf{refl}_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(\mathsf{tot}(\mathsf{ap}_{f}))}

commutes, and the top map is an equivalence.

Proposition 2.4

(Theorem 4.7.7 of
[
33
]
)
.

Let
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\to C(x)
be a fiberwise transformation. The following are logically equivalent:

(i)

For each
x:A
, the map
f_{x}:B(x)\to C(x)
is an equivalence. In this case we say that
f
is a
fiberwise equivalence
.

(ii)

The map
\mathsf{tot}(f):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}C(x)
is an equivalence.

The following theorem is the key to many results about identity types, which we will use instead of the
encode-decode method
of
[
24
]
. We refer to it as the
Fundamental Theorem of Identity Types
.

Theorem 2.5

(Theorem 5.8.2 of
[
33
]
)
.

Let
A
be a type with
a:A
, and let
B
be a type family over
A
with
b:B(a)
.
Then the following are logically equivalent:

(i)

The canonical family of maps

\mathsf{ind}_{a{=}}(b):{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x)

is a fiberwise equivalence.

(ii)

The total space

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)

is contractible.

Proof.

By
Section

2
it follows that the fiberwise transformation
\mathsf{ind}_{a{=}}(b)
is a fiberwise equivalence if and only if it induces an equivalence

\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}a=x\Big)\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)

on total spaces. We have that
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}a=x
is contractible. Now it follows by the 3-for-2 property of equivalences, applied in the case

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}a=x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(\mathsf{ind}_{a{=}}(b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

that
\mathsf{tot}(\mathsf{ind}_{a{=}}(b))
is an equivalence if and only if
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)
is contractible.
∎

Observe that in the proof of
2.5
we haven’t used the actual definition of the fiberwise transformation. Indeed, for any fiberwise transformation

f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x)

we have that
f
is a fiberwise equivalence if and only if the total space of
B
is contractible.

Since retracts of contractible types are again contractible, it follows that the only retract of the identity type is the identity type itself:

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
a:A
, and let
B
be a type family over
A
. If each
B(x)
is a retract of
a\mathbin{{=}}x
, then
B(x)
is equivalent to
a\mathbin{{=}}x
for every
x:A
.

As a first application of the fundamental theorem we give a quick new proof that equivalences are embeddings. The proof of the corresponding theorem in
[
33
]
is more involved.

Definition 2.7
.

An
embedding
is a map
f:A\to B
satisfying the property that

\mathsf{ap}_{f}:(x\mathbin{{=}}y)\to(f(x)\mathbin{{=}}f(y))

is an equivalence for every
x,y:A
. We write
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}emb}(f)
for the type of witnesses that
f
is an embedding.

Proposition 2.8

(Theorem 2.11.1 in
[
33
]
)
.

Any equivalence is an embedding.

Proof.

Let
e:A\simeq B
be an equivalence, and let
x:A
. By
2.5
it follows that

\mathsf{ap}_{e}:(x\mathbin{{=}}y)\to(e(x)\mathbin{{=}}e(y))

is an equivalence for every
y:A
if and only if the total space

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:A)}}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}e(x)=e(y)

is contractible for every
y:A
. Now observe that
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:A)}}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}e(x)=e(y)
is equivalent to the fiber
\mathsf{fib}_{e}(e(x))
, which is contractible by
Section

1
.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

A type
A
is said to be a
proposition
if there is a term of type

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}prop}(A)\vcentcolon\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(x=y).

Furthermore, we write
\mathsf{Prop}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}prop}(X)
for the type of all small propositions.

We will often use either of the following characterizations of propositions.

Lemma 2.10

(Lemma 3.11.10 and Exercise 3.5 of
[
33
]
)
.

For any type
A
the following are equivalent:

(i)

A
is a proposition.

(ii)

A
is
proof irrelevant
in the sense that
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}x=y
.

(iii)

A\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)
.

Example 2.11
.

Any contractible type is a proposition. The empty type is a proposition by a direct application of the induction principle of the empty type. Furthermore, any retract of a proposition is again a proposition. In particular, propositions are closed under equivalences.

Lemma 2.12
.

Consider a function
f:A\to B
, and let
(a,p),(a^{\prime},p^{\prime}):\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)
for some
b:B
. Then the canonical map

((a,p)=(a^{\prime},p^{\prime}))\to\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{ap}_{f}}(p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p^{\prime-1})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

By
Section

2
it suffices to show that the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:A)}}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}{\sum_{(y:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:f(y)=b)}}}{\sum_{(q:f(y)=b)}}{\sum_{(q:f(y)=b)}}{\sum_{(q:f(y)=b)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=y)}}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=y)}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=y)}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s)=y)}}\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({p}\right)=\mathsf{pr}_{2}(s)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q^{-1}

is contractible, which is immediate by two applications of
Section

1
.
∎

Proposition 2.13

(Lemma 7.6.2 of
[
33
]
)
.

A map is an embedding if and only if its fibers are propositions.

Proof.

If
f
is an embedding, then the fibers of
\mathsf{ap}_{f}
are contractible by
Section

1
. Thus it follows by
Section

2
that the fibers of
f
are propositions.

Conversely, if the fibers of
f
are propositions, then we have by
Section

2
an equivalence

((x,p)=(y,\mathsf{refl}_{f(y)}))\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{ap}_{f}}(p)

for any
p:f(x)=f(y)
, which shows that the fibers of
\mathsf{ap}_{f}
are contractible. Thus
f
is an embedding by
Section

1
.
∎

Definition 2.14
.

A type family
B
over
A
is said to be a
subtype
of
A
if for each
x:A
the type
B(x)
is a proposition.

Corollary 2.15
.

A type family
B
over
A
is a subtype if and only if the projection map

\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)\to A

is an embedding.

Proof.

Immediate by
Sections

1
and
2
.
∎

3
Function extensionality

Proposition 3.1

(Theorem 4.9.5 of
[
33
]
)
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

The
function extensionality principle
: For every type family
B
over
A
, and any two dependent functions
f,g:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)
, the canonical map

\mathsf{htpy\underline{\ }{}eq}(f,g):(f\mathbin{{=}}g)\to(f\sim g)

by path induction (sending
\mathsf{refl}_{f}
to
{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{refl}_{f(x)}
) is an equivalence. We will write
\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}htpy}
for its inverse.

(ii)

The
weak function extensionality principle
holds: For every type family
B
over
A
one has

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(B(x))\Big)\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\Big).

From now on we will assume that function extensionality holds.

Corollary 3.2

(Theorem 7.1.9 of
[
33
]
)
.

For any type family
B
over
A
one has

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}prop}(B(x))\Big)\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}prop}\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\Big).

In particular, if
B
is a proposition, then
A\to B
is a proposition for any type
A
.

We show in this section that a map
f:A\to B
is an equivalence if and only if for any type family
P
over
B
, the precomposition map

\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:B}P(y)\Big)\to\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}P(f(x))\Big)

is an equivalence.
In the proof we use the notion of
path-split
maps, which was introduced in
[
28
]
.

Definition 3.3
.

We say that a map
f:A\to B
is
path-split
if
f
has a section, and for each
x,y:A
the map

\mathsf{ap}_{f}(x,y):(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y))

also has a section. We write
\mathsf{path\underline{\ }{}split}(f)
for the type

\mathsf{sec}(f)\times{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}\mathsf{sec}(\mathsf{ap}_{f}(x,y)).

We will also use the notion of
half-adjoint equivalences
, which were introduced in
[
33
]
.

Definition 3.4

(Definition 4.2.1 of
[
33
]
)
.

We say that a map
f:A\to B
is a
half-adjoint equivalence
, in the sense that there are

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle:B\to A

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle:f\circ g\sim\mathsf{id}_{B}

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:g\circ f\sim\mathsf{id}_{A}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:G\cdot f\sim f\cdot H.

We write
\mathsf{half\underline{\ }{}adj}(f)
for the type of such quadruples
(g,G,H,K)
.

Furthermore, we will need ‘type theoretic choice’.

Proposition 3.5

(Theorem 2.15.7 of
[
33
]
)
.

Let
C(x,y)
be a type in context
\Gamma,x:A,y:B(x)
. Then the map

\varphi:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B(x))}}}{\sum_{(y:B(x))}}{\sum_{(y:B(x))}}{\sum_{(y:B(x))}}C(x,y)\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x))}}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x))}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x))}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x))}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}C(x,f(x))\Big)

given by
{\lambda}h.\,({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{pr}_{1}(h(x)),{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{pr}_{2}(h(x)))
is an equivalence.

Corollary 3.6
.

For type
A
and any type family
C
over
B
, the map

\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}}{\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}{\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}{\sum_{(f:A\to B)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}C(f(x))\Big)\to\Big(A\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}C(x)\Big)

given by
{\lambda}(f,g).\,{\lambda}x.\,(f(x),g(x))
is an equivalence.

Proposition 3.7
.

For any map
f:A\to B
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is an equivalence.

(ii)

f
is path-split.

(iii)

f
is a half-adjoint equivalence.

(iv)

For any type family
P
over
B
the map

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:B}P(y)\Big)\to\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}P(f(x))\Big)

given by
s\mapsto s\circ f
is an equivalence.

(v)

For any type
X
the map

(B\to X)\to(A\to X)

given by
g\mapsto g\circ f
is an equivalence.

Proof.

To see that (i) implies (ii) we note that any equivalence has a section, and its action on paths is an equivalence by
Section

2
so again it has a section.

To show that (ii) implies (iii), assume that
f
is path-split. Thus we have
(g,G):\mathsf{sec}(f)
, and the assumption that
\mathsf{ap}_{f}:(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y))
has a section for every
x,y:A
gives us a term of type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{ap}_{f}}(G(f(x))).

By
Section

3
this type is equivalent to

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}g(f(x))=x)}}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}g(f(x))=x)}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}g(f(x))=x)}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}g(f(x))=x)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}G(f(x))=\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({H(x)}\right),

so we obtain
H:g\circ f\sim\mathsf{id}_{A}
and
K:G\cdot f\sim f\cdot H
, showing that
f
is a half-adjoint equivalence.

To show that (iii) implies (iv), suppose that
f
comes equipped with
(g,G,H,K)
witnessing that
f
is a half-adjoint equivalence. Then we define the inverse of
\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f
to be the map

\varphi:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}P(f(x))\Big)\to\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:B}P(y)\Big)

given by
s\mapsto{\lambda}y.\,\mathsf{tr}_{P}(G(y),sg(y))
.

To see that
\varphi
is a section of
\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f
, let
s:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}P(f(x))
. By function extensionality it suffices to construct a homotopy
\varphi(s)\circ f\sim s
. In other words, we have to show that

\mathsf{tr}_{P}(G(f(x)),s(g(f(x)))=s(x)

for any
x:A
. Now we use the additional homotopy
K
from our assumption that
f
is a half-adjoint equivalence. Since we have
K(x):G(f(x))=\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({H(x)}\right)
it suffices to show that

\mathsf{tr}_{P}(\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({H(x)}\right),sgf(x))=s(x).

A simple path-induction argument yields that

\mathsf{tr}_{P}(\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({p}\right))\sim\mathsf{tr}_{P\circ f}(p)

for any path
p:x=y
in
A
, so it suffices to construct an identification

\mathsf{tr}_{P\circ f}(H(x),sgf(x))=s(x).

We have such an identification by
\mathsf{apd}_{H(x)}\mathopen{}\left(s\right)\mathclose{}
.

To see that
\varphi
is a retraction of
\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f
, let
s:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:B}P(y)
. By function extensionality it suffices to construct a homotopy
\varphi(s\circ f)\sim s
. In other words, we have to show that

\mathsf{tr}_{P}(G(y),sfg(y))=s(y)

for any
y:B
. We have such an identification by
\mathsf{apd}_{G(y)}\mathopen{}\left(s\right)\mathclose{}
. This completes the proof that (iii) implies (iv).

Note that (v) is an immediate consequence of (iv), since we can just choose
P
to be the constant family
X
.

It remains to show that (v) implies (i). Suppose that

\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f:(B\to X)\to(A\to X)

is an equivalence for every type
X
. Then its fibers are contractible by
Section

1
. In particular, choosing
X\equiv A
we see that the fiber

\mathsf{fib}_{\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f}(\mathsf{id}_{A})\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}}{\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}{\sum_{(h:B\to A)}}h\circ f=\mathsf{id}_{A}

is contractible. Thus we obtain a function
h:B\to A
and a homotopy
H:h\circ f\sim\mathsf{id}_{A}
showing that
h
is a retraction of
f
. We will show that
h
is also a section of
f
. To see this, we use that the fiber

\mathsf{fib}_{\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f}(f)\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:B\to B)}}}{\sum_{(i:B\to B)}}{\sum_{(i:B\to B)}}{\sum_{(i:B\to B)}}i\circ f=f

is contractible (choosing
X\equiv B
).
Of course we have
(\mathsf{id}_{B},\mathsf{refl}_{f})
in this fiber. However we claim that there also is an identification
p:(f\circ h)\circ f=f
, showing that
(f\circ h,p)
is in this fiber, because

\displaystyle(f\circ h)\circ f

\displaystyle\equiv f\circ(h\circ f)

\displaystyle=f\circ\mathsf{id}_{A}

\displaystyle\equiv f

Now we conclude by the contractibility of the fiber that there is an identification
(\mathsf{id}_{B},\mathsf{refl}_{f})=(f\circ h,p)
. In particular we obtain that
\mathsf{id}_{B}=f\circ h
, showing that
h
is a section of
f
.
∎

4
Homotopy pullbacks

1
Cartesian squares

Recall that a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is said to
commute
if there is a homotopy
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
.

Definition 4.1
.

A
cospan
consists of three types
A
,
X
, and
B
, and maps
f:A\to X
and
g:B\to X
. Given a type
C
, a
cone
on the cospan
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\leftarrow}}B
with
vertex

C
consists of maps
p:C\to A
,
q:C\to B
and a homotopy
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes. We write

\mathsf{cone}(C)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:C\to A)}}}{\sum_{(p:C\to A)}}{\sum_{(p:C\to A)}}{\sum_{(p:C\to A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:C\to B)}}}{\sum_{(q:C\to B)}}{\sum_{(q:C\to B)}}{\sum_{(q:C\to B)}}f\circ p\sim g\circ q

for the type of cones with vertex
C
.

Given a cone with vertex
C
on a span
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\leftarrow}}B
and a map
h:C^{\prime}\to C
, we construct a new cone with vertex
C^{\prime}
in the following definition.

Definition 4.2
.

For any cone
(p,q,H)
with vertex
C
and any type
C^{\prime}
, we define a map

\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}(p,q,H):(C^{\prime}\to C)\to\mathsf{cone}(C^{\prime})

by
h\mapsto(p\circ h,q\circ h,H\cdot h)
.

Definition 4.3
.

We say that a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
is a
pullback square
, or that it is
cartesian
, if it satisfies the
universal property
of pullbacks, which asserts that the map

\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}(p,q,H):(C^{\prime}\to C)\to\mathsf{cone}(C^{\prime})

is an equivalence for every type
C^{\prime}
.

We often indicate the universal property with a diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

since the universal property states that for every cone
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},H^{\prime})
with vertex
C^{\prime}
, the type of pairs
(h,\alpha)
consisting of
h:C^{\prime}\to C
equipped with
\alpha:\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}((p,q,H),h)=(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},H^{\prime})
is contractible by
Section

1
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q

Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square.

(ii)

For every type
C^{\prime}
and every cone
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},H^{\prime})
with vertex
C^{\prime}
, the type of quadruples
(h,K,L,M)
consisting of

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle:C^{\prime}\to C

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:p\circ h\sim p^{\prime}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle:q\circ h\sim q^{\prime}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:(H\cdot h)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(g\cdot L)\sim(f\cdot K)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}H^{\prime}

is contractible.

Remark 4.5
.

The homotopy
M
in
Section

1
witnesses that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ p\circ h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ p^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ q\circ h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\cdot K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\cdot h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\cdot L}

of homotopies commutes.

2
The unique existence of pullbacks

Definition 4.6
.

Let
f:A\to X
and
B\to X
be maps. Then we define

\displaystyle A\times_{X}B

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}f(x)=g(y)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{pr}_{1}

\displaystyle:A\times_{X}B\to A

\displaystyle\pi_{2}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ\mathsf{pr}_{2}

\displaystyle:A\times_{X}B\to B

\displaystyle\pi_{3}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{pr}_{2}\circ\mathsf{pr}_{2}

\displaystyle:f\circ\pi_{1}\sim g\circ\pi_{2}.

The type
A\times_{X}B
is called the
canonical pullback
of
f
and
g
.

Note that
A\times_{X}B
depends on
f
and
g
, although this dependency is not visible in the notation.

Proposition 4.7

(Exercise 2.11 of
[
33
]
)
.

Given maps
f:A\to X
and
g:B\to X
, the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square.

In the following lemma we establish the uniqueness of pullbacks up to equivalence via a
3-for-2 property
for pullbacks.

Lemma 4.8
.

Consider the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with homotopies
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
and
H^{\prime}:f\circ p^{\prime}\sim g\circ q^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, suppose we have a map
h:C^{\prime}\to C
equipped with

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:p\circ h\sim p^{\prime}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle:q\circ h\sim q^{\prime}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:(H\cdot h)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(g\cdot L)\sim(f\cdot K)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}H^{\prime}.

If any two of the following three properties hold, so does the third:

(i)

C
is a pullback.

(ii)

C^{\prime}
is a pullback.

(iii)

h
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The type of triples
(K,L,M)
is equivalent to the type of identifications

\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}((p,q,H),h)=(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},H^{\prime}).

Let
D
be a type, and let
k:D\to C^{\prime}
be a map. We observe that

\displaystyle\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}((p,q,H),(h\circ k))

\displaystyle\equiv(p\circ(h\circ k),q\circ(h\circ k),H\circ(h\circ k))

\displaystyle\equiv((p\circ h)\circ k,(q\circ h)\circ k,(H\circ h)\circ k)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}(\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}((p,q,H),h),k)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}((p^{\prime},q^{\prime},H^{\prime}),k).

Thus we see that the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\to C^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\to C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cone}(D)}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},H^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}(p,q,H)}

commutes. Therefore it follows from the 3-for-2 property of equivalences that if any two of the following properties hold, then so does the third:

(i)

The map
\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}(p,q,H):(D\to C)\to\mathsf{cone}(D)
is an equivalence,

(ii)

The map
\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},H^{\prime}):(D\to C^{\prime})\to\mathsf{cone}(D)
is an equivalence,

(iii)

The map
h\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}:(D\to C^{\prime})\to(D\to C)
is an equivalence.

Thus the 3-for-2 property for pullbacks follows from the fact that
h
is an equivalence if and only if
h\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}:(D\to C^{\prime})\to(D\to C)
is an equivalence for any type
D
.
∎

Definition 4.9
.

Given a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
, we define the
gap map

\mathsf{gap}(p,q,H):C\to A\times_{X}B

by
{\lambda}z.\,(p(z),q(z),H(z))
. Furthermore, we will write

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}pullback}(f,g,H)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(\mathsf{gap}(p,q,H)).

Proposition 4.10
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square

(ii)

There is a term of type

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}pullback}(p,q,H)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(\mathsf{gap}(p,q,H)).

Proof.

Note that there are homotopies

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:\pi_{1}\circ\mathsf{gap}(p,q,H)\sim p

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle:\pi_{2}\circ\mathsf{gap}(p,q,H)\sim q

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:(\pi_{3}\cdot\mathsf{gap}(p,q,H))\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(g\cdot L)\sim(f\cdot K)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}H.

given by

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}z.\,\mathsf{refl}_{p(z)}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}z.\,\mathsf{refl}_{q(z)}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}z.\,\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}unit}(H(z))\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}(H(z))^{-1}.

Therefore the claim follows by
Section

2
.
∎

3
Fiberwise equivalences

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
f:A\to B
, and let
g:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P(a)\to Q(f(a))
be a fiberwise transformation. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}P(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}Q(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}_{f}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square.

(ii)

g
is a fiberwise equivalence.

Proof.

The gap map factors as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{B}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}Q(y)\Big)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}Q(f(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{gap}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{gap}^{\prime}\,\vcentcolon\equiv\,{\lambda}(x,q).\,(x,(f(x),q),\mathsf{refl}_{f(x)})}

Since
\mathsf{gap}^{\prime}
is an equivalence, it follows by
Section

2
that the gap map is an equivalence if and only if
g
is a fiberwise equivalence.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
, and consider the fiberwise transformation

\mathsf{fib}_{(f,q,H)}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}\mathsf{fib}_{p}(a)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(f(a))

given by
{\lambda}a.\,{\lambda}(c,u).\,(q(c),H(c)^{-1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({u}\right))
. Then there is an equivalence

\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{gap}(p,q,H)}((a,b,\alpha))\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{fib}_{(f,q,H)}(a)}((b,\alpha^{-1}))

Proof.

To obtain an equivalence of the desired type we simply concatenate known equivalences:

\displaystyle\mathsf{fib}_{h}((a,b,\alpha))

\displaystyle\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(z:C)}}}{\sum_{(z:C)}}{\sum_{(z:C)}}{\sum_{(z:C)}}(p(z),q(z),H(z))=(a,b,\alpha)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(z:C)}}}{\sum_{(z:C)}}{\sum_{(z:C)}}{\sum_{(z:C)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:p(z)=a)}}}{\sum_{(u:p(z)=a)}}{\sum_{(u:p(z)=a)}}{\sum_{(u:p(z)=a)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}}{\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}{\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}{\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}H(z)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{ap}_{g}\left({v}\right)=\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({u}\right)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\alpha

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{((z,u):\mathsf{fib}_{p}(a))}}}{\sum_{((z,u):\mathsf{fib}_{p}(a))}}{\sum_{((z,u):\mathsf{fib}_{p}(a))}}{\sum_{((z,u):\mathsf{fib}_{p}(a))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}}{\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}{\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}{\sum_{(v:q(z)=b)}}H(z)^{-1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({u}\right)=\mathsf{ap}_{g}\left({v}\right)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\varphi(a)}((b,\alpha^{-1}))\qed

Corollary 4.13
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square.

(ii)

The induced map on fibers

\mathsf{fib}_{(p,q,H)}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}\mathsf{fib}_{p}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(f(x))

is a fiberwise equivalence.

Corollary 4.14
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and suppose that
g
is an equivalence. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square.

(ii)

The map
p:C\to A
is an equivalence.

Proof.

If the square is a pullback square, then by
Section

3
the fibers of
p
are equivalent to the fibers of
g
, which are contractible by
Section

1
. Thus it follows that
p
is a contractible map, and hence that
p
is an equivalence.

If
p
is an equivalence, then by
Section

1
both
\mathsf{fib}_{p}(x)
and
\mathsf{fib}_{g}(f(x))
are contractible for any
x:X
. It follows that the induced map
\mathsf{fib}_{p}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(f(x))
is an equivalence. Thus we apply
Section

3
to conclude that the square is a pullback.
∎

5
The univalence axiom

The univalence axiom characterizes the identity type of the universe. It is considered to be an
extensionality principle
for types. In the following theorem we introduce the univalence axiom and give two more equivalent ways of stating this.

Proposition 5.1
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

The
univalence axiom
: for any
A:\mathcal{U}
the map

\mathsf{equiv\underline{\ }{}eq}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{ind}_{A=}(\mathsf{id}_{A}):{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{B:\mathcal{U}}(A\mathbin{{=}}B)\to(A\simeq B).

is a fiberwise equivalence.
If this is the case, we write

\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}equiv}

for the inverse of
\mathsf{equiv\underline{\ }{}eq}
.

(ii)

The type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}A\simeq B

is contractible for each
A:\mathcal{U}
.

(iii)

The principle of
equivalence induction
: for every
A:\mathcal{U}
and for every type family

P:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{B:\mathcal{U}}(A\simeq B)\to

the map

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{B:\mathcal{U}}{e:A\simeq B}P(B,e)\Big)\to P(A,\mathsf{id}_{A})

given by
f\mapsto f(A,\mathsf{id}_{A})
has a section.

It is a trivial observation, but nevertheless of fundamental importance, that by the univalence axiom the identity types of
\mathcal{U}
are equivalent to types in
\mathcal{U}
, because it provides an equivalence
(A=B)\simeq(A\simeq B)
, and the type
A\simeq B
is in
\mathcal{U}
for any
A,B:\mathcal{U}
. Since the identity types of
\mathcal{U}
are equivalent to types in
\mathcal{U}
, we also say that the universe is
locally small
.

Definition 5.2
.

(i)

A type
A
is said to be
essentially small
if there is a type
X:\mathcal{U}
and an equivalence
A\simeq X
. We write

\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(A)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}A\simeq X.

(ii)

A map
f:A\to B
is said to be
essentially small
if for each
b:B
the fiber
\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)
is essentially small.
We write

\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)).

(iii)

A type
A
is said to be
locally small
if for every
x,y:A
the identity type
x=y
is essentially small.
We write
