\mathsf{loc\underline{\ }{}small}(A)\vcentcolon\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(x=y).

(iv)

Similarly, a map
f:A\to X
is said to be
locally small
if
\delta_{f}:A\to A\times_{X}A
is essentially small.

Lemma 5.3
.

The type
\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(X)
is a proposition for any type
X
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a type. Our goal is to show that the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}X\simeq Y

is a proposition. Suppose there is a type
X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U}
and an equivalence
e:X\simeq X^{\prime}
, then the map

(X\simeq Y)\to(X^{\prime}\simeq Y)

given by precomposing with
e^{-1}
is an equivalence. This induces an equivalence on total spaces

\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}X\simeq Y\Big)\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}X^{\prime}\simeq Y\Big)

However, the codomain of this equivalence is contractible by
Section

5
. Thus it follows that the asserted type is a proposition.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

For each function
f:A\to B
, the type
\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(f)
is a proposition, and for each type
X
the type
\mathsf{loc\underline{\ }{}small}(X)
is a proposition.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that propositions are closed under dependent products, established in
Section

3
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

The property of essentially smallness is preserved by
\Pi
,
\Sigma
, and
\mathsf{Id}
. Of course, any contractible type is essentially small, and so is any small type. The property of essentially smallness is preserved by
\Sigma
and
\mathsf{Id}
, and the exponent
X^{A}
of a locally small type
X
by an essentially small type
A
is again locally small. Furthermore, any proposition is locally small, and any universe is locally small with respect to itself.

Definition 5.6
.

Consider two functions
f:A\to X
and
g:B\to X
. We define the type

\hom_{X}(f,g)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(h:A\to B)}}}{\sum_{(h:A\to B)}}{\sum_{(h:A\to B)}}{\sum_{(h:A\to B)}}f\sim g\circ h.

In other words, the type
\hom_{X}(f,g)
is the type of functions
h:A\to B
equipped with a homotopy witnessing that the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
P
and
Q
be two type families over
X
, and write
\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{P}
and
\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{Q}
for their first projections, respectively. Then the map

\mathsf{tot\underline{\ }{}triangle}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)\to Q(x)\Big)\to\hom_{X}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{P},\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{Q})

given by
\mathsf{tot\underline{\ }{}triangle}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{tot}(f),{\lambda}(x,y).\,\mathsf{refl}_{x})
, is an equivalence.

Corollary 5.8
.

For any two maps
f:A\to X
and
g:B\to X
, the map

\mathsf{fib\underline{\ }{}triangle}:\hom_{X}(f,g)\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(x)

given by
{\lambda}(h,H).\,{\lambda}x.\,{\lambda}(a,p).\,(h(a),H(a)^{-1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p)
is an equivalence.

Theorem 5.9
.

For any small type
A:\mathcal{U}
there is an equivalence

(A\to\mathcal{U})\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}X\to A\Big).

Proof.

Note that we have the function

\varphi:{\lambda}B.\,\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x),\mathsf{pr}_{1}\Big):(A\to\mathcal{U})\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}X\to A\Big).

The fiber of this map at
(X,f)
is by univalence and function extensionality equivalent to the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))\simeq X)}}}{\sum_{(e:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))\simeq X)}}{\sum_{(e:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))\simeq X)}}{\sum_{(e:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x))\simeq X)}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}\sim f\circ e.

By
Section

5
this type is equivalent to the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:A\to\mathcal{U})}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}B(a)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{f}(a),

and by ‘type theoretic choice’, which was established in
Section

3
, this type is equivalent to

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}X\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{f}(a).

We conclude that the fiber of
\varphi
at
(X,f)
is equivalent to the type
\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(f)
. However, since
f:X\to A
is a map between small types it is essentially small. Moreover, since being essentially small is a proposition by
Section

5
, it follows that
\mathsf{fib}_{\varphi}((X,f))
is contractible for every
f:X\to A
. In other words,
\varphi
is a contractible map, and therefore it is an equivalence.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

The inverse of the map

\varphi:(A\to\mathcal{U})\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}X\to A\Big).

constructed in
5.9
is the map
(X,f)\mapsto\mathsf{fib}_{f}
.

6
The object classifier

Definition 6.1
.

Let
p:E\to B
and
p^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
be maps. A morphism from
p^{\prime}
to
p
is a triple
(f,g,H)
consisting of maps
f:B^{\prime}\to B
and
g:E^{\prime}\to E
and a homotophy
H:f\circ p^{\prime}\sim p\circ g
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(1)

commutes. We write
\mathsf{hom}(p^{\prime},p)
for the type of such triples
(f,g,H)
, and sometimes we write
\mathsf{hom}_{f}(p^{\prime},p)
for the type of pairs
(g,H)
. A morphism
(f,g,H)
is said to be
cartesian
if the square in
Eq.

1
is cartesian. We write
\mathsf{cart}(p^{\prime},p)
for the type of cartesian morphisms from
p^{\prime}
to
p
, and we write
\mathsf{cart}_{f}(p^{\prime},p)
for the type of triples
(g,H,t)
for the type of triples, where
t:\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}pullback}(p^{\prime},g,H)
.

Definition 6.2
.

A morphism
p:E\to B
is said to be an
object classifier
if the type
\mathsf{cart}(p^{\prime},p)
is a proposition for each
p^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
. If
p:E\to B
is an object classifier, we also write

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}classified}(p^{\prime})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{hom}(p^{\prime},p).

Our goal in this section is to show that a univalent universe is an object classifier.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\alpha:I\to J
be a map, and let
A:I\to\mathcal{U}
and
B:J\to\mathcal{U}
be type families.
Then the map

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}A_{i}\to B_{\alpha(i)}\Big)\to\mathsf{hom}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{A},\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{B})

given by
{\lambda}f.\,(\mathsf{tot}_{\alpha}(f),{\lambda}(x,y).\,\mathsf{refl}_{x})
is an equivalence. Furthermore, the map

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}A_{i}\simeq B_{\alpha(i)}\Big)\to\mathsf{cart}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{A},\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{B})

given by
{\lambda}e.\,(\mathsf{tot}_{\alpha}(e),{\lambda}(x,y).\,\mathsf{refl}_{x},t)
where
t
is the term constructed in
Section

3
, is an equivalence.

Proof.

We have the equivalences

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}A_{i}\to B_{\alpha(i)}

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}{a:A_{i}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\gamma:\alpha(i)=j)}}}{\sum_{(\gamma:\alpha(i)=j)}}{\sum_{(\gamma:\alpha(i)=j)}}{\sum_{(\gamma:\alpha(i)=j)}}B_{j}

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}{a:A_{i}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B_{j})}}}{\sum_{(b:B_{j})}}{\sum_{(b:B_{j})}}{\sum_{(b:B_{j})}}\alpha(i)=j

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{(i,a):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{((j,b):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j})}}}{\sum_{((j,b):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j})}}{\sum_{((j,b):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j})}}{\sum_{((j,b):\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j})}}\alpha(i)=j

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}\big)\to\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j}\big))}}}{\sum_{(f:\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}\big)\to\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j}\big))}}{\sum_{(f:\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}\big)\to\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j}\big))}}{\sum_{(f:\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}\big)\to\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:J)}}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}{\sum_{(j:J)}}B_{j}\big))}}\alpha\circ\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{A}\sim\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{B}\circ f

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{hom}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{A},\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{B}).

It is easy to check that this composite is the asserted map. The second claim follows from
Section

3
.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

Consider a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Then the map

\mathsf{hom}_{\alpha}(f,g)\to\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(i)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(\alpha(i))\Big)

given by
{\lambda}(h,H).\,{\lambda}i.\,{\lambda}(a,p).\,(h(a),H(a)^{-1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{ap}_{\alpha}\left({p}\right))
is an equivalence.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a map, and let
\mathcal{U}
be a univalent universe with universal family
\mathrm{El}
over
\mathcal{U}
. Then there is an equivalence

\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(f)\simeq\mathsf{cart}(f,\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{\mathrm{El}}).

In particular, the type
\mathsf{cart}(f,\mathsf{pr}_{1}^{\mathrm{El}})
is a proposition for each map
f
, so the universe is an object classifier in the sense of
Section

6
.

Proof.

From
Section

6
we obtain that the type of pairs
(\tilde{F},H)
is equivalent to the type of fiberwise transformations

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)\to F(b).

By
Section

3
the square is a pullback square if and only if the induced map

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)\to F(b)

is a fiberwise equivalence. Thus the data
(F,\tilde{F},H,pb)
is equivalent to the type of pairs
(F,e)
where
e
is a fiberwise equivalence from
\mathsf{fib}_{f}
to
F
. By
Section

3
the type of pairs
(F,e)
is equivalent to the type
\mathsf{ess\underline{\ }{}small}(f)
.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

For any type
A
(not necessarily small), and any
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}(x,y).\,(B(x),y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

is a pullback square. Therefore it follows that for any family
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
of small types, the projection map
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\to A
is an essentially small map.
To see that the claim is a direct consequence of
Section

3
we write the asserted square in its rudimentary form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathrm{El}(B(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}\mathrm{El}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}(x,y).\,(B(x),y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

In the following theorem we show that a type is locally small if and only if its diagonal is classified by
\mathcal{U}
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
A
be a type. The following are equivalent:

(i)

A
is locally small.

(ii)

The diagonal
\delta_{A}:A\to A\times A
is classified by
\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

The identity type
x=y
is the fiber of
\delta_{A}
at
(x,y):A\times A
. Therefore it follows that
A
is locally small if and only if the diagonal
\delta_{A}
is essentially small.
Now the result follows from
6.5
.
∎

Chapter 2
Type theoretic descent

In this chapter we study homotopy pushouts, which were established as higher inductive types in homotopy type theory in section 6.8 of
[
33
]
. From this chapter on, we will assume that universes are closed under homotopy pushouts. This is the last assumption that we will be making in the present work. In particular, we will not assume the existence of higher inductive types with some self-reference in the constructors (e.g. the propositional truncation).

Our first main result is the descent theorem for homotopy pushouts (
2.9
and
2
), in which we establish that a cartesian transformation of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

extends uniquely to a cartesian transformation of the pushout squares, i.e. a commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\sqcup^{S^{\prime}}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcup^{S}B}

of which the vertical sides are pullback squares.

The second main theorem of this chapter,
2.12
, is an adaption to homotopy type theory of a theorem due to
[
1
]
. It is closely related to the descent theorem but can be stated without a universe: for any commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

of which the two vertical back squares are pullback squares, the two vertical front squares are pullback squares if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\sqcup^{S^{\prime}}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcup^{S}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}

is a pullback square. Even though this statement does not involve a universe, we use the univalence axiom in our proof that this square being pullback implies that the front two vertical squares of the cube are pullback squares. Function extensionality suffices for the converse direction.

1
Homotopy pushouts

1
Pushouts as higher inductive types

Definition 1.1
.

A
span

\mathcal{S}
from
A
to
B
is a triple
(S,f,g)
consisting of a type
S
and maps
f:S\to A
and
g:S\to B
. We write
\mathsf{span}(A,B)
for the type of small spans from
A
to
B
.

Definition 1.2
.

Consider a span
\mathcal{S}\equiv(S,f,g)
from
A
to
B
, and let
X
be a type. A cocone with vertex
X
on
\mathcal{S}
is a triple
(i,j,H)
consisting of maps
i:A\to X
,
j:B\to X
, and a homotopy
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

commutes. We write
\mathsf{cocone}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
for the type of cocones with vertex
X
on
\mathcal{S}
.

Definition 1.3
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
, and let
Y
be a type. We define the operation

\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}((i,j,H),Y)\vcentcolon\equiv(X\to Y)\to\mathsf{cocone}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y).

by
h\mapsto(h\circ i,h\circ j,h\cdot H)
.

Definition 1.4
.

A commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
is said to be a
(homotopy) pushout square
if the cocone
(i,j,H)
with vertex
X
on the span
\mathcal{S}\equiv(S,f,g)

satisfies the
universal property of pushouts
, which asserts that the map

\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}(i,j,H):(X\to Y)\to\mathsf{cocone}(Y)

is an equivalence for any type
Y
. Sometimes pushout squares are also called
cocartesian squares
.

Definition 1.5
.

Consider a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
, and consider a cocone
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})
with vertex
Y
on the same span
\mathcal{S}\equiv(S,f,g)
. Then the unique map
h:X\to Y
such that

\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}((i,j,H),Y,h)=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})

is called the
cogap map
of
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})
. We also write
\mathsf{cogap}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})
for the cogap map, and we write

\displaystyle\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}comp}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})

\displaystyle:i^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{cogap}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})\circ{\mathsf{inl}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}comp}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})

\displaystyle:j^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{cogap}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})\circ{\mathsf{inr}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{coh\underline{\ }{}comp}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})

\displaystyle:(\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}comp}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})\cdot g)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}H^{\prime}\sim(\mathsf{cogap}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})\cdot\mathsf{glue})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}comp}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})\cdot f).

for the homotopies determining the uniqueness of
\mathsf{cogap}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},H^{\prime})
.

Proposition 1.6
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pushout square.

(ii)

The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{S},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f}

which commutes by the homotopy

{\lambda}h.\,\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}htpy}(h\cdot H),

is a pullback square, for every type
Y
.

(iii)

For every type family
P
over
X
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P(j(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P(i(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}P(j(g(x))),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}h.\,{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{tr}_{P}(H(x),h(f(x)))}

which commutes by the homotopy

{\lambda}h.\,\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}htpy}({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{apd}_{h}\mathopen{}\left(H(x)\right)\mathclose{})

is a pullback square. This property is also called the
dependent universal property of pushouts
.

(iv)

The gap map of the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P(j(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P(i(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}P(j(g(x)))}

has a section, for any type family
P
over
X
. This property is also called the
induction principle of pushouts
.

Definition 1.7
.

From now on we will assume that any span has a pushout, and moreover that universes are closed under pushouts. We will write
A\sqcup^{\mathcal{S}}B
for the pushout of the span
\mathcal{S}\equiv(S,f,g)
from
A
to
B
. The type
A\sqcup^{\mathcal{S}}B
comes equipped with a colimiting cocone
({\mathsf{inl}},{\mathsf{inr}},\mathsf{glue})
, as displayed in the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcup^{\mathcal{S}}B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}

Remark 1.8
.

We note that if
\mathcal{S}\equiv(S,f,g)
is a span of
pointed
types and pointed maps between them, then the pushout
A\sqcup^{\mathcal{S}}B
of
\mathcal{S}
is again a pointed type. The cocone
({\mathsf{inl}},{\mathsf{inr}},\mathsf{glue})
consists of two pointed maps and a pointed homotopy filling the square of pointed maps. Moreover, the pushout
A\sqcup^{\mathcal{S}}B
satisfies a pointed version of the universal property: for any pointed type
Y
the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\sqcup^{\mathcal{S}}B\to_{\ast}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\to_{\ast}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\to_{\ast}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S\to_{\ast}Y)}

is a pullback square.

2
Examples of pushouts

Definition 1.9
.

Let
X
be a type. We define the
suspension

\mathsf{\Sigma}X
of
X
to be the pushout of the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Sigma}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}

We will write
\mathsf{N}\vcentcolon\equiv{\mathsf{inl}}(\star)
and
\mathsf{S}\vcentcolon\equiv{\mathsf{inr}}(\star)
.

Remark 1.10
.

By the universal property it follows that the map

(\mathsf{\Sigma}X\to Y)\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y,y^{\prime}:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y,y^{\prime}:Y)}}{\sum_{(y,y^{\prime}:Y)}}{\sum_{(y,y^{\prime}:Y)}}X\to(y=y^{\prime})

given by
h\mapsto(h(\mathsf{N}),h(\mathsf{S}),h\cdot\mathsf{glue})
is an equivalence.

Moreover, if
X
is a pointed type, then the suspension is considered to be a pointed type with base point
\mathsf{N}
. By the universal property of
\mathsf{\Sigma}X
it follows that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{\Sigma}X\to_{\ast}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{1}\to_{\ast}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{1}\to_{\ast}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\to_{\ast}Y)}

is a pullback square. Since
\mathbf{1}\to_{\ast}Y
is contractible, it follows that

(\mathsf{\Sigma}X\to_{\ast}Y)\simeq\mathsf{\Omega}(X\to_{\ast}Y)\simeq X\to_{\ast}\mathsf{\Omega}(Y)

Definition 1.11
.

Given a map
f:A\to B
, we define the
cofiber

\mathsf{cof}_{f}
of
f
as the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cof}_{f}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}

The cofiber of a map is sometimes also called the
mapping cone
.

Definition 1.12
.

We define the
join

{X}\ast{Y}
of
X
and
Y
to be the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\ast Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}

Definition 1.13
.

We define the
n
-sphere

\mathbb{S}^{n}
for any
n\geq-1
by induction on
n
, by taking

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{-1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{n+1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\mathbf{2}}\ast{\mathbb{S}^{n}}.

Definition 1.14
.

Suppose
A
and
B
are pointed types, with base points
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
, respectively. The
(binary) wedge

A\vee B
of
A
and
B
is defined as the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A+B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vee B.}

Definition 1.15
.

Given a type
I
, and a family of pointed types
A
over
i
, with base points
a_{0}(i)
. We define the
(indexed) wedge

\bigvee_{(i:I)}A_{i}
as the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee_{(i:I)}A_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}i.\,(i,a_{0}(i))}

Definition 1.16
.

Suppose
A
and
B
are pointed types. We define the
wedge inclusion

\mathsf{wedge\underline{\ }{}in}:A\vee B\to A\times B
to be the unique map obtained via the universal property of pushouts as indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vee B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}b.\,(a_{0},b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}a.\,(a,b_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{wedge\underline{\ }{}in}}

We define the
smash product

A\wedge B
of
A
and
B
as the cofiber of the wedge inclusion, i.e. as a pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vee B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\wedge B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{wedge\underline{\ }{}in}}

3
Properties of iterated pushouts

The following corollary is also called the
pasting property
of pullbacks.
†
†
margin:

edit

Corollary 1.17
.

Consider a commuting diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

with homotopies
H:i\circ f\sim g\circ k
and
K:j\circ g\sim h\circ l
, and the homotopy

(j\cdot H)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(K\cdot k):j\circ i\circ f\sim h\circ l\circ k

witnessing that the outer rectangle commutes. Furthermore, suppose that the square on the right is a pullback square. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The square on the left is a pullback square.

(ii)

The outer rectangle is a pullback square.

Proof.

The commutativity of the two squares induces fiberwise transformations

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(i(x))

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:Y}\mathsf{fib}_{g}(y)\to\mathsf{fib}_{h}(j(y)).

By the assumption that the square on the right is a pullback square, it follows from
Section

3
that the fiberwise transformation

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:Y}\mathsf{fib}_{g}(y)\to\mathsf{fib}_{h}(j(y))

is a fiberwise equivalence. Therefore it follows from 3-for-2 property of equivalences that the fiberwise transformation

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{g}(i(x))

is a fiberwise equivalence if and only if the fiberwise transformation

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{h}(j(i(x)))

is a fiberwise equivalence. Now the claim follows from one more application of
Section

3
.
∎

Corollary 1.18
.

Consider a commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}

of which the two front squares are pullback squares. Then the back left square is a pullback square if and only if the back right square is a pullback square.

Proposition 1.19
.

Consider the following configuration of commuting squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

with homotopies
H:j\circ f\sim g\circ i
and
K:l\circ g\sim h\circ k
, and suppose that the square on the left is a pushout square.
Then the square on the right is a pushout square if and only if the outer rectangle is a pushout square.

Proof.

Let
T
be a type. Taking the exponent
T^{(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})}
of the entire diagram of the statement of the theorem, we obtain the following commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{A}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}

By the assumption that
Y
is the pushout of
B\leftarrow A\rightarrow X
, it follows that the square on the right is a pullback square. It follows by
Section

3
that the rectangle on the left is a pullback if and only if the outer rectangle is a pullback. Thus the statement follows by the second characterization in
Section

1
.
∎

Lemma 1.20
.

Consider a map
f:A\to B
. Then the cofiber of the map
{\mathsf{inr}}:B\to\mathsf{cof}_{f}
is equivalent to the suspension
\mathsf{\Sigma}{A}
of
A
.

Proposition 1.21
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

and write
\mathsf{cogap}:X\sqcup^{A}B\to Y
for the cogap map.
Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cof}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cof}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cof}_{\mathsf{cogap}}}

is a pushout square.

2
Descent for pushouts

1
Type families over pushouts

Definition 2.1
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
, where all types involved are in
\mathcal{U}
. The type
\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})
of
descent data
for
X
, is defined to be the type of triples
(P_{A},P_{B},P_{S})
consisting of

\displaystyle P_{A}

\displaystyle:A\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle P_{B}

\displaystyle:B\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle P_{S}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}P_{A}(f(x))\simeq P_{B}(g(x)).

Furthermore, we define the map

\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}_{\mathcal{S}}(i,j,H):(X\to\mathcal{U})\to\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})

by
P\mapsto(P\circ i,P\circ j,{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{tr}_{P}(H(x)))
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
. If the square is a pushout square, then the function

\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}_{\mathcal{S}}(i,j,H):(X\to\mathcal{U})\to\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

By the 3-for-2 property of equivalences it suffices to construct an equivalence
\varphi:\mathsf{cocone}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{U})\to\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})
such that the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}^{X}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}_{\mathcal{S}}(i,j,H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}_{\mathcal{S}}(i,j,H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commutes.

Since we have equivalences

\mathsf{equiv\underline{\ }{}eq}:\Big(P_{A}(f(x))=P_{B}(g(x))\Big)\simeq\Big(P_{A}(f(x))\simeq P_{B}(g(x))\Big)

for all
x:S
, we obtain an equivalence on the dependent products

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}P_{A}(f(x))=P_{B}(g(x))\Big)\simeq\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}P_{A}(f(x))\simeq P_{B}(g(x))\Big).

by post-composing with the equivalences
\mathsf{equiv\underline{\ }{}eq}
.
We define
\varphi
to be the induced map on total spaces. Explicitly, we have

\varphi\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}(P_{A},P_{B},K).\,(P_{A},P_{B},{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{equiv\underline{\ }{}eq}(K(x))).

Then
\varphi
is an equivalence by
Section

2
, and the triangle commutes because there is a homotopy

\mathsf{equiv\underline{\ }{}eq}(\mathsf{ap}_{P}\left({H(x)}\right))\sim\mathsf{tr}_{P}(H(x)).\qed

Corollary 2.3
.

Consider descent data
(P_{A},P_{B},P_{S})
for a pushout square as in
Section

1
.
Then the type of quadruples
(P,e_{A},e_{B},e_{S})
consisting of a family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}
equipped with fiberwise equivalences

\displaystyle e_{A}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P_{A}(a)\simeq P(i(a))

\displaystyle e_{B}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P_{B}(a)\simeq P(j(b))

and a homotopy
e_{S}
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}(f(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(i(f(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{B}(g(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(j(g(x)))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{A}(f(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{S}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tr}_{P}(H(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{B}(g(x))}

commutes, is contractible.

Proof.

The fiber of
\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}_{\mathcal{S}}(i,j,H)
map at
(P_{A},P_{B},P_{S})
is equivalent to the type of quadruples
(P,e_{A},e_{B},e_{S})
as described in the theorem, which are contractible by
Section

1
.
∎

For the remainder of this subsection we consider a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
, descent data

\displaystyle P_{A}

\displaystyle:A\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle P_{B}

\displaystyle:B\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle P_{S}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}P_{A}(f(x))\simeq P_{B}(g(x)),

and a family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}
equipped with

\displaystyle e_{A}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P_{A}(a)\simeq P(i(a))

\displaystyle e_{B}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P_{B}(a)\simeq P(j(b))

and a homotopy
e_{S}
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}(f(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(i(f(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{B}(g(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(j(g(x)))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{A}(f(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{S}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tr}_{P}(H(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{B}(g(x))}

commutes.

Definition 2.4
.

We define the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S)}}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}P_{A}(f(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}P_{B}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}P_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}\,\vcentcolon\equiv\,\mathsf{tot}_{f}({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{id}_{P_{A}(f(x))})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\,\vcentcolon\equiv\,\mathsf{tot}_{g}(e_{S})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}\,\vcentcolon\equiv\,\mathsf{tot}_{j}(e_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}\,\vcentcolon\equiv\,\mathsf{tot}_{i}(e_{A})}

with the homotopy
H^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}\sim j^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}
defined as

{\lambda}(x,y).\,\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}pair}(H(x),e_{S}(x,y)^{-1}).

Furthermore, we will write
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
for the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}P_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S)}}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}P_{A}(f(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}P_{B}(b).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

We now state the flattening lemma for pushouts, which should be compared to the flattening lemma for coequalizers, stated in Lemma 6.12.2 of
[
33
]
. We note that, using the dependent universal property of pushouts, our proof is substantially shorter.

Lemma 2.5

(The flattening lemma)
.

The commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S)}}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}P_{A}(f(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}P_{B}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}P_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

is a pushout square.

Proof.

Note that we have a commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}P_{A}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}Y^{P(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}P_{B}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}Y^{P_{A}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S)}}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}P_{A}(f(x))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}Y^{P_{B}(b)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}Y^{P_{A}(f(x))}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}

for any type
Y
. In this cube, the bottom square is a pullback square by property (iii) of
Section

1
. The vertical maps (of the form
\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}
) are equivalences, so it follows that the top square is a pullback square. We conclude that
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
is a pushout.
∎

2
The descent property for pushouts

Definition 2.6
.

Consider a span
\mathcal{S}
from
A
to
B
, and a span
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
from
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
. A
cartesian transformation
of spans from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{S}
is a tuple

(h_{A},h_{S},h_{B},F,G,p_{f},p_{g})

consisting of maps
h_{A}:A^{\prime}\to A
,
h_{S}:S^{\prime}\to S
, and
h_{B}:B^{\prime}\to B
, as indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with homotopies
F:f\circ h_{S}\sim h_{A}\circ f^{\prime}
and
G:g\circ h_{S}\sim h_{B}\circ g^{\prime}
, satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle p_{f}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}pullback}(h_{S},f^{\prime},F)

\displaystyle p_{g}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}pullback}(h_{S},g^{\prime},G)

that both squares are pullback squares. We write
\mathsf{cart}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S})
for the type of cartesian transformations from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{S}
, and we write

\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{S})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:\mathsf{span}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}))}}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:\mathsf{span}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}))}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:\mathsf{span}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}))}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:\mathsf{span}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}))}}\mathsf{cart}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}).

Given descent data
(P_{A},P_{B},P_{S})
on a span
\mathcal{S}
from
A
to
B
, we obtain a cartesian transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}P_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S)}}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}{\sum_{(x:S)}}P_{A}(f(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}P_{B}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}_{f}(\mathsf{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}_{g}(P_{S})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with the canonical homotopies witnessing that the squares commute. Note that both the left and right commuting squares are pullback squares by
Section

3
. Thus we obtain an operation

\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}desc}_{\mathcal{S}}:\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{S}).

Lemma 2.7
.

For any span
\mathcal{S}
, the operation

\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}desc}_{\mathcal{S}}:\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{S})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Note that by
Section

6
it follows that the types of triples
(f^{\prime},F,p_{f})
and
(g^{\prime},G,p_{g})
are equivalent to the types of fiberwise equivalences

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{S}}(x)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(f(x))

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{S}}(x)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{h_{B}}(g(x))

respectively. Furthermore, by
5.9
the types of pairs
(S^{\prime},h_{S})
,
(A^{\prime},h_{A})
, and
(B^{\prime},h_{B})
are equivalent to the types
S\to\mathcal{U}
,
A\to\mathcal{U}
, and
B\to\mathcal{U}
, respectively. Therefore it follows that the type
\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{S})
is equivalent to the type of tuples
(Q,P_{A},\varphi,P_{B},P_{S})
consisting of

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle:S\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle P_{A}

\displaystyle:A\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle P_{B}

\displaystyle:B\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}Q(x)\simeq P_{A}(f(x))

\displaystyle P_{S}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}Q(x)\simeq P_{B}(g(x)).

However, the type of
\varphi
is equivalent to the type
P_{A}\circ f=Q
. Thus we see that the type of pairs
(Q,\varphi)
is contractible, so our claim follows.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
.
We define an operation

\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}map}_{\mathcal{S}}:{\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}X^{\prime}\to X\Big)}\to\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{S}).

Construction.

Let
X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U}
and
h_{X}:X^{\prime}\to X
. Then we define
A^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}
, and
S^{\prime}
as the pullbacks

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv A\times_{X}X^{\prime}

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv B\times_{X}X^{\prime}

\displaystyle S^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv S\times_{A}A^{\prime},

resulting in a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

By the universal property of
B^{\prime}
it follows that there is a unique map
g^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
making the cube commute.
Moreover, since the two front squares and the back left squares are pullback squares by construction, it follows by
Section

3
that also the back right square is a pullback square. Thus we obtain a cartesian transformation of spans.
∎

The following theorem is analogous to
Section

1
.

Theorem 2.9

(The descent theorem for pushouts)
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
H:i\circ f\sim j\circ g
.
If this square is a pushout square, then the operation
\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}map}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an equivalence

\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}X^{\prime}\to X\Big)\simeq\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{S})

Proof.

It suffices to show that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}X^{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{S})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}_{\mathcal{S}}(i,j,H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{map\underline{\ }{}fam}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}desc}_{\mathcal{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}map}_{\mathcal{S}}}

commutes. To see that this suffices, note that the operation
\mathsf{map\underline{\ }{}fam}_{X}
is an equivalence by
5.9
, the operation
\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}_{\mathcal{S}}(i,j,H)
is an equivalence by
Section

1
, and the operation
\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}desc}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an equivalence by
Section

2
.

To see that the square commutes, note that the composite

\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}map}_{\mathcal{S}}\circ\mathsf{map\underline{\ }{}fam}_{X}

takes a family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}
to the cartesian transformation of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}\tilde{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times_{A}\Big(A\times_{X}\tilde{P}\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times_{X}\tilde{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

where
\tilde{P}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
.

The composite

\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}desc}_{\mathcal{S}}\circ\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}_{X}

takes a family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}
to the cartesian transformation of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}P(i(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:S)}}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}P(i(f(s)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}P(j(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

These cartesian natural transformations are equal by
Section

3
.
∎

Since
\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}map}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an equivalence it follows that its fibers are contractible.

Corollary 2.10
.

Consider a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

with homotopies

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle:f\circ h_{S}\sim h_{A}\circ f^{\prime}

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle:g\circ h_{S}\sim h_{B}\circ g^{\prime}

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:i\circ f\sim j\circ g,

and suppose that the bottom square is a pushout square, and the top squares are pullback squares.
Then the type of tuples
((X^{\prime},h_{X}),(i^{\prime},I,p),(j^{\prime},J,q),(H^{\prime},C))
consisting of

(i)

A type
X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U}
together with a morphism

h_{X}:X^{\prime}\to X,

(ii)

A map
i^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
, a homotopy
I:i\circ h_{A}\sim h_{X}\circ i^{\prime}
, and a term
p
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is a pullback square.

(iii)

A map
j^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
, a homotopy
J:j\circ h_{B}\sim h_{X}\circ j^{\prime}
, and a term
q
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is a pullback square,

(iv)

A homotopy
H^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}\sim j^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}
, and a homotopy

C:(i\cdot F)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}((I\cdot f^{\prime})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(h_{X}\cdot H^{\prime}))\sim(H\cdot h_{S})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}((j\cdot G)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(J\cdot g^{\prime}))

witnessing that the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}

commutes,

is contractible.

The following theorem should be compared to the flattening lemma,
Section

1
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Consider a commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

in which the bottom square is a pushout, and the two vertical squares in the back are pullbacks. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The two vertical squares in the front are pullback squares.

(ii)

The top square is a pushout square.

Proof.

By
Section

3
we have fiberwise equivalences

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{S}}(x)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(f(x))

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{S}}(x)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{h_{B}}(g(x))

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(a)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{h_{X}}(i(a))

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{B}}(b)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{h_{X}}(j(b)).

Moreover, since the cube commutes we obtain a fiberwise homotopy

K:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}I(f(x))\circ F(x)\sim J(g(x))\circ G(x).

We define the descent data
(P_{A},P_{B},P_{S})
consisting of
P_{A}:A\to\mathcal{U}
,
P_{B}:B\to\mathcal{U}
, and
P_{S}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:S}P_{A}(f(x))\simeq P_{B}(g(x))
by

\displaystyle P_{A}(a)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(a)

\displaystyle P_{B}(b)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{fib}_{h_{B}}(b)

\displaystyle P_{S}(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv G(x)\circ F(x)^{-1}.

We have

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{fib}_{h_{X}}

\displaystyle e_{A}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv I

\displaystyle e_{B}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv J

\displaystyle e_{S}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv K.

Now consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:S)}}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{S}}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:S)}}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(f(s))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{B}}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{X}}(x)}

Since the top and bottom map in the left square are equivalences, we obtain that the left square is a pushout square. Moreover, the right square is a pushout by
Section

1
. Therefore it follows by
Section

3
that the outer rectangle is a pushout square.

Now consider the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:S)}}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}{\sum_{(s:S)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{S}}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{A}}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B)}}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}{\sum_{(b:B)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{B}}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h_{X}}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}.}

We have seen that the top square is a pushout. The vertical maps are all equivalences, so the vertical squares are all pushout squares. Thus it follows from one more application of
Section

3
that the bottom square is a pushout.
∎

Theorem 2.12
.

Consider a commuting cube of types

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}

and suppose the two vertical squares in the back are pullback squares. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The two vertical squares in the front are pullback squares.

(ii)

The commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\sqcup^{S^{\prime}}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcup^{S}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

is a pullback square.

Proof.

To see that (i) implies (ii), it suffices to show that the pullback

(A\sqcup^{S}B)\times_{X}X^{\prime}

has the universal property of the pushout. This follows by the descent theorem, since the vertical squares in the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\sqcup^{S}B)\times_{X}X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcup^{S}B}

are pullback squares by
Section

3
.

To prove that (ii) implies (i), we note that in the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\sqcup^{S^{\prime}}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcup^{S}B,}

the two back squares are pullback squares, and the top and bottom squares are pushout squares. Therefore it follows from
2.11
that the two front squares are pullback squares. Now we obtain (i) from the pasting lemma for pushouts.
∎

3
Applications of the descent theorem for pushouts

1
Fiber sequences

Definition 3.1
.

A
pointed type
is a pair
(X,x)
consisting of a type
X
equipped with a
base point

x:X
. We will write
\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
for the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}X
of all pointed types.

In the following lemma we characterize the identity type of
\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any
(A,a),(B,b):\mathcal{U}_{\ast}
we have an equivalence

\Big({\mathopen{}(A,a)\mathclose{}}={\mathopen{}(B,b)\mathclose{}}\Big)\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}e(a)=b\Big).

Construction.

By
Section

1
the type on the left hand side is
equivalent to the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:A=B)}}}{\sum_{(p:A=B)}}{\sum_{(p:A=B)}}{\sum_{(p:A=B)}}\mathsf{tr}_{\mathrm{El}}({p},{a})=b
.
By the univalence axiom, the map

\mathsf{equiv\underline{\ }{}eq}_{A,B}:(A=B)\to(A\simeq B)

is an equivalence for each
B:\mathcal{U}
.
Therefore, we have an equivalence of type

\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:A=B)}}}{\sum_{(p:A=B)}}{\sum_{(p:A=B)}}{\sum_{(p:A=B)}}\mathsf{tr}_{\mathrm{El}}({p},{a})=b\Big)\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}\mathsf{tr}_{\mathrm{El}}({\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}equiv}(e)},{a})=b\Big)

Moreover, by equivalence induction (the analogue of path induction for
equivalences), we can compute the transport:

\mathsf{tr}_{\mathrm{El}}({\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}equiv}(e)},{a})=e(a).

It follows that
(\mathsf{tr}_{\mathrm{El}}({\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}equiv}(e)},{a})=b)\simeq(e(a)=b)
.
∎

Definition 3.3
.

(i)

Let
(X,\ast_{X})
be a pointed type. A
pointed family
over
(X,\ast_{X})
consists of a type family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}
equipped with a base point
\ast_{P}:P(\ast_{X})
.

(ii)

Let
(P,\ast_{P})
be a pointed family over
(X,\ast_{X})
. A
pointed section
of
(P,\ast_{P})
consists of a dependent function
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)
and an identification
p:f(\ast_{X})=\ast_{P}
. We define the
pointed
\Pi
-type
to be the type of pointed sections:

\Pi^{\ast}_{(x:X)}P(x)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x))}}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x))}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x))}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x))}}f(\ast_{X})=\ast_{P}

In the case of two pointed types
X
and
Y
, we may also view
Y
as a pointed family over
X
. In this case we write
X\to_{\ast}Y
for the type of pointed functions.

(iii)

Given any two pointed sections
f
and
g
of a pointed family
P
over
X
, we define the type of pointed homotopies

f\sim_{\ast}g\vcentcolon\equiv\Pi^{\ast}_{(x:X)}f(x)=g(x),

where the family
x\mapsto f(x)=g(x)
is equipped with the base point
p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q^{-1}
.

Definition 3.4
.

(i)

For any pointed type
X
, we define the
pointed identity function

\mathsf{id}^{\ast}_{X}\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{id}_{X},\mathsf{refl}_{\ast})
.

(ii)

For any two pointed maps
f:X\to_{\ast}Y
and
g:Y\to_{\ast}Z
, we define the
pointed composite

g\mathbin{\circ_{\ast}}f\vcentcolon\equiv(g\circ f,\mathsf{ap}_{g}\left({p_{f}}\right)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p_{g}).

Definition 3.5
.

Let
X
be a pointed type with base point
x
. We define the
loop space

\mathsf{\Omega}(X,x)
of
X
at
x
to be the pointed type
x=x
with base point
\mathsf{refl}_{x}
.

Definition 3.6
.

The loop space operation
\mathsf{\Omega}
is
functorial
in the sense that

(i)

For every pointed map
f:X\to_{\ast}Y
there is a pointed map

\mathsf{\Omega}(f):\mathsf{\Omega}(X)\to_{\ast}\mathsf{\Omega}(Y),

defined by
\mathsf{\Omega}(f)(\omega)\vcentcolon\equiv p_{f}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({\omega}\right)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p_{f}^{-1}
, which is base point preserving by
\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}inv}(p_{f})
.

(ii)

For every pointed type
X
there is a pointed homotopy

\mathsf{\Omega}(\mathsf{id}^{\ast}_{X})\sim_{\ast}\mathsf{id}^{\ast}_{\mathsf{\Omega}(X)}.

(iii)

For any two pointed maps
f:X\to_{\ast}Y
and
g:Y\to_{\ast}X
, there is a pointed homotopy witnessing that the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(Z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(g\circ_{\ast}f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(f)}

of pointed types commutes.

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
{\mathopen{}(e,p)\mathclose{}},{\mathopen{}(f,q)\mathclose{}}:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}{\sum_{(e:A\simeq B)}}e(a)=b
, we have an equivalence of type

\Big({\mathopen{}(e,p)\mathclose{}}={\mathopen{}(f,q)\mathclose{}}\Big)\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(h:e\sim f)}}}{\sum_{(h:e\sim f)}}{\sum_{(h:e\sim f)}}{\sum_{(h:e\sim f)}}p=h(a)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q\Big).

Construction.

The type
{\mathopen{}(e,p)\mathclose{}}={\mathopen{}(f,q)\mathclose{}}
is equivalent
to the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(h:e=f)}}}{\sum_{(h:e=f)}}{\sum_{(h:e=f)}}{\sum_{(h:e=f)}}\mathsf{tr}({h},{p})=q
.
Note that by the principle of function extensionality,
the map
\mathsf{htpy\underline{\ }{}eq}:(e=f)\to(e\sim f)

is an equivalence. Furthermore, it follows by homotopy induction that for any

h:e\sim f
we have an equivalence of type

(\mathsf{tr}({\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}htpy}(h)},{p})=q)\simeq(p=h(a)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q).\qed

Definition 3.8
.

A
fiber sequence

F\hookrightarrow E\twoheadrightarrow B
consists of:

(i)

Pointed types
F
,
E
, and
B
, with base points
x_{0}
,
y_{0}
, and
b_{0}
respectively,

(ii)

Base point preserving maps
i:F\to_{\ast}E
and
p:E\to_{\ast}B
, with
\alpha:i(x_{0})=y_{0}
and
\beta:p(y_{0})=b_{0}
,

(iii)

A pointed homotopy
H:\mathsf{const}_{b_{0}}\sim_{\ast}p\circ_{\ast}i
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{const}_{b_{0}}}

commutes and is a pullback square.

We will write
\mathsf{Fib\underline{\ }{}Seq}
for the type of all fiber sequences in
\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

The type of all fiber sequences is equivalent to the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{((B,b):\mathcal{U}_{\ast})}}}{\sum_{((B,b):\mathcal{U}_{\ast})}}{\sum_{((B,b):\mathcal{U}_{\ast})}}{\sum_{((B,b):\mathcal{U}_{\ast})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(P:B\to\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(P:B\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(P:B\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(P:B\to\mathcal{U})}}P(b).

2
Fiber sequences obtained by the descent property

Definition 3.10
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a map. The

\nabla_{f}
of

is the map obtained from the universal property of the pushout, as indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sqcup^{A}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{f}}

Proposition 3.11
.

For any map
f:A\to B
and any
y:B
, there is an equivalence
\mathsf{fib}_{\nabla_{f}}(y)\simeq\mathsf{\Sigma}(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(y))
.

Proof.

For any
b:B
we have the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

of which the vertical sides are pullback squares. Hence we obtain the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Sigma}{\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sqcup^{A}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

from
2.12
, from which the claim follows.
∎

Definition 3.12
.

Consider two maps
f:A\to B
and
g:C\to D
.
The
pushout-product

f\square g:(A\times D)\sqcup^{A\times C}(B\times C)\to B\times D

of
f
and
g
is defined by the universal property of the pushout as the unique map rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\times D)\sqcup^{A\times C}(B\times C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\mathsf{id}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{A}\times g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{B}\times g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\mathsf{id}_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\square g}

commutative.

Proposition 3.13
.

For any two maps
f:A\to B
and
g:C\to D
, and any
(b,d):B\times D
, there is an equivalence

\mathsf{fib}_{f\square g}(b,d)\simeq{\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)}\ast{\mathsf{fib}_{g}(d)}.

Proof.

Let
b:B
and
d:D
. Then we have the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)\times\mathsf{fib}_{g}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{g}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times D}

of which the vertical sides are pullback squares. Hence the claim follows from
2.12
.
∎

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
f:A\to X
and
g:B\to X
be maps into
X
, and let
x:X
. Then there is
an equivalence

\mathsf{fib}_{{f}\ast{g}}(x)\simeq{\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)}\ast{\mathsf{fib}_{g}(x)}.

Proof.

We have the following commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\times\mathsf{fib}_{g}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{g}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

in which the vertical squares are pullback squares. Therefore it follows by
2.12
that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)}\ast{\mathsf{fib}_{g}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast_{X}{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

is a pullback square.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

The join operation on maps with a common codomain is associative up to homotopy (this was formalized by Brunerie, see Proposition 1.8.6 of
[
9
]
), and it is a commutative operation on the generalized elements of a type
X
. Furthermore, the unique map of type
\mathbf{0}\to X
is a unit for the join operation.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
A
and
B
be pointed types with base points

and

. The

is defined as follows by the universal property of the wedge:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}b.\,(a_{0},b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}a.\,(a,b_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{wedge\underline{\ }{}in}_{A,B}}

Proposition 3.18
.

There is a fiber sequence

{\mathsf{\Omega}(A)}\ast{\mathsf{\Omega}(B)}\hookrightarrow A\vee B\twoheadrightarrow A\times B.

Proof.

We have the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(B)\times\mathsf{\Omega}(A)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{const}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{const}_{b}}

of which the vertical sides are pullback squares. Hence the claim follows from
2.12
.
∎

.

. We define the map

by the universal property of the wedge as indicated in the diagram

.

There is a fiber sequence

\mathsf{\Sigma}\mathsf{\Omega}(X)\hookrightarrow X\vee X\twoheadrightarrow X.

Proof.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(X)}

Remark 3.21
.

As a corollary, there are fiber sequences

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}\vee\mathbb{R}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}\vee\mathbb{C}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}.

Here we take
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}\vcentcolon\equiv K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)
and
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{P}^{\infty}\vcentcolon\equiv K(\mathbb{Z},2)
, where the Eilenberg-Mac Lane space
K(G,n)
is defined in
[
23
]
.

Corollary 3.22
.

There is a fiber sequence

(\mathsf{\Sigma}\mathsf{\Omega}(X))^{\vee n}\hookrightarrow X^{\vee(n+1)}\twoheadrightarrow X.

Definition 3.23
.

A
coherent H-space
consists of a type
X
equipped with a unit
1:X
, a multiplication operation
\mu:X\to(X\to X)
such that the function
\mu(x,\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})
and
\mu(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt},y)
are equivalences for each
x:X
and
y:X
, respectively, and
coherent
unit laws

\displaystyle\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}unit}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mu(x,1)=x

\displaystyle\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:X}\mu(1,y)=y

\displaystyle\mathsf{coh\underline{\ }{}unit}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}(1)=\mathsf{right\underline{\ }{}unit}(1).

The following theorem is also known as the Hopf-construction.

Theorem 3.24
.

For any coherent H-space
X
there is a fiber sequence

X\hookrightarrow{X}\ast{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{\Sigma}X.

The map
\eta_{X}:{X}\ast{X}\to\mathsf{\Sigma}X
is called the
Hopf fibration
for
X
.

Proof.

We have the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}\ast{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Sigma}X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

where the front map is obtained by the universal property of the pushout.

In this cube, the two vertical squares in the back are pullback squares by
Section

3
, since
\mu(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt},y)
and
\mu(x,\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})
are equivalences for every
x:X
and
y:X
, respectively. Since the bottom and top squares are pushout squares it follows by
2.12
