that the front two squares are pullback squares.
∎

Remark 3.25
.

The classical Hopf fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{2}}

is now obtained from the fact that
{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\ast{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{3}
, which was established in
[
9
]
. Indeed, the circle is a coherent H-space.

Chapter 3
Reflexive coequalizers

The material of this chapter is joint work with Bas Spitters, which we started in the academic year 2012-2013 while I was a research assistant at the Radboud University of Nijmegen.

We begin this chapter with a proof of the type theoretic Yoneda lemma,
Section

1
. The Yoneda lemma is used to show in
Section

2
that the discrete functor
\Delta:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{rGph}
from small types into the type of small reflexive graphs, is an embedding. In
2.6
we show that a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
is discrete if and only if it satisfies any one (and hence all) of the unique extension properties

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

where
\mathcal{I}
is the walking edge.

The (homotopy) reflexive coequalizer satisfies the universal property of the left adjoint of
\Delta:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{rGph}
. We show in
Section

3
that the reflexive coequalizer of a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
is just a pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

In particular it follows that if a universe is closed under pushouts (which is our running assumption), then it is also closed under reflexive coequalizers. More practically, this characterization of reflexive coequalizers as pushouts allows us to compute in
Section

3
many reflexive coequalizers in terms of previously defined operations.

Our next purpose is to study the morphisms
f:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
of reflexive graphs that are
fibrations
in the sense that they satisfy the right orthogonality conditions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We show in
Section

4
that the class of fibrations is precisely the class of
cartesian
morphisms of reflexive graphs, i.e. the morphisms
f:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
for which the naturality squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{B}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{B}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}

are pullback squares. It follows that
f:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is a fibration whenever it appears as a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

of some map between discrete reflexive graphs. Pulling back along
h:\mathcal{A}\to\Delta X
for a fixed type
X
therefore provides an operation

h^{\ast}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}(Y\to X)\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\mathcal{B}:\mathsf{rGph})}}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{B}:\mathsf{rGph})}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{B}:\mathsf{rGph})}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{B}:\mathsf{rGph})}}\mathsf{fib}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\Big).

One way of stating the descent property for reflexive coequalizers, is that this map is an equivalence whenever
f:\mathcal{A}\to\Delta X
is a reflexive coequalizer. As a consequence of the descent property we obtain in
4.11
that for any fibration
f:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
of reflexive graphs, a commuting square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square of reflexive graphs if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)}

is a pullback square. A minor note about the forward direction is that the hypothesis that
f
is a fibration is implied by the assumption that the square is a pullback square, so this assumption is unrestrictively superfluous. However, this assumption is necessary for the converse direction.

The situation here is that we have an adjunction
F\dashv G
(in the present case
\mathsf{rcoeq}\dashv\Delta
) satisfying the ‘descent condition’ that if
f
is a fibration, i.e. the naturality square of the unit
\eta:\mathsf{id}\Rightarrow GF
at
f
is a pullback square, then any square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gg}

is a pullback square if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is a pullback square. We will investigate this situation further in
Section

1
, where we study the descent condition for general modalities.

In the final section of this chapter we apply the previous results to colimits of diagrams indexed by a (reflexive) graph. Most useful to us are the results on sequential colimits, which we will rely on in later chapters. Here we apply our results on diagrams over (reflexive) graphs to obtain results on sequential colimits. In
[
14
]
these results are obtained directly, and moreover they are formalized.

1
The Yoneda lemma

The universal property of identity types is sometimes called the type theoretic Yoneda lemma: families of maps out of the identity type are uniquely determined by their action on the reflexivity identification.

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
B
be a type family over
A
, and let
a:A
. Then the map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x)\Big)\to B(a)

given by
{\lambda}f.\,f(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a})
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The inverse of
\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}
is
\mathsf{ind}_{a=}:B(a)\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x)
. We have the homotopy
{\lambda}b.\,\mathsf{refl}_{b}:\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}\circ\mathsf{ind}_{a=}\sim\mathsf{id}_{B(a)}
by the computation rule for identity types, so it is indeed the case that
\mathsf{ind}_{a=}
is a section of
\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}
.

To see that
\mathsf{ind}_{a=}\circ\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}\sim\mathsf{id}
, let
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x)
. To show that
\mathsf{ind}_{a=}(f(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a}))=f
we use function extensionality (twice), so it suffices to show that

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}{p:a=x}\varphi(f(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a}),x,p)=f(x,p).

This follows by path induction on
p
, since
\mathsf{ind}_{a=}(f(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a}),a,\mathsf{refl}_{a})\equiv f(a,\mathsf{refl}_{a})
.
∎

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
B
be a type family over
A
, and let
a:A
. Then there is an equivalence

\Big(\mathsf{Id}_{A}(a)=B\Big)\simeq\Big(B(a)\times\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)\Big).

Furthermore, for
b:B(a)
there is an equivalence

\Big((\mathsf{Id}_{A}(a),\mathsf{refl}_{a})=(B,b)\Big)\simeq\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big).

Proof.

First we show that there is an equivalence

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\simeq B(x)\Big)\simeq B(a)\times\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big).

To see this, note that the type of fiberwise equivalences
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\simeq B(x)
is equivalent to the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x))}}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x))}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x))}}{\sum_{(f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}(a=x)\to B(x))}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)

By
2.5
, the type
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
is equivalent to the contractibility of the total space of
B
. Now the claim follows from
Section

1
. The claims in the statement are now easy consequences.
∎

Definition 1.3
.

A (small)
reflexive relation

\mathcal{R}
on
A
is a pair
(R,\rho)
consisting of a binary type-valued relation
R:A\to(A\to\mathcal{U})
equipped with a proof of reflexivity

\rho:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}R(x,x).

We will write
\mathsf{rRel}(A)
for the type of all small reflexive relations on
A
, i.e.

\mathsf{rRel}(A)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(R:A\to(A\to\mathcal{U}))}}}{\sum_{(R:A\to(A\to\mathcal{U}))}}{\sum_{(R:A\to(A\to\mathcal{U}))}}{\sum_{(R:A\to(A\to\mathcal{U}))}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}R(x,x).

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\equiv(R,\rho)
be a reflexive relation on
A
.
Then there is an equivalence

\Big((\mathsf{Id}_{,}\mathsf{refl})=(R,\rho)\Big)\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}R(a,x)\Big).

The following theorem, which was proven independently by Escardó
[
17
]
around the same time, shows that the canonical map

(x=y)\to(\mathsf{Id}_{A}(x)=\mathsf{Id}_{A}(y))

is an equivalence, for any
x,y:A
. This will be particularly relevant to us once we study
\infty
-equivalence relations.

Proposition 1.5
.

For any type
A:\mathcal{U}
, the map

\mathsf{Id}_{A}:A\to(A\to\mathcal{U})

is an embedding.

Proof.

Let
a:A
. Then we calculate the fiber of
\mathsf{Id}_{A}
at a type family
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{Id}_{A}}(B)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}\mathsf{Id}_{A}(a)=B

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}\Big(B(a)\times\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)\Big)

(by
Section

1
)

\displaystyle\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}B(a)\Big)\times\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big).

The last equivalence follows since
X\times\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(X)\simeq\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(X)
for every type
X
. We conclude that the fibers of
\mathsf{Id}_{A}
are propositions, so
\mathsf{Id}_{A}
is an embedding by
Section

2
.
∎

2
Discrete reflexive graphs

Definition 2.1
.

A
non-reflexive graph

\mathcal{A}
in
\mathcal{U}
is a pair
{\mathopen{}({A}_{0},{A}_{1})\mathclose{}}
consisting of

\displaystyle{A}_{0}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle{A}_{1}

\displaystyle:{A}_{0}\to{A}_{0}\to\mathcal{U}.

We write
\mathsf{Gph}
for the type of all graphs in
\mathcal{U}
. When
\mathcal{A}
is a graph, we say that
{A}_{0}
is its type of
vertices
, and that
{A}_{1}
is its family of
edges
.

A
reflexive graph

\mathcal{A}
in
\mathcal{U}
consists of a graph
({A}_{0},{A}_{1})
equipped with a
reflexivity
term

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(i,i).

We write

\mathsf{rGph}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(V:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(V:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(V:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(V:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(E:V\to(V\to\mathcal{U}))}}}{\sum_{(E:V\to(V\to\mathcal{U}))}}{\sum_{(E:V\to(V\to\mathcal{U}))}}{\sum_{(E:V\to(V\to\mathcal{U}))}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{v:V}E(v,v)

for the type of reflexive graphs in
\mathcal{U}
.

Example 2.2
.

(i)

A
reflexive pair
consists of types
V
and
E
, and maps
s
,
t
, and
r
between
V
and
E
as indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

equipped with homotopies
H_{sr}:s\circ r\sim\mathsf{id}_{V}
and
H_{tr}:t\circ r\sim\mathsf{id}_{V}
.
Given a reflexive pair as above, we obtain a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
by taking

\displaystyle{A}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv V

\displaystyle{A}_{1}(v,w)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:E)}}}{\sum_{(e:E)}}{\sum_{(e:E)}}{\sum_{(e:E)}}(s(e)=v)\times(t(e)=w)

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(v)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\mathopen{}(r(v),H_{sr}(v),H_{tr}(v))\mathclose{}}.

By a routine construction it can be shown that the type of small reflexive pairs is equivalent to the type of small reflexive graphs.

(ii)

Given a type
X
, the
discrete graph

\Delta(X)
on
X
is the reflexive graph consisting of

\displaystyle{\Delta(X)}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv X

\displaystyle{\Delta(X)}_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{Id}_{X}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\Delta(X)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}.

(iii)

Given a map
f:A\to X
, the
pre-kernel

k(f)
of
f
is the reflexive relation on
A
given by
x,y\mapsto f(x)=f(y)
.

(iv)

The
indiscrete graph

\nabla(X)
on
X
is the reflexive graph consisting of

\displaystyle{\nabla(X)}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv X

\displaystyle{\nabla(X)}_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}x.\,{\lambda}y.\,\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\nabla(X)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\star.

The reflexive pair corresponding to
\nabla(X)
is (equivalent to) the reflexive pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

In particular, we have the
unit
graph
\mathbf{1}\vcentcolon\equiv\nabla(\mathbf{1})
, which happens to also be
\Delta(\mathbf{1})
.

(v)

The
walking edge

\mathcal{I}
is an example of a reflexive graph. One way of defining it is by taking

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\tau(x)\to\tau(y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{I}}(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{id}_{\tau(x)}

where
\tau:\mathbf{2}\to\mathcal{U}
is the
tautological family
on the type
\mathbf{2}
of booleans, given by
\tau({0_{\mathbf{2}}})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{0}
and
\tau({1_{\mathbf{2}}})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{1}
. We write
a
for the (unique) edge from
{0_{\mathbf{2}}}
to
{1_{\mathbf{2}}}
. It may be helpful to think of the walking edge
\mathcal{I}
as an interval, hence the choice of notation.

Definition 2.3
.

A
morphism
of graphs
f
from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is a pair

\displaystyle{f}_{0}

\displaystyle:{A}_{0}\to{B}_{0}

\displaystyle{f}_{1}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i,j:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(i,j)\to{B}_{1}({f}_{0}(i),{f}_{0}(j)).

We write
\mathsf{Gph}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
for the type of graph morphisms from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
.

A
morphism
of reflexive graphs
f
from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is a morphism of graphs equipped with a term

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{f}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:{A}_{0}}{f}_{1}(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(i))=\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{B}}({f}_{0}(i))

witnessing that reflexivity is preserved. We write
\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
for the type of reflexive graph morphisms from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
.

For any reflexive graph there is an identity morphism, and for any composable pair of morphisms there is a composite. Furthermore, these operations are associative, and satisfy the unit laws, both up to homotopy. It should be noted, however, that composition of reflexive graph morphisms is not expected to be associative on the nose, since the reflexivity is only preserved up to higher identification.

Example 2.4
.

(i)

Given a function
f:X\to Y
we obtain a morphism
\Delta f:\mathsf{rGph}(\Delta X,\Delta Y)
given by

\displaystyle{\Delta f}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv f

\displaystyle{\Delta f}_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{ap}_{f}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\Delta f}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{refl}_{\mathsf{refl}_{f(x)}}

The action of
\Delta
on morphisms preserves identity morphisms and compositions, and moreover it preserves the unit laws and associativity.

(ii)

The graph
\nabla(\mathbf{1})
is the terminal reflexive graph in the sense that for any graph
\mathcal{A}
, the type
\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\mathbf{1})
is contractible.

(iii)

For any reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
, the map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pt}:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})\to{A}_{0}

given by
f\mapsto{f}_{0}(\star)
, is an equivalence. The analogous statement is false for non-reflexive graphs.

(iv)

The universal property of the walking edge
\mathcal{I}
is that every edge
e
in a graph
\mathcal{A}
, the type of reflexive graph morphisms that map the edge
a
of
\mathcal{I}
to
e
is contractible. In other words, the map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}edge}:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)

given by
f\mapsto({f}_{0}(0),{f}_{0}(1),{f}_{1}(a))
is an equivalence.

(v)

For the walking edge
\mathcal{I}
there are reflexive graph morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

This is a
cograph
object of reflexive graphs, since the morphism
\mathcal{I}\to\mathbf{1}
is a common
retraction
of the end-point inclusions
0,1:\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{I}
, whereas in a reflexive pair (a graph object) the morphism
r
is a common
section
of the source and target maps.

(vi)

Since we do not have the technology available to establish that graphs and reflexive graphs form
\infty
-categories, a comparison between the two structures is limited to what we can say directly using the basic categorical operations such as composition. However, this is just enough to establish the universal property of an adjunction.

Given a non-reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}\equiv{\mathopen{}({A}_{0},{A}_{1})\mathclose{}}
, we can obtain a reflexive graph
F\mathcal{A}
by freely adjoining reflexivity:

\displaystyle{F\mathcal{A}}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{A}_{0}

\displaystyle{F\mathcal{A}}_{1}(i,j)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{A}_{1}(i,j)+(i=j)

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{F\mathcal{A}}(i)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\mathsf{inr}}(\mathsf{refl}_{i}).

On the other hand, there is the projection
U:\mathsf{rGph}\to\mathsf{Gph}
which forgets the reflexivity structure, and for each graph
\mathcal{A}
there is a graph morphism
\eta:\mathcal{A}\to UF\mathcal{A}
. Both
F
and
U
are functorial in the sense that they act on morphisms, and preserve units and composition in the obvious way, and
\eta
is natural in
\mathcal{A}
.

The universal property of the construction
F
of freely adjoining reflexivity, is that the map

U(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})\circ\eta:\mathsf{rGph}(F\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\to\mathsf{Gph}(\mathcal{A},U\mathcal{B})

is an equivalence. Indeed, this is the universal property that establishes
F
as a left adjoint to
U
, even though we cannot manifest
F
and
U
as functors.

The construction
F
of freely adjoining reflexivity is not surjective on morphisms. For example, there are no morphisms from
\mathbf{1}
to the graph
\mathcal{A}
with a contractible type of vertices but no edges. However
F\mathcal{A}
is the terminal reflexive graph.

Proposition 2.5
.

The operation
\Delta:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{rGph}
is an embedding in the following sense:

(i)

As a function,
\Delta:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{rGph}
is an embedding.

(ii)

For every two types
X
and
Y
, the action on morphisms

\Delta:(X\to Y)\to\mathsf{rGph}(\Delta X,\Delta Y)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a reflexive graph. Then the fiber of
\Delta:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{rGph}
is calculated as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{fib}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}\Delta X=\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathsf{Id}_{{A}_{0}},\mathsf{refl})=({A}_{1},\mathsf{rfx}_{A})

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{A}_{0}}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(y:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\Big),

(by
Section

1
)

which is a proposition.

The map
(X\to Y)\to\mathsf{rGph}(\Delta X,\Delta Y)
is an equivalence, since the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:X}(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y)))}}}{\sum_{(\varphi:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:X}(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y)))}}{\sum_{(\varphi:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:X}(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y)))}}{\sum_{(\varphi:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:X}(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y)))}}\varphi(\mathsf{refl}_{x})=\mathsf{refl}_{f(x)}

is equivalent to the fiber of the map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:X}(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y))\Big)\to\Big(f(x)=f(x)\Big),

which is contractible by
Section

1
.
∎

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a reflexive graph. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The canonical map

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i,j:{A}_{0}}(i=j)\to{A}_{1}(i,j)

given by
\mathsf{refl}_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(i)
is a fiberwise equivalence. In particular,
\mathcal{A}
is in the image of
\Delta:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{rGph}
.

(ii)

The graph
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathcal{I}
-null
in the sense that the map

\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})\to\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})

given by pre-composition by the unique morphism
\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathbf{1})
, is an equivalence.

(iii)

The map

\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})\to\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})

given by pre-composing with the end-point inclusion
0:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{I})
, is an equivalence.

(iv)

The map

\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})\to\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})

given by pre-composing with the end-point inclusion
1:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{I})
, is an equivalence.

If any of these conditions hold, we say that
\mathcal{A}
is
discrete
.

Proof.

The outline of our argument is as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(iii)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(ii)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(iv)}

Suppose (i) holds. We have a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}edge}}

where both vertical maps are equivalences, and the bottom map is an equivalence by assumption. Therefore the top map is an equivalence. This proves that (i) implies (ii).

Since the reflexive graph morphism
\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathbf{1})
is a common retraction of both end-point inclusions, it follows that the pre-composition map

\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})\to\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})

is a common section of both pre-composition maps

0^{\ast},1^{\ast}:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})\to\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})

However, assuming (ii) it follows that both
0^{\ast}
and
1^{\ast}
are equivalences, so (ii) implies both (iii) and (iv).

Now suppose that (iii) holds; we will show that (i) follows. We have the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}edge}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

in which both vertical maps are equivalences. Therefore the fibers of
\pi_{1}
are equivalent to the fibers of
0^{\ast}
. Note that the fibers of
\pi_{1}
are of the form

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j),

so it follows from (iii) that these are contractible. Then (i) follows by the fundamental theorem of identity types,
2.5
.

The argument that (i) follows from (iv) is similar, using
\pi_{2}
and
1^{\ast}
instead of
\pi_{1}
and
0^{\ast}
in the square.
∎

3
Reflexive coequalizers

Definition 3.1
.

Consider a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
and a type
X
equipped with a morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta(X))
. We say that
f
is a
reflexive coequalizer of
\mathcal{A}
if the map

\Delta(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})\circ f:(X\to Y)\to\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta(Y))

is an equivalence.

Our goal in this section is to show that reflexive coequalizers can be constructed as pushouts. We will use the following lemma, which was discovered jointly with Simon Boulier.

Lemma 3.2
.

Consider a type
A
with a type family
B
, and
a:A
. Furthermore, suppose that

\alpha:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\to(a=x).

Then the
coherence reduction
map

\mathsf{coh\underline{\ }{}red}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B(a))}}}{\sum_{(y:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y:B(a))}}\alpha(y)=\mathsf{refl}_{a}\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)

given by
(y,q)\mapsto(a,y)
is an equivalence.

Remark 3.3
.

A quick way to see that there is an equivalence

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:B(a))}}}{\sum_{(b:B(a))}}{\sum_{(b:B(a))}}{\sum_{(b:B(a))}}\alpha_{a}(b)=\mathsf{refl}_{a}.

is to use the contractibility of the total space of identity types twice:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B(x))}}}{\sum_{(y:B(x))}}{\sum_{(y:B(x))}}{\sum_{(y:B(x))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:a=x)}}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}\alpha(y)=p

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B(a))}}}{\sum_{(y:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y:B(a))}}\alpha(y)=\mathsf{refl}_{a}.

However, it is not clear at once that the underlying map of this composite of equivalences is indeed the map
\mathsf{coh\underline{\ }{}red}
defined in
Section

3
.

Proof of
Section

3
.

We show that the fibers are contractible:

\displaystyle\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{coh\underline{\ }{}red}}((x,y))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}(a,y^{\prime})=(x,y)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:a=x)}}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}\mathsf{tr}_{B}(p,y^{\prime})=y

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:B(a))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}{\sum_{(q:\alpha(y^{\prime})=\mathsf{refl}_{a})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:a=x)}}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}y^{\prime}=\mathsf{tr}_{B}(p^{-1},y)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:a=x)}}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}\alpha(\mathsf{tr}_{B}(p^{-1},y))=\mathsf{refl}_{a}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:a=x)}}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}{\sum_{(p:a=x)}}p=\alpha(y).\qed

Corollary 3.4
.

Consider a type
A
with a relation
R:A\to A\to\mathcal{U}
such that

\alpha:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}R(x,y)\to(x=y).

Then the map

\mathsf{coh\underline{\ }{}red}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:R(x,x))}}}{\sum_{(r:R(x,x))}}{\sum_{(r:R(x,x))}}{\sum_{(r:R(x,x))}}\alpha(r)=\mathsf{refl}_{x}\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:A)}}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}{\sum_{(x,y:A)}}R(x,y)\Big).

given by
(x,r,c)\mapsto(x,x,r)
is an equivalence.∎

Definition 3.5
.

For any reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
we define the span
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{A}
to consist of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

where
\tilde{A}_{0}\vcentcolon\equiv A_{0}
and
\tilde{A}_{1}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:A_{0})}}}{\sum_{(x,y:A_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:A_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:A_{0})}}A_{1}(x,y)
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
, and any type
X
, the map

f\mapsto(f_{0},f_{0},{\lambda}(x,y,e).\,{f}_{1}(e)):\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta X)\to\mathsf{cocone}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}(X),

is an equivalence, where
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is the span
(\tilde{A}_{1},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
from
A_{0}
to
A_{0}
.

Proof.

First observe that
\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta X)
is equivalent to the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{({f}_{0}:{A}_{0}\to X)}}}{\sum_{({f}_{0}:{A}_{0}\to X)}}{\sum_{({f}_{0}:{A}_{0}\to X)}}{\sum_{({f}_{0}:{A}_{0}\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{({f}_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\to f(x)=f(y))}}}{\sum_{({f}_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\to f(x)=f(y))}}{\sum_{({f}_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\to f(x)=f(y))}}{\sum_{({f}_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\to f(x)=f(y))}}\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}htpy}({\lambda}x.\,{f}_{1}(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(x)))=\mathsf{refl}_{{f}_{0}}.

We write
\mathsf{rGph}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A},\Delta X)
for the latter type. Furthermore, we observe that the type
\mathsf{cocone}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}(X)
is equivalent to the type

\mathsf{cocone}^{\prime}_{\tilde{A}}(X)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f,g:{A}_{0}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(f,g:{A}_{0}\to X)}}{\sum_{(f,g:{A}_{0}\to X)}}{\sum_{(f,g:{A}_{0}\to X)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\to f(x)=f(y).

Now we note that we have a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A},\Delta X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone}^{\prime}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}(X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{coh\underline{\ }{}red}}

where the map
\varphi
is the map in the statement, and the coherence reduction map uses the homotopy

(f,g,H)\mapsto\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}htpy}({\lambda}x.\,H(\mathsf{rfx}_{A}(x))).

Both vertical maps and the coherence reduction map are equivalences, so it follows that the asserted map is an equivalence.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a reflexive graph, and let
X
be a type equipped with
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta X)
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

X
is a reflexive coequalizer of
\mathcal{A}
.

(ii)

The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}_{0}}

which commutes by
{\lambda}(i,j,e).\,{f}_{1}(e)
, is a pushout square.

In particular, there is a reflexive coequalizer for every reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
, for which we write

\mathsf{constr}:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))).

Proof.

The triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

commutes, for any type
Y
. In this triangle, the bottom map is the map defined in
Section

3
, which is an equivalence, so the claim follows by the 3-for-2 property of equivalences.
∎

Example 3.8
.

(i)

The reflexive graph quotient of the discrete graph
\Delta(X)
of a type
X
is just
X
itself. It also follows that
\mathsf{constr}:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})))
is an equivalence of reflexive graphs if and only if
\mathcal{A}
is a discrete graph.

(ii)

The reflexive graph quotient of the indiscrete graph
\nabla(X)
on a type
X
is the join square
{X}\ast{X}
.

(iii)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a non-reflexive graph, and let
F(\mathcal{A})
be the reflexive graph obtained by freely adding a proof of reflexivity, as in
Item

(vi)
of
Section

2
. Then the non-reflexive graph quotient of
\mathcal{A}
is the reflexive graph quotient of
F(\mathcal{A})
.

(iv)

Let
f:A\to B
be a map. Then the reflexive coequalizer of the reflexive graph
(A,k(f))
is the fiberwise join
{A}\ast_{X}{A}
, which was introduced in
Section

2
.

(v)

Let
X
be a type with base point
x_{0}:X
. Define the reflexive graph
SX
by

\displaystyle{SX}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle{SX}_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,{\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,X

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{SX}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,x_{0}.

The reflexive graph quotient of
SX
is the suspension of
X
.

(vi)

The reflexive coequalizer of the walking edge
\mathcal{I}
is the interval, which is contractible.

4
Descent for reflexive coequalizers

Recall from
[
1
]
that morphism
f:X\to Y
is right orthogonal to a map
i:A\to B
if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}

is a pullback square. We use this way of stating the orthogonality condition in our definition of fibrations of reflexive graphs: a morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of reflexive graphs is said to be right orthogonal to a morphism
i:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}

is a pullback square.
1
1

1

One may note that the category of reflexive graphs is also locally cartesian closed (has
\Pi
-types), so that we could also state the orthogonality condition internally. Although this is straightforward, it is extra work and we will not need it in this thesis.

Definition 4.1
.

A morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is said to be a
left fibration
of reflexive graphs if it is right orthogonal to the morphism
0:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{I})
. Similarly, we say that
f
is a
right fibration
of reflexive graphs if it is right orthogonal to the morphism
1:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{I})
, and we say that
f
is a
fibration
if it is both a left and a right fibration.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
is a left or a right fibration. Then
f
is right orthogonal to the terminal projection
t:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathbf{1})
.

Proof.

We prove the assertion assuming that
f
is a left fibration, the case of a right fibration being similar. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{B})}

The square on the right is a pullback. Since the composite
\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{I}\to\mathbf{1}
is the identity morphism of reflexive graphs, the outer rectangle is also a pullback. Therefore the square on the left is a pullback.
∎

Definition 4.3
.

A morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
of reflexive graphs is said to be
left cartesian
if the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{B}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

is a pullback square. Similarly we say that
f
is
right cartesian
if the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{B}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

is a pullback square, and we say that
f
is
cartesian
if it is both left and right cartesian.

Example 4.4
.

A common way to obtain a cartesian morphism of reflexive graphs is via equifibered families.
An
equifibered family

\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{A}
consists of

\displaystyle{E}_{0}

\displaystyle:{A}_{0}\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle{E}_{1}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i,j:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(i,j)\to({E}_{0}(i)\simeq{E}_{0}(j))

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{E}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:{A}_{0}}{E}_{1}(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(i))\sim\mathsf{id}_{{E}_{0}(i)}

Given an equifibered family
\mathcal{E}
, we form the reflexive graph
\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})
by

\displaystyle{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(x:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x:{A}_{0})}}{E}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})}_{1}((x,u),(y,v))

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(x,y))}}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(x,y))}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(x,y))}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(x,y))}}{E}_{1}(e,u)=v

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})}(x,u)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(x),\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,u)).

There is an obvious projection morphism
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}),\mathcal{A})
, which is cartesian, because the squares in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s,t:\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}))}}}{\sum_{(s,t:\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}))}}{\sum_{(s,t:\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}))}}{\sum_{(s,t:\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}))}}{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(x,y:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

are pullback squares by
Section

3
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Consider
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
and
g:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B})
, and suppose that
f
is (left/right) cartesian. Then
g
is (left/right) cartesian if and only if
f\circ g
is (left/right) cartesian, respectively.

Proof.

Immediate by
Section

3
.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
be a morphism of reflexive graphs. The following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is a (left/right) fibration.

(ii)

f
is (left/right) cartesian.

Proof.

We only show that
f
is a left fibration if and only if
f
is left cartesian, the right case being similar.

For any morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
we have the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{B}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}

in which all vertical maps are equivalences. Therefore the top square is a pullback if and only if the bottom square is a pullback, which proves that (ii) holds if and only if (iii) holds.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Suppose that
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
is left or right cartesian. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{B}_{0})}}{B}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}

is a pullback square.

Proposition 4.8
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}

of reflexive graphs. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square.

(ii)

The squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{C}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{C}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X}_{1}}

are pullback squares.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

The following proposition is true more generally:

Proposition 4.9
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

of reflexive graphs, and let
h:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})
be a morphism of reflexive graphs. If
f
is right orthogonal to
h
, then so is
f^{\prime}
. In particular, if
f
is a (left/right) fibration, then so is
f^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Consider the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}).}

Note
\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{X},\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})
preserves pullbacks for any reflexive graph
\mathcal{X}
. Therefore it follows that the top and bottom squares are pullback squares. Moreover, the square on the front right is a pullback square by the assumption that
f
is right orthogonal to
h:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})
. Now it follows by the pasting property of pullbacks that the square on the back left is a pullback square. In other words,
f^{\prime}
is right orthogonal to
h
.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

For any map
f:X\to Y
, the morphism
\Delta f:\Delta X\to\Delta Y
is a fibration.

Proof.

The commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\Delta X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\Delta X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{I},\Delta Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\Delta Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}/1^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}/1^{\ast}}

is a pullback square, since both horizontal maps are equivalences.
∎

Theorem 4.11
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(1)

of reflexive graphs, where
f:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is a fibration of reflexive graphs. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square of reflexive graphs.

(ii)

The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)}

of types is a pullback square.

Proof.

The commuting square
Eq.

1
induces a commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

in which the two vertical squares in the back are pullback squares. Moreover, the square in
Eq.

1
is a pullback square if and only if the front two squares in the cube are pullback squares. The front two squares are pullback squares if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{0}\sqcup^{\tilde{B}_{1}}\tilde{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{A}_{0}\sqcup^{\tilde{A}_{1}}\tilde{A}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

is a pullback square. Since the pushouts on the left are reflexive coequalizers, the claim follows.
∎

5
Colimits of diagrams over graphs

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a reflexive graph. A diagram
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{A}
is a triple
({D}_{0},{D}_{1},\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{D}})
consisting of

\displaystyle{D}_{0}

\displaystyle:{A}_{0}\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle{D}_{1}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i,j:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(i,j)\to({D}_{0}(i)\to{D}_{0}(j))

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:{A}_{0}}{D}_{1}(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(i))\sim\mathsf{id}_{{D}_{0}(i)}.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a diagram over
\mathcal{A}
. We form the
total graph

\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
of
\mathcal{D}
by

\displaystyle{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}{D}_{0}(i)

\displaystyle{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}_{1}((i,x),(j,y))

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}{D}_{1}(e,x)=y

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}((i,x))

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(i),\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{D}}(i,x)).

There is an obvious projection
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{A})
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any diagram
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{A}
, the projection
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{A})
is a left fibration.

Proof.

It suffices to show that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{((i,x),(j,y):{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}_{0})}}}{\sum_{((i,x),(j,y):{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}_{0})}}{\sum_{((i,x),(j,y):{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}_{0})}}{\sum_{((i,x),(j,y):{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}_{0})}}{\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}_{1}((i,x),(j,y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

is a pullback square. Note that this square is equivalent to the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}{\sum_{(e:{A}_{1}(i,j))}}{D}_{0}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i:{A}_{0})}}{D}_{0}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{\sum_{(i,j:{A}_{0})}}{A}_{1}(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}_{0},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}(i,j,e,x).\,(i,x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

which is clearly a pullback square.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

It can be shown that the total graph operation is an equivalence from diagrams over
\mathcal{A}
to left fibrations over
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
D
be a diagram over
\mathcal{A}
, and let
X
be a type. A
D
-cocone on
X
is a morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}),\Delta X)
of reflexive graphs. A
D
-cocone
f
on
X
is colimiting if the map

\Delta(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})\circ f:(X\to Y)\to\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}),\Delta Y)

is an equivalence for every type
Y
.

Remark 5.6
.

By
Section

3
it follows that every diagram
D
over any reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
has a colimit.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be diagrams over
\mathcal{A}
. A
natural transformation

\tau:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
of diagrams consists of

\displaystyle{\tau}_{0}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:{A}_{0}}{D^{\prime}}_{0}(i)\to{D}_{0}(i)

\displaystyle{\tau}_{1}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}*{i,j:{A}_{0}}{e:{A}_{1}(i,j)}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{D}_{0}(i)}{\tau}_{0}({D^{\prime}}_{1}(e,x))={D}_{1}(e,{\tau}_{0}(x))

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\tau}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:{A}_{0}}{x:{D}_{0}(i)}{\tau}_{1}(\mathsf{rfx}_{A}(i),x)=\mathsf{refl}_{{\tau}_{0}(x)}

A natural transformation
\tau:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
of diagrams over
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
cartesian
if the commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}_{0}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}_{0}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}_{0}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}_{0}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}_{1}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau}_{0}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau}_{0}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}_{1}(e)}

are all pullback squares.

We will use the following general fact about pullbacks.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
I
be a type, and consider for each
i:I
a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}

with
H_{i}:f_{i}\circ p_{i}\sim g_{i}\circ q_{i}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

For each
i:I
, the square is a pullback square.

(ii)

The induced square on total spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}X_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(f)}

which commutes via the homotopy
\mathsf{tot}(H):\mathsf{tot}(f)\circ\mathsf{tot}(p)\sim\mathsf{tot}(g)\circ\mathsf{tot}(q)
, is a pullback square.

Proof.

The gap map of the square in assertion (ii) factors as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}\times_{X_{i}}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}A_{i}\Big)\times_{\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}X_{i}\big)}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(i:I)}}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}{\sum_{(i:I)}}B_{i}\Big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(\mathsf{gap}(p_{i},q_{i},H_{i}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{gap}(\mathsf{tot}(p),\mathsf{tot}(q),\mathsf{tot}(H))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}(i,a,b,p).\,((i,a),(i,b),(\mathsf{refl}_{i},p))}

and the bottom map is an equivalence. Therefore it follows by the 3-for-2 property and an application of
Section

2
that
\mathsf{gap}(p_{i},q_{i},H_{i})
is an equivalence for each
i:I
, if and only if
\mathsf{gap}(\mathsf{tot}(p),\mathsf{tot}(q),\mathsf{tot}(H))
is an equivalence.
∎

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
\tau:\mathsf{nat}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
be a natural transformation of diagrams over
\mathcal{A}
. Then
\tau
is cartesian if and only if the induced morphism of reflexive graphs

\mathsf{tot}(\tau):\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}),\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}))

is cartesian.

Proof.

Straightforward consequence of
Section

5
.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\tau:\mathsf{cart}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
be a cartesian morphisms of diagrams over
\mathcal{A}
, and consider a commuting square of reflexive graphs of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(\tau)}

Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square.

(ii)

The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\tau)}

is a pullback square.

6
Sequential colimits

Type sequences are diagrams of the following form.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

Their formal specification is as follows.

Definition 6.1
.

An
(increasing) type sequence

\mathcal{A}
consists of

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to A_{n+1}.

Sequential colimits are characterized by their universal property.

Definition 6.2
.

(i)

A
(sequential) cocone
on a type sequence
\mathcal{A}
with vertex
X
consists of

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to X

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}f_{n}\sim f_{n+1}\circ H_{n}.

We write
\mathsf{cocone}(X)
for the type of cones with vertex
X
.

(ii)

Given a cone
(h,H)
with vertex
X
on a type sequence
\mathcal{A}
we define the map

\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}(h,H):(X\to Y)\to\mathsf{cocone}(X)

given by
f\mapsto(f\circ h,{\lambda}n.\,{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{ap}_{f}(H_{n}(x)))
.

(iii)

We say that a cone
(h,H)
with vertex
X
is
colimiting
if the map
\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}(h,H)
is an equivalence for any type
Y
.

In order to study sequential colimits, we first note that type sequences appear as diagrams over the graph
(\mathbb{N},\prec)
with type of vertices
\mathbb{N}
, and with

(n\prec m)\vcentcolon\equiv(n+1=m).

Note that this is a non-reflexive graph. A reflexive graph is obtained by the adjunction explained in
Item

(vi)
of
Section

2
, which transforms the relation
\prec
into the reflexive relation
\preceq
given by

(n\preceq m)\vcentcolon\equiv(n+1=m)+(n=m).

A diagram
\mathcal{A}
on
(\mathbb{N},\prec)
consists of

{A}_{0}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\\
{A}_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n,m:\mathbb{N}}{e:n+1=m}A_{n}\to A_{m}.

Note that the type of
{A}_{1}
is equivalent to the type
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to A_{n+1}
, so we see indeed that type sequences are equivalently described as diagrams over
(\mathbb{N},\prec)
. Similarly, a sequential cocone on
\mathcal{A}
with vertex
X
is equivalently described as a cocone on the diagram
\mathcal{A}
with vertex
X
. Thus, we also have the following:

Lemma 6.3
.

Consider a cocone
(h,H)
with vertex
B
for a type sequence
\mathcal{A}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The cocone
(h,H)
is colimiting.

(ii)

The cocone
(h,H)
is inductive in the sense that for every type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
, the map

\displaystyle\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P(b)\Big)\to{}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}P(h_{n}(x)))}}}{\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}P(h_{n}(x)))}}{\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}P(h_{n}(x)))}}{\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}P(h_{n}(x)))}}

\displaystyle\qquad{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}\mathsf{tr}_{P}(H_{n}(x),h_{n}(x))={h_{n+1}(f_{n}(x))}

given by

s\mapsto({\lambda}n.\,s\circ h_{n},{\lambda}n.\,{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{apd}_{s}(H_{n}(x)))

has a section.

(iii)

The map in (ii) is an equivalence.

Definition 6.4
.

The type of
descent data
on a type sequence
\mathcal{A}\equiv(A,f)
is defined to be

\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(B:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(B:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to\mathcal{U})}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}B_{n}(x)\simeq B_{n+1}(f_{n}(x)).

Equivalently, if
\mathcal{A}
is seen as a diagram over
(\mathbb{N},\prec)
,

Definition 6.5
.

We define a map

\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}:(A_{\infty}\to\mathcal{U})\to\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{A})

by
B\mapsto({\lambda}n.\,{\lambda}x.\,B(\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}(n,x)),{\lambda}n.\,{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{tr}_{B}(\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}glue}(n,x)))
.

Theorem 6.6
.

The map

\mathsf{desc\underline{\ }{}fam}:(A_{\infty}\to\mathcal{U})\to\mathsf{Desc}(\mathcal{A})

is an equivalence.

Definition 6.7
.

A
cartesian transformation
of type sequences from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is a pair
(h,H)
consisting of

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to B_{n}

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}g_{n}\circ h_{n}\sim h_{n+1}\circ f_{n},

such that each of the squares in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}

is a pullback square. We define

\displaystyle\mathsf{cart}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to B_{n})}}}{\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to B_{n})}}{\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to B_{n})}}{\sum_{(h:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to B_{n})}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}g_{n}\circ h_{n}\sim h_{n+1}\circ f_{n})}}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}g_{n}\circ h_{n}\sim h_{n+1}\circ f_{n})}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}g_{n}\circ h_{n}\sim h_{n+1}\circ f_{n})}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}g_{n}\circ h_{n}\sim h_{n+1}\circ f_{n})}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}pullback}(h_{n},f_{n},H_{n}),

and we write

\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{B})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\mathcal{A}:\mathsf{Seq})}}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{A}:\mathsf{Seq})}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{A}:\mathsf{Seq})}}{\sum_{(\mathcal{A}:\mathsf{Seq})}}\mathsf{cart}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Definition 6.8
.

We define a map

\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}map}(\mathcal{B}):\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X^{\prime}:\mathcal{U})}}X^{\prime}\to X\Big)\to\mathsf{Cart}(\mathcal{B}).

which associates to any morphism
h:X^{\prime}\to X
a cartesian transformation of type sequences into
\mathcal{B}
.

Theorem 6.9
.

The operation
\mathsf{cart\underline{\ }{}map}(\mathcal{B})
is an equivalence.

The flattening lemma for sequential colimits essentially states that sequential colimits commute with
\Sigma
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Consider

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}B_{n}(x)\simeq B_{n+1}(f_{n}(x)).

and suppose
P:A_{\infty}\to\mathcal{U}
is the unique family equipped with

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}(x)\simeq P(\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}(n,x))

and homotopies
H_{n}(x)
witnessing that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}(f_{n}(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}(n,x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}(n+1,f_{n}(x)))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{n}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{n+1}(f_{n}(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tr}_{P}(\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}glue}(n,x))}

commutes. Then
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(t:A_{\infty})}}}{\sum_{(t:A_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(t:A_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(t:A_{\infty})}}P(t)
satisfies the universal property of the sequential colimit of the type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A_{0})}}}{\sum_{(x:A_{0})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{0})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{0})}}B_{0}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A_{1})}}}{\sum_{(x:A_{1})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{1})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{1})}}B_{1}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A_{2})}}}{\sum_{(x:A_{2})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{2})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{2})}}B_{2}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}_{f_{0}}(g_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}_{f_{1}}(g_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}_{f_{2}}(g_{2})}

In the following theorem we rephrase the flattening lemma in using cartesian transformations of type sequences.

Theorem 6.11
.

Consider a commuting diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

If each of the vertical squares is a pullback square, and
Y
is the sequential colimit of the type sequence
B_{n}
, then
X
is the sequential colimit of the type sequence
A_{n}
.

Corollary 6.12
.

Consider a commuting diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

If each of the vertical squares is a pullback square, then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

is a pullback square.

Chapter 4
Homotopy images

We have observed in
Section

3
that the reflexive coequalizer of the pre-kernel of a map
f:A\to X
is the fiberwise join
{A}\ast_{X}{A}
, i.e. we have a reflexive coequalizer diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast_{X}{A}.}

It can also be shown that the colimit of the 2-pre-kernel of a map
f:A\to X
is the triple fiberwise join
{A}\ast_{X}{{A}\ast_{X}{A}}
, i.e. we have a colimiting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}A\times_{X}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast_{X}{{A}\ast_{X}{A}},}

although one has to take care to use the right amount of coherence data.
These results suggest that geometric realization of the Cech nerve of a map
f:A\to X
, i.e. the homotopy image of
f
, is the sequential colimit of the type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast_{X}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast_{X}{({A}\ast_{X}{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

and its sequential colimit. We do not have a way of presenting the Cech nerve of a map in type theory, due to the infinite coherence problem of presenting simplicial types in type theory. Nevertheless, we
can
analyze this type sequence and its colimit in homotopy type theory, since it is constructed entirely in terms of known operations. We will show in
2.13
that for any map
f:A\to X
, the infinite fiberwise join-power
{\cdots}\ast_{X}{{A}\ast_{X}{{A}\ast_{X}{A}}}
is the image of
f
. We conclude that the image of a map always exists in univalent type theory with homotopy pushouts. We note that an earlier construction of the propositional truncation in a similar setting is due to van Doorn
[
15
]
, and another one is due to Kraus
[
20
]
. The present construction of the image of
f
is called the join construction implies, as we show in
2.17
, that the image of an essentially small type mapping into a locally small type is again essentially small. In particular, the image of a map from a small type into the universe is essentially small. This corollary should be viewed as a type theoretic replacement axiom. This fact has the important consequence that any connected component of the universe is essentially small. We also note that the join construction leads to new constructions of set quotients and of Rezk completions, see
[
27
]
, where also a construction of the
n
-truncations is given as an application of the join construction.

1
The universal property of the image

Definition 1.1
.

Consider a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

with
I:f\sim m\circ i
, and where
m
is an embedding.
We say that
m
has the
universal property of the image of
f
if the map

(i,I)^{\ast}:\mathrm{hom}_{X}(m,m^{\prime})\to\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,m^{\prime})

defined by
(i,I)^{\ast}(h,H)\vcentcolon\equiv(h\circ i,I\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(i\cdot H))
,
is an equivalence for every embedding
m^{\prime}:U^{\prime}\to X
.

Lemma 1.2
.

For any
f:A\to X
and any embedding
m:U\to X
, the type
\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,m)
is a proposition.

Proof.

From
Section

5
we obtain that the type
\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,m)
is equivalent to the type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{m}(x),

so it suffices to show that this is a proposition.
Recall from
Section

2
that a map is an embedding if and only if its fibers are propositions.
Thus we see that the type
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{m}(x)
is a product of propositions, so it is a proposition by
Section

3
.
∎

Corollary 1.3
.

Consider a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

with
I:f\sim m\circ i
, and where
m
is an embedding. Then
m
satisfies the universal property of the image of
f
if and only if the implication

\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,m^{\prime})\to\mathrm{hom}_{X}(m,m^{\prime})

holds for every embedding
m^{\prime}:U^{\prime}\to X
.

Recall that embeddings into the unit type are just propositions.
Therefore, the universal property of the image of the map
A\to\mathbf{1}
is a proposition
P
satisfying the universal property of the propositional truncation:

Definition 1.4
.

Let
A
be a type, and let
P
be a proposition that comes equipped with a map
f:A\to P
. We say that
f:A\to P
satisfies the
universal property of propositional truncation
if for every proposition
Q
, the precomposition map

\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f:(P\to Q)\to(A\to Q)

is an equivalence.

2
The join construction

1
Step one: constructing the propositional truncation

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
f:A\to P
, where
A
is any type, and
P
is a proposition.
Then the map

({A}\ast{B}\to P)\to(B\to P)

given by
h\mapsto h\circ{\mathsf{inr}}
is an equivalence, for any type
B
.

Proof.

Since both types are propositions by
Section

3
it suffices to construct a map

(B\to P)\to({A}\ast{B}\to P).

Let
g:B\to P
. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes since
P
is a proposition. Therefore we obtain a map
{A}\ast{B}\to P
by the universal property of the join.
∎

The idea of the construction of the propositional truncation is that if we are given a map
f:A\to P
, where
P
is a proposition, then it extends uniquely along
{\mathsf{inr}}:A\to{A}\ast{A}
to a map
{A}\ast{A}\to P
. This extension again extends uniquely along
{\mathsf{inr}}:{A}\ast{A}\to{A}\ast{({A}\ast{A})}
to a map
{A}\ast{({A}\ast{A})}\to P
and so on, resulting in a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast{({A}\ast{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}

Definition 2.2
.

The
join powers

A^{\ast n}
of a type
X
are defined by

\displaystyle A^{\ast 0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle A^{\ast 1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv A

\displaystyle A^{\ast(n+1)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{A}\ast{A^{\ast n}}.

Furthermore, we define
A^{\ast\infty}
to be the sequential colimit of the type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\ast 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\ast 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\ast 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}

Our goal is now to show that
A^{\ast\infty}
is a proposition and satisfies the universal property of the propositional truncation.

Lemma 2.3
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

with sequential colimit
A_{\infty}
, and let
P
be a proposition. Then the map

\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}^{\ast}:(A_{\infty}\to P)\to\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to P\Big)

given by
h\mapsto{\lambda}n.\,(h\circ\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}_{n})
is an equivalence.

Proof.

By the universal property of sequential colimits established in
Section

6
we obtain that
\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}
is an equivalence. Note that we have a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{A_{\infty}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to P\Big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cocone\underline{\ }{}map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

Note that for any
g:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to P
the type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}g_{n}\sim g_{n+1}\circ f_{n}

is a product of contractible types, since
P
is a proposition. Therefore it is contractible by
Section

3
, and it follows that the projection is an equivalence. We conclude by the 3-for-2 property of equivalences that
\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}^{\ast}
is an equivalence.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
A
be a type, and let
P
be a proposition. Then the function

\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}_{0}:(A^{\ast\infty}\to P)\to(A\to P)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

We have the commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{A^{\ast\infty}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A^{\ast n}\to P\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{A}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}h.\,h_{0}}

Therefore it suffices to show that the bottom map is an equivalence. Since this is a map between propositions, it suffices to construct a map in the converse direction. Let
f:A\to P
. We will construct a term of type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A^{\ast n}\to P

by induction on
n:\mathbb{N}
. The base case is trivial. Given a map
g:A^{\ast n}\to P
, we obtain a map
g:A^{\ast(n+1)}\to P
by
Section

1
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
