\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

and suppose that each
A_{n}
is equipped with a base point
a_{n}:A_{n}
, and each
f_{n}
is equipped with a homotopy
H_{n}:\mathsf{const}_{a_{n+1}}\sim f_{n}
. Then the sequential colimit
A_{\infty}
is contractible.∎

Lemma 2.6
.

The type
A^{\ast\infty}
is a proposition for any type
A
.

Proof.

By
Section

2
it suffices to show that
A^{\ast\infty}\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A^{\ast\infty})
, and by
Section

1
it suffices to show that

A\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A^{\ast\infty}),

because
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A^{\ast\infty})
is a proposition.

Let
x:A
. To see that
A^{\ast\infty}
is contractible it suffices by
Section

1
to show that
{\mathsf{inr}}:A^{\ast n}\to A^{\ast(n+1)}
is homotopic to the constant function
\mathsf{const}_{{\mathsf{inl}}(x)}
. However, we get a homotopy
\mathsf{const}_{{\mathsf{inl}}(x)}\sim{\mathsf{inr}}
immediately from the path constructor
\mathsf{glue}
.
∎

Theorem 2.7
.

For any type
A:\mathcal{U}
there is a proposition
\|A\|:\mathcal{U}
that comes equipped with a map
\eta:A\to\|A\|
, and satisfies the universal property of propositional truncation.

Proof.

Let
A
be a type. Then we define
\|A\|\vcentcolon\equiv A^{\ast\infty}
, and we define
\eta\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}_{0}:A\to A^{\ast\infty}
. Then
\|A\|
is a proposition by
Section

1
, and
\eta:A\to\|A\|
satisfies the universal property of propositional truncation by
Section

1
.
∎

2
Step two: constructing the image of a map

Following Definition 7.6.3 of
[
33
]
, we recall that the image of a map
f:A\to X
can be defined using the propositional truncation:

Definition 2.8
.

For any map
f:A\to X
we define the
image
of
f
to be the type

\im(f)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}\|\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)\|

and we define the
image inclusion
to be the projection
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\im(f)\to X
.

However, the construction of the fiberwise join in
Section

2
suggests that we can also define the image of
f
as the infinite join power
f^{\ast\infty}
, where we repeatedly take the fiberwise join of
f
with itself. Our reason for defining the image in this way is twofold:

•

We use this construction to show that the image of a map
f:A\to B
from an essentially small type
A
into a locally small type
B
is again essentially small.

•

Some interesting types, such as the real and complex projective spaces, appear in specific instances of this construction.

Lemma 2.9
.

Consider a map
f:A\to X
, an embedding
m:U\to X
, and
h:\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,m)
. Then the map

\mathrm{hom}_{X}({f}\ast{g},m)\to\mathrm{hom}_{X}(g,m)

is an equivalence for any
g:B\to X
.

Proof.

Note that both types are propositions, so any equivalence can be used to prove the claim. Thus, we simply calculate

\displaystyle\mathrm{hom}_{X}({f}\ast{g},m)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{{f}\ast{g}}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{m}(x)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}{\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)}\ast{\mathsf{fib}_{g}(x)}\to\mathsf{fib}_{m}(x)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{fib}_{g}(x)\to\mathsf{fib}_{m}(x)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{hom}_{X}(g,m).

The first equivalence holds by
Section

5
; the second equivalence holds by
3.15
; the third equivalence holds by
Section

1
; the last equivalence again holds by
Section

5
.
∎

For the construction of the image of
f:A\to X
we observe that if we are given an embedding
m:U\to X
and a map
(i,I):\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,m)
, then
(i,I)
extends uniquely along
{\mathsf{inr}}:A\to{A}\ast_{X}{A}
to a map
\mathrm{hom}_{X}({f}\ast{f},m)
. This extension again extends uniquely along
{\mathsf{inr}}:{A}\ast_{X}{A}\to{A}\ast_{X}{({A}\ast_{X}{A})}
to a map
\mathrm{hom}_{X}({f}\ast{({f}\ast{f})},m)
and so on, resulting in a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast_{X}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}\ast_{X}{({A}\ast_{X}{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}

Definition 2.10
.

Suppose
f:A\to X
is a map. Then we define the
fiberwise join powers

f^{\ast n}\vcentcolon\equiv A_{X}^{\ast n}\to X.

Construction.

Note that the operation
(B,g)\mapsto({A}\ast_{X}{B},{f}\ast{g})
defines an endomorphism on the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(B:\mathcal{U})}}B\to X.

We also have
(\mathbf{0},\mathsf{ind}_{\mathbf{0}})
and
(A,f)
of this type. For
n\geq 1
we define

\displaystyle A_{X}^{\ast(n+1)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{A}\ast_{X}{A_{X}^{\ast n}}

\displaystyle f^{\ast(n+1)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{f}\ast{f^{\ast n}}.\qed

Definition 2.11
.

We define
A_{X}^{\ast\infty}
to be the sequential colimit of the type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{X}^{\ast 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{X}^{\ast 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{X}^{\ast 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}

Since we have a cocone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{X}^{\ast 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{X}^{\ast 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{X}^{\ast 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast 1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{inr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast 2}}

we also obtain a map
f^{\ast\infty}:A_{X}^{\ast\infty}\to X
by the universal property of
A_{X}^{\ast\infty}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
f:A\to X
be a map, and let
m:U\to X
be an embedding. Then the function

\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}_{0}:\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f^{\ast\infty},m)\to\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,m)

is an equivalence.

Theorem 2.13
.

For any map
f:A\to X
, the map
f^{\ast\infty}:A_{X}^{\ast\infty}\to X
is an embedding that satisfies the universal property of the image inclusion of
f
.

3
Step three: establishing the smallness of the image

Recall from
Section

5
that a type is said to be locally small if its identity types are equivalent to small types.

Lemma 2.14
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}

(i)

If the square is cartesian,
B
and
C
are essentially small, and
D
is locally small, then
A
is essentially small.

(ii)

If the square is cocartesian, and
A
,
B
, and
C
are essentially small, then
D
is essentially small.

Corollary 2.15
.

Suppose
f:A\to X
and
g:B\to X
are maps from essentially small types
A
and
B
, respectively, to a locally small type
X
. Then
A\times_{X}B
is again essentially small.

Lemma 2.16
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

where each
A_{n}
is essentially small. Then its sequential colimit is again essentially small.

Theorem 2.17
.

For any map
f:A\to X
from a small type
A
into a locally small type
X
, the image
\im(f)
is an essentially small type.

Recall that in set theory, the replacement axiom asserts that for any family of sets
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
indexed by a set
I
, there is a set
X[I]
consisting of precisely those sets
x
for which there exists an
i\in I
such that
x\in X_{i}
. In other words: the image of a set-indexed family of sets is again a set. Without the replacement axiom,
X[I]
would be a class. In the following corollary we establish a type-theoretic analogue of the replacement axiom: the image of a family of small types indexed by a small type is again (essentially) small.

Corollary 2.18
.

For any small type family
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
, where
A
is small, the image
\im(B)
is essentially small. We call
\im(B)
the
univalent completion
of
B
.

Chapter 5
Reflective subuniverses

In this chapter we study reflective subuniverses. Reflective subuniverses were first introduced in section 7.7 of
[
33
]
, and were studied in much more detail in
[
28
]
.

In
Section

1
we establish the basic closure properties of reflective subuniverses. In particular, we show in
Section

1
that pullbacks of
L
-local types are again
L
-local. It follows that cartesian products of
L
-local types and identity types of
L
-local types are again
L
-local. In
Section

1
we show that any dependent product of
L
-local types is also
L
-local, regardless of whether the indexing type is
L
-local or not. We use this fact in
Section

1
to show that the map
{\eta}\times{\eta}:X\times Y\to LX\times LY
is an
L
-localization, and that the
L
-localization of a proposition is again a proposition.

In
Section

7
we study accessible reflective subuniverses.

In
Section

2
we introduce the notion of
L
-separated type, for any reflective subuniverse
L
, and we show that the subuniverse of
L
-separated types is again a reflective subuniverse. The contents of
Section

2
are joint work with Dan Christensen, Morgan Opie, and Luis Scoccola. As a corollary we obtain in
2.17
that the
k
-truncation can be constructed, for any
k\geq-2
. A more elementary way of obtaining this result appears in
[
27
]
.

1
Localizations

A
subuniverse
is simply a subtype of the universe. Note that we do not require that subuniverses are closed under any type constructors
1
1

1

In particular, we will see that reflective subuniverses aren’t necessarily closed under
\Sigma
.

For any subuniverse
P:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
, we write
\mathcal{U}_{P}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}P(X)
, and we say that
X:\mathcal{U}
is a
P
-type if
X
is in
\mathcal{U}_{P}
, i.e. if
P(X)
holds.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
P:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
be a subuniverse, and let
X
be a type. A
P
-localization
of
X
is a triple
(Y,l,p)
consisting of a
P
-type
Y:\mathcal{U}_{P}
, a map
l:X\to Y
, and a term
p
witnessing that the map

l^{\ast}:(Y\to Z)\to(X\to Z)

is an equivalence, for every
Z:\mathcal{U}_{P}
. This property is also called the
universal property
of the
P
-localization of
X
.

In other words, a
P
-localization of
X
is a map
l:X\to Y
into a
P
-type
Y
, such that every map
f:X\to Z
into a
P
-type
Z
extends uniquely along
l
, as indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

Proposition 1.2
.

For any subuniverse
P:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
and any type
X
, the type of
P
-localizations of
X
is a proposition.

Proof.

Consider
(Y,f,p)
and
(Y^{\prime},f^{\prime},p^{\prime})
of the described type. Since
p
and
p^{\prime}

are terms of a proposition, it suffices to show that
(Y,f)=(Y^{\prime},f^{\prime})
. In
other words, we have to find an equivalence
g:Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
g\circ f^{\prime}=f
.

By
p(Y^{\prime})
, the type of
pairs
(g,h)
consisting of a function
g:Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
h:g\circ f=f^{\prime}
is contractible. By

p^{\prime}(Y)
, the type of pairs
(g^{\prime},h^{\prime})
consisting of a function
g^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to Y

such that
h^{\prime}:g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}=f
is contractible.

Now
g^{\prime}\circ g
is a function such that
g^{\prime}\circ g\circ f=g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}=f
, as
is
\mathsf{id}_{Y}
. By contractibility, it follows that
g^{\prime}\circ g=\mathsf{id}_{Y}
.
Similarly,
g\circ g^{\prime}=\mathsf{id}_{Y^{\prime}}
.
∎

Proposition 1.3
.

Consider a subuniverse
P:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
and a
P
-localization
l:X\to Y
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

X
is a
P
-type (i.e.
X
is in
\mathcal{U}_{P}
).

(ii)

The
P
-localization
l:X\to Y
is an equivalence.

(iii)

The map
{l}^{\ast}:(Y\to X)\to(X\to X)
is an equivalence.

(iv)

The
P
-localization
l:X\to Y
has a retraction.

Proof.

Certainly if
l
is an equivalence, then
X
is in
P
since it is equivalent to the type
Y:\mathcal{U}_{P}
.
Conversely, if
X
is in
P
then
\mathsf{id}_{X}
has the same universal property of
l
; so by
Section

1
they are equivalent and hence
l
is an equivalence. This shows that (i) holds if and only if (ii) holds.

It is clear that (ii) implies (iii). Furthermore, (iii) implies (iv) because the fiber of
{l}^{\ast}
at
\mathsf{id}_{X}
is contractible. In particular, there is a function
g:Y\to X
equipped with a homotopy
g\circ l\sim\mathsf{id}
. In other words,
l
has a retraction. To see that (iv) implies (ii), suppose
g
is a retraction of
l
, i.e.
g\circ l=\mathsf{id}_{X}
.
Then
l\circ g\circ l=l
, so
l\circ g
is a factorization of
l
through itself.
By uniqueness of such factorizations,
l\circ g=\mathsf{id}_{Y}
.
Thus
g
is also a section of
l
, hence
l
is an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 1.4
.

For any subuniverse
P
, the unit type
\mathbf{1}
has a
P
-localization if and only if
\mathbf{1}
is already a
P
-type.

Proof.

Immediate from the fact that
\mathbf{1}
is a retract of any pointed type.
∎

The universal property of
P
-localization is by itself not sufficient to imply a dependent universal property. However, we have the following ‘constrained’ dependent universal property.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
P
be a subuniverse, and let
l:X\to Y
be a
P
-localization. Furthermore, consider a type family
Z:Y\to\mathcal{U}
such that the total space
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}Z(y)
is a
P
-type.
Then the precomposition map

{l}^{\ast}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:Y}Z(y)\Big)\longrightarrow\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}Z(l(x))\Big)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Since
Y
is a
P
-type and
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}Z(y)
is a
P
-type, the precomposition maps
{l}^{\ast}
in the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}Z(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}Z(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y\to Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\to Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{l}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{l}^{\ast}}

are equivalences. It follows that they induce an equivalence from the fiber of the left-hand map
\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}
at
\mathsf{id}_{Y}
to the fiber of the right-hand map
\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}
at
l
. In other words, we have an equivalence

{l}^{\ast}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:Y}Z(y)\Big)\longrightarrow\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}Z(l(y))\Big).\qed

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
P:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
be a subuniverse, and write
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{P}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U}_{P})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U}_{P})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U}_{P})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U}_{P})}}X
. The projection

\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{P}\to\mathcal{U}_{P}
classifies the small maps whose fibers satisfy
P
.

Proof.

Let
f:Y\to X
be any map into
X
. Then
\mathsf{fib}_{f}:X\to\mathcal{U}
factors through

\mathcal{U}_{P}
if and only if all the fibers of
f
satisfy
P
. Let us write

P(f)
for
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x))
. Then we see that the equivalence

\chi
of Theorem 4.8.3 of
[
34
]
restricts to an
equivalence

\chi^{P}:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:Y\to X)}}}{\sum_{(f:Y\to X)}}{\sum_{(f:Y\to X)}}{\sum_{(f:Y\to X)}}P(f))\to(X\to\mathcal{U}_{P}).

Now observe that the outer square and the square on the right in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}y.\,{\mathopen{}(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(f(y)),\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt},{\mathopen{}(y,\mathsf{refl}_{f(y)})\mathclose{}})\mathclose{}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{f}}

are pullback squares. Hence the square on the left is a pullback square.
∎

Definition 1.7
.

A
reflective subuniverse

L
is a subuniverse
\mathcal{U}_{L}\to\mathcal{U}
equipped with an
L
-localization

{\eta_{X}}:X\to LX

for every type
X:\mathcal{U}
. The
L
-localization
{\eta_{X}}:X\to LX
is sometimes also called the
unit
of the localization. A type in
\mathcal{U}_{L}
is also said to be
local
.

Theorem 1.8
.

The data of any two reflective subuniverses with the same local types are the same.

Proof.

Immediate from the fact that the type of localizations is a proposition.
∎

Lemma 1.9
.

Any reflective subuniverse is a functor up to homotopy: given
f:A\to B
we have an induced map
Lf:LA\to LB
, preserving identities and composition up to homotopy.
Moreover,
{\eta}
is a natural transformation up to homotopy, i.e. for any
f
we have
Lf\circ{\eta_{A}}={\eta_{B}}\circ f
.

Proof.

Define
Lf
to be the unique function such that
Lf\circ{\eta_{A}}={\eta_{B}}\circ f
, using the universal property of
{\eta_{A}}
.
The rest is easy to check using further universal properties.
∎

Corollary 1.10
.

The subuniverse of
L
-local types is closed under retracts.

Proof.

Consider an
L
-local type
Y
, and suppose that
i:X\to Y
has a retraction
r:Y\to X
. By the functoriality of
L
it follows that
LX
is also a retract of
LY
, since we have
Lr\circ Li\sim\mathsf{id}
. Therefore we see that the
L
-localization
{\eta}:X\to LX
is a retract of the
L
-localization
{\eta}:Y\to LY
, as indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LX.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Li}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lr}

Since
{\eta}:Y\to LY
is an equivalence, and equivalences are closed under retracts, it follows that
{\eta}:X\to LX
is an equivalence, hence
X
is
L
-local.
∎

Definition 1.11
.

A map
f:A\to B
is said to be an
L
-equivalence
if
Lf:LA\to LB
is an equivalence.

Proposition 1.12
.

For a map
f:A\to B
the following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is an
L
-equivalence.

(ii)

For any
L
-local type
X
, the precomposition map

{f}^{\ast}:(B\to X)\to(A\to X)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Suppose first that
f
is an
L
-equivalence, and let
X
be
L
-local. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{LB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{LA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Lf}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}^{\ast}}

commutes. In this square the two vertical maps are equivalences by the universal property of localization, and the top map is an equivalence since
Lf
is an equivalence. Therefore the map
{f}^{\ast}:X^{B}\to X^{A}
is an equivalence, as desired.

Conversely, assume that
{f}^{\ast}:X^{B}\to X^{A}
is an equivalence for every
L
-local type
X
. By the square above it follows that
{Lf}^{\ast}:X^{LB}\to X^{LA}
is an equivalence for every
L
-local type
X
. The fiber of
LA^{LB}\to LA^{LA}
at
\mathsf{id}:LA\to LA
is contractible, so we obtain a retraction
g
of
Lf
. To see that
g
is also a section observe that the fiber of
LB^{LB}\to LB^{LA}
at
Lf
is contractible. This fiber contains
(\mathsf{id}_{LB},\mathsf{refl}_{Lf})
. However, we also have an identification
p:{Lf}^{\ast}(Lf\circ g)=Lf
, since

{Lf}^{\ast}(Lf\circ g)\equiv(Lf\circ g)\circ Lf\equiv Lf\circ(g\circ Lf)=Lf.

Therefore
(Lf\circ g,p)
is in the fiber of
{Lf}^{\ast}:LB^{LB}\to LB^{LA}
at
Lf
. By the contractibility of the fibers it follows that
(Lf\circ g,p)=(\mathsf{id}_{LB},\mathsf{refl}_{Lf})
, so it follows that
Lf\circ g=\mathsf{id}_{LB}
. In other words,
g
is both a retraction and a section of
Lf
, so
Lf
is an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 1.13
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map into an
L
-local type
Y
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is an
L
-localization.

(ii)

f
is an
L
-equivalence.

In particular, the map
{\eta_{X}}:X\to LX
is an
L
-equivalence.

Corollary 1.14
.

Any retract of an
L
-localization is again an
L
-localization.

Proof.

Suppose that
f:A\to B
is a retract of an
L
-localization
l:X\to Y
. Then
B
is
L
-local by the previous claim.
Moreover,
Lf
is a retract of
Ll
, which is an equivalence, so
f
is an
L
-equivalence. Therefore
f
is an
L
-localization by
Section

1
.
∎

Proposition 1.15
.

Given a reflective subuniverse
L
, a type
X
is
L
-local if and only if
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f):(B\to X)\to(A\to X)
is an equivalence, for any
L
-equivalence
f:A\to B
.

Proof.

If
Lf
is an equivalence and
X
is
L
-local, then by the universal property of
{\eta}
, we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{LB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{LA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ Lf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ{\eta_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ{\eta_{A}}}

in which all but the top map are equivalences; thus so is the top map.

Conversely, since
L{\eta_{X}}
is an equivalence, the hypothesis implies that

(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ{\eta_{X}}):(LX\to X)\to(X\to X)

is an equivalence.
In particular, its fiber over
\mathsf{id}_{X}
is inhabited, i.e.
{\eta_{X}}
has a retraction; hence
X
is
L
-local.
∎

Proposition 1.16
.

For any family

f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)\to Q(x)

of
L
-equivalences, the induced map on total spaces

\mathsf{tot}(f):\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Q(x)\Big)

is an
L
-equivalence.

Proof.

Note that we have a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Q(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}Y^{Q(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}Y^{P(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{tot}(f)}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}g.\,{\lambda}x.\,g(x)\circ f(x)}

in which all but the top map are known to be equivalences. Therefore the top map is an equivalence, so the claim follows by
Section

1
.
∎

Corollary 1.17

(Theorem 1.24 of
[
28
]
)
.

For any family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}
, the map

{\lambda}(x,y).\,\eta(x,\eta(y)):\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)\Big)\to L\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}LP(x)\Big)

is a localization.

Lemma 1.18
.

For any two maps
f,g:LX\to Y
into an
L
-local type
Y
, the map

(f\sim g)\to(f\circ{\eta}\sim g\circ{\eta})

given by
H\mapsto H\cdot{\eta}
, is an equivalence.

Proof.

The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f=g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\circ{\eta}=g\circ{\eta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\sim g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\circ{\eta}\sim g\circ{\eta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ap}_{{{\eta}}^{\ast}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{htpy\underline{\ }{}eq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{htpy\underline{\ }{}eq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}H.\,H\cdot{\eta}}

commutes, and all but one of the maps are known equivalences. Therefore it follows that the bottom map is an equivalence, as claimed.
∎

Proposition 1.19
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p\sim g\circ q
. If
A
,
B
, and
X
are
L
-local types, then so is
C
.

Proof.

We have the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(LC\to C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C\to C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cone}(LC)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cone}(C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\eta}}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cone\underline{\ }{}map}}

where the bottom map is the equivalence given by
(\tilde{p},\tilde{q},\tilde{H})\mapsto(\tilde{p}\circ\eta,\tilde{q}\circ\eta,\tilde{H}\cdot\eta)
. This is an equivalence by the assumption that
A
,
B
, and
X
are local, and an application of
Section

1
. The two vertical maps are equivalences by the assumption that
C
is a pullback. Therefore it follows that the top map is an equivalence. By
Section

1
(iii) this suffices to show that
C
is
L
-local.
∎

Corollary 1.20
.

Cartesian products of
L
-local types are
L
-local.

Proof.

Suppose that
X
and
Y
are
L
-local. Then their cartesian product is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{2}}

Since the unit type is
L
-local for any reflective subuniverse by
Section

1
, the claim follows.
∎

Corollary 1.21
.

If
X
is
L
-local, then so is the identity type
x=y
for any
x,y:X
.

Proof.

This follows at once from the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x=y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{const}_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{const}_{x}}

noting that the unit type is
L
-local for any reflective subuniverse by
Section

1
, and
X
is
L
-local by assumption.
∎

Proposition 1.22
.

Given a reflective subuniverse,
if
P(x)
is
L
-local for every
x:X
, then so is
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)
. In particular, the type
Y^{X}
is
L
-local whenever
Y
is
L
-local.

Proof.

Consider the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big(L\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:X}P(y)\Big)\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big(\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:X}P(y)\Big)\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}L\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:X}P(y)\Big)\to P(x)\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:X}P(y)\Big)\to P(x)\Big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\eta}}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{swap}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{swap}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

The vertical maps swap the order of the arguments, and are therefore equivalences. The bottom map is an equivalence by the assumption that each
P(x)
is
L
-local. By
Section

1
(iii) this suffices to show that
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(x)
is
L
-local.
∎

Corollary 1.23
.

For any two
L
-local types
X
and
Y
, the type of equivalences
X\simeq Y
is again
L
-local.

Proof.

The type
X\simeq Y
is equivalent to the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\simeq Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{X}\times X^{Y}\times X^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{X}\times Y^{Y}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{const}_{(\mathsf{id}_{X},\mathsf{id}_{Y})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g,h)\mapsto(hf,fg)}

of
L
-local types, so it is
L
-local.
∎

Remark 1.24
.

Similarly it follows that
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}trunc}_{k}(X)
is
L
-local for any
L
-local type
X
, and
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}trunc}_{k}(f)
is
L
-local for any map
f:X\to Y
between
L
-local types.

Proposition 1.25
.

For any two types
X
and
Y
, the map

{\eta}\times{\eta}:X\times Y\to LX\times LY

is an
L
-localization.
Thus
L
-localization preserves finite products, for any reflective subuniverse
L
.

Proof.

First we note that the product
LX\times LY
is indeed
L
-local by
Section

1
. To see that
{\eta}\times{\eta}
is an
L
-localization, consider an
L
-local type
Z
. Then we have the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(LX\times LY\to Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times Y\to Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(LX\to(LY\to Z))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\to(Y\to Z)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\eta}\times{\eta})}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}f.\,{\lambda}x.\,{\lambda}y.\,f({\eta}(x),{\eta}(y))}

The bottom map is an equivalence by the fact that
Z
and
LY\to Z
are both
L
-local types, and the vertical maps are equivalences too. Therefore
{\eta}\times{\eta}
is an
L
-localization.
∎

Corollary 1.26
.

Given any reflective subuniverse, the modal operator preserves propositions.

Proof.

A type
P
is a proposition if and only if the diagonal
P\to P\times P
is an equivalence.
The result then follows from
Section

1
.
∎

By contrast, localizations, and even modalities, do not generally preserve
n
-types for any
n\geq 0
.
For instance, the “shape” modality of
[
30
]
takes the topological circle, which is a 0-type, to the homotopical circle, which is a 1-type, and the topological 2-sphere, which is also a 0-type, to the homotopical 2-sphere, which is (conjecturally) not an
n
-type for any finite
n
.

2
The reflective subuniverse of separated types

Definition 2.1
.

Consider a subuniverse
P:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
. We say that a type
X
is
P
-separated
if the identity types of
X
are
P
-types. We write
P^{\prime}
for the subuniverse of
P
-separated types.

Example 2.2
.

We define
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}trunc}:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-2}\to\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
by induction on
k:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-2}
, taking

\displaystyle\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}trunc}_{-2}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)

\displaystyle\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}trunc}_{k+1}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:A}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}trunc}_{k}(x\mathbin{{=}}y).

For any type
A
, we say that
A
is
k
-truncated
, or a
k
-type
, if there is a term of type
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}trunc}_{k}(A)
. We say that a map
f:A\to B
is
k
-truncated if its fibers are
k
-truncated.

In other words, the subuniverse of
(n+1)
-truncated types is precisely the subuniverse of
n
-separated types
, i.e. the subuniverse of types whose identity types are
n
-truncated.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
L
be a reflective subuniverse and let
X:\mathcal{U}
be a type.
An
L^{\prime}
-localization
of a type
X
is a localization with respect to the subuniverse of
L
-separated types.

In other words, a type
X
is
L
-separated if its diagonal
\Delta:X\to X\times X
is classified by
\mathcal{U}_{L}
.

Example 2.4
.

Given
n\geq-2
, the subuniverse of
(n+1)
-truncated types is precisely the subuniverse of separated types for the reflective subuniverse of
n
-truncated types.

Lemma 2.5
.

Any
L
-local type is
L
-separated.

Proof.

Immediate by
Section

1
.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Any small subtype of an
L
-separated type is again
L
-separated. In particular, any small proposition is
L
-separated.

The following lemma can be proven directly. However, it also follows once we have shown that the subuniverse of
L
-separated types is a reflective subuniverse, so we will omit the proof.

Lemma 2.7
.

The subuniverse of
L
-separated types is closed under pullbacks, retracts, and dependent products of families of
L
-separated types.

Remark 2.8

(Move to modalities)
.

If
L
is closed under dependent sums, then
L^{\prime}
is also closed under dependent sums,
by the characterization of identity types of dependent sums
[
33
, Theorem 2.7.2]
.
So, given that separated types form a reflective subuniverse, it will follow that if
L
is a modality,
then so is
L^{\prime}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

If
X
is an
L
-separated type and
P:X\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
is a family of
L
-local types, then the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
is
L
-separated.

Proof.

For any
(x,p)
and
(y,q)
in
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
, the type
(x,p)=(y,q)
is equivalent to the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,p)=(y,q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x=y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{transport}^{P}(-,p)}

of
L
-local types, so it is
L
-local.
∎

Corollary 2.10
.

Suppose
l^{\prime}:X\to Y^{\prime}
is an
L^{\prime}
-localization, and let
P:L^{\prime}X\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
be a family of
L
-local types.
Then the precomposition map

{l^{\prime}}^{\ast}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}}P(y^{\prime})\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(l^{\prime}(x)).

is an equivalence.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Section

1
.
∎

Proposition 2.11
.

Any small subtype of the subuniverse
\mathcal{U}_{L}
is
L
-separated.

Proof.

Note that for any two
L
-local types
A
and
B
we have
(A=B)\simeq(A\simeq B)
by univalence and the
fact that being
L
-local is a mere proposition. Therefore the claim follows from
Section

1
.
∎

The only thing that prevents
\mathcal{U}_{L}
from actually being
L
-separated is the fact that
\mathcal{U}_{L}
is not small. In other words, we could say that
\mathcal{U}_{L}
is
essentially
L
-separated
. Using the fact that the image of a small type into
\mathcal{U}_{L}
is essentially small, the condition of being essentially
L
-separated suffices to eliminate from
L^{\prime}X
into
\mathcal{U}_{L}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Consider an
L^{\prime}
-localization
l^{\prime}:X\to Y^{\prime}
. Then any type family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
of
L
-local types has a unique extension along
l^{\prime}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{L}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

Proof.

We prove the first form of the statement.
By
Section

2
, the identity types of
\mathcal{U}_{L}
are
equivalent to small types, i.e.,
\mathcal{U}_{L}
is a locally small type.
By the join construction
[
27
]
, the image of
P

can be taken to be a small type
I
in
\mathcal{U}
, so there is a factorization
of
P
into a surjection
\hat{P}:X\to I
followed by
an embedding
i:I\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

Since the identity types of
I
are equivalent to identity types
of
\mathcal{U}_{L}
, and
I
is small, it follows that the identity types of
I
are actually
L
-local.
This means that
I
is an
L
-separated type, so we can extend
\hat{P}
to
L^{\prime}X

giving us the desired extension of
P
by composing with
i
.

Since
X\to L^{\prime}X
is surjective (
2.14
),
any such extension must factor through the image
I
.
So uniqueness follows from the universal property of
L^{\prime}
-localization.
∎

Before we show that
L^{\prime}
-localizations exist for any type
X
, we characterize them.
To establish our characterization of
L^{\prime}
-localizations, we need the following simple lemma, that allows us to construct unique extensions.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
g:A\to B
and
f:A\to C
be maps for which we have a unique extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{g}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

for every
b:B
.
Then
f
extends uniquely along
g
.

Proof.

By assumption we have

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(c:C)}}}{\sum_{(c:C)}}{\sum_{(c:C)}}{\sum_{(c:C)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}{p:g(a)=b}f(a)=c\Big).

The center of contraction gives us an extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

and its uniqueness follows from the contraction.
∎

Theorem 2.14
.

Consider a map
l^{\prime}:X\to Y^{\prime}
, where
Y^{\prime}
is assumed to be
L
-separated. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The map
l^{\prime}:X\to Y^{\prime}
is an
L^{\prime}
-localization.

(ii)

The map
l^{\prime}:X\to Y^{\prime}
is surjective, and for each
x,y:X
, the map

\mathsf{ap}_{l^{\prime}}:(x=y)\to(l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))

is an
L
-localization.

Proof.

First, suppose that
l^{\prime}:X\to Y^{\prime}
is an
L^{\prime}
-localization. To see that
l^{\prime}
is surjective, we note that
\im(l^{\prime})
is
L
-separated since it is a subtype of
Y^{\prime}
, so the surjective map
q:X\to\im(l^{\prime})
extends uniquely along
l^{\prime}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im(l^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

By the universal property of
L^{\prime}
-localization it follows that
h
is a section of the image inclusion
\im(l^{\prime})\to Y^{\prime}
. In particular, the image inclusion is both surjective and an embedding, so it must be an equivalence. It follows that
l^{\prime}
is surjective.

Next, we need to show that for each
x,y:X
, the map

\mathsf{ap}_{l^{\prime}}:(x=y)\to(l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))

is an
L
-localization. Fix
x:X
.
Since
l^{\prime}
is an
L^{\prime}
-localization, there is a unique extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{L}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\mapsto L(x=y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

The family
P
comes equipped with a point
p_{0}:P(l^{\prime}(x))
that is induced by
\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}):L(x=x)
. Moreover, by the fact that
P
extends
y\mapsto L(x=y)
we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:X)}}}{\sum_{(y:X)}}{\sum_{(y:X)}}{\sum_{(y:X)}}L(x=y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime})}}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime})}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime})}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime})}}P(y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}

Thus we see that our claim follows by
2.5
, once we show that the total space of
P
is contractible.

For the center of contraction of
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}P(y)
we take
(l^{\prime}(x),\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))
.
It remains to construct a contraction

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}}{p:P(y^{\prime})}(l^{\prime}(x),\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=(y^{\prime},p).

Since the fibers of
P
are
L
-local, it follows by
Section

2
that the total space of
P
is
L
-separated. Therefore we obtain by
Section

1
that the type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{p:P(y^{\prime})}(l^{\prime}(x),\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=(y^{\prime},p)

is
L
-local for every
y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}
. Thus
Section

2
reduces the problem to
constructing a term of type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:X}{p:L(x=y)}(l^{\prime}(x),\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=(l^{\prime}(y),p).

Furthermore, for
y:X
we have equivalences

\displaystyle\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}}{\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}{\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}{\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}(l^{\prime}(x),\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=(l^{\prime}(y),p)\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}}{\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}{\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}{\sum_{(p:L(x=y))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}}{\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}{\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}{\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}{\alpha}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x})}\right)\mathclose{}=p\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}}{\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}{\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}{\sum_{(\alpha:l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))}}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y),

where the last type is clearly
L
-local.
So we can apply
Section

1
to reduce the problem to
the problem of constructing a term of type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:X}{p:x=y}(l^{\prime}(x),\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=(l^{\prime}(y),\eta(p)).

This can be done by a simple application of path induction. This completes the proof that (i) implies (ii).

To show that (ii) implies (i), assume that
l^{\prime}
is surjective, and that for every
x,y:X
the map

\mathsf{ap}_{l^{\prime}}:(x=y)\to(l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))

is an
L
-localization. Our goal to show that
l^{\prime}
satisfies the universal property of
L^{\prime}
-localization, so assume
f:X\to Z
is a map into an
L
-separated type
Z
.

By
Section

2
, it is enough to show that
f
restricts to a unique constant map
on the fibers of
l^{\prime}
. This means that we must show that

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(z:Z)}}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}(l^{\prime}(x)=y^{\prime})\longrightarrow(f(x)=z)

is contractible for every
y^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}
.
Since this is a mere proposition, and
l^{\prime}
is surjective, we can assume that

y^{\prime}=l^{\prime}(y)
. In other words, it suffices to show that

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(z:Z)}}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}(l^{\prime}(x)=l^{\prime}(y))\longrightarrow(f(x)=z)

is contractible for every
y:X
.

Since
Z
is assumed to be
L
-separated and
\mathsf{ap}_{l^{\prime}}
is assumed to be an
L
-localization,
this type is equivalent to

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(z:Z)}}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\sum_{(z:Z)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}(x=y)\longrightarrow(f(x)=z)

and it is easy to see that this is a contractible type by applying the contractibility of
the total space of the path fibration twice.
∎

Our final goal for this section is to show that
L^{\prime}
is a reflective subuniverse, i.e. that there is an
L^{\prime}
-localization for every type
X:\mathcal{U}
.
We will use a ‘local version’ of the type theoretic Yoneda Lemma.

Lemma 2.15
.

For each
y:X
and each
P:X\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
, the map

\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}locrefl}:\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(y=z)\to P(z)\Big)\longrightarrow P(y)

given by
f\mapsto f(y,\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{y}))
is an equivalence.

Proof.

By the universal property of
L(y=z)
and identity elimination,
the map in the statement can be factored as follows:

\displaystyle\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(y=z)\to P(z)\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}(y=z)\to P(z)\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq P(y).\qed

Theorem 2.16
.

For any reflective subuniverse
L
, the subuniverse of
L
-separated types is again reflective. We will write

{\eta}^{\prime}:X\to L^{\prime}X

for the
L^{\prime}
-localization of a type
X
.

Proof.

Fix a type
X:\mathcal{U}
. Let
\mathcal{Y}_{L}:X\to(X\to\mathcal{U})
be given by

\mathcal{Y}_{L}(x,y)\vcentcolon\equiv L(x=y).

We would like to define
L^{\prime}X
to be
\im(\mathcal{Y}_{L})
, but this is a subtype of

X\to\mathcal{U}
, so it is not small (i.e., it does not live in
\mathcal{U}
).
However, since
\mathcal{U}
is locally small, so is
X\to\mathcal{U}
.
Thus the join construction
[
27
]
implies that the image
is equivalent to a small type which we denote
L^{\prime}X
.
This comes equipped with a surjective map

\eta^{\prime}:X\longrightarrow L^{\prime}X,

which we take to be the unit of the reflective subuniverse.

To show that
\eta^{\prime}
is a localization, we apply
2.14
.
First we show that
L^{\prime}X
is
L
-separated. Since
\eta^{\prime}
is surjective
and being
L
-local is a proposition, it is enough to show that

\eta^{\prime}(x)=\eta^{\prime}(y)
is
L
-local for
x
and
y
in
X
.
Since
L^{\prime}X
embeds in
X\to\mathcal{U}
, we have an equivalence between

\eta^{\prime}(x)=\eta^{\prime}(y)
and
(\lambda z.L(x=z))=(\lambda z.L(y=z))
.
The latter is equivalent to
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(x=z)=L(y=z)
, which
is
L
-local by
Section

2
.

It remains to show that the canonical map
L(x=y)\to(\eta^{\prime}(x)=\eta^{\prime}(y))
is an equivalence.
By the above argument, combined with univalence,
the problem reduces to showing that the canonical map

L(x=y)\longrightarrow\left({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(x=z)\simeq L(y=z)\right)

is an equivalence.
Using symmetry of equivalences, it suffices to show that the map

L(x=y)\longrightarrow\left({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(y=z)\simeq L(x=z)\right)

is an equivalence.
Moreover, since the forgetful map from equivalences to maps is an embedding,
it is enough to show that the composite map

\alpha_{x,y}:L(x=y)\longrightarrow\left({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(y=z)\to L(x=z)\right)

is an equivalence.
Indeed, if
i
is an embedding and
i\circ g
is an equivalence,
then
i
is surjective. Therefore
i
is an equivalence and hence so is
g
.

By the local Yoneda Lemma
2
, with
P(z)\vcentcolon\equiv L(x=z)
, there is an equivalence

\beta_{x,y}:L(x=y)\longrightarrow\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(y=z)\to L(x=z)\Big)

which sends
p:L(x=y)
to the unique function
f
such that

f(x,\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=p
.
So it suffices to show that
\alpha_{x,y}=\beta_{x,y}
.
By the universal property of
L(x=y)
, it is enough to show that

\alpha_{x,y}\circ\eta=\beta_{x,y}\circ\eta
as maps

(x=y)\to\big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:X}L(y=z)\to L(x=z)\big)
.
Letting
x
and
y
vary and using path induction, we reduce the
problem to showing that
\alpha_{x,x}(\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=\beta_{x,x}(\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))
.

Since
\alpha_{x,y}
is defined by path induction, it is easy to see
that
\alpha_{x,x}(\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))
is equal to
{\lambda}z.\,\mathsf{id}_{L(x=z)}
.
On the other hand,
\beta_{x,x}(\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))
is the unique function
f
such that

f(x,\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x})
.
Therefore, this
f
must also equal
{\lambda}z.\,\mathsf{id}_{L(x=z)}
,
showing that
\alpha_{x,x}(\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))=\beta_{x,x}(\eta(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))
.
∎

Theorem 2.17
.

For each
k\geq-2
, the subuniverse of
k
-truncated types is reflective.

Proof.

The subuniverse of contractible types is obviously reflective, it’s localization is just the constant function
{\lambda}X.\,\mathbf{1}
mapping every type to the unit type. Since the subuniverse of
(k+1)
-truncated types is precisely the subuniverse of
k
-separated types, the claim follows inductively by
2.16
.
∎

3
L
-local maps

Definition 3.1
.

A map
f:A\to B
is said to be
L
-local
if its fibers are
L
-local.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
B
be a type family over
A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

For each
x:A
the type
B(x)
is
L
-local.

(ii)

The projection map

\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)\to A

is an
L
-local map.

Proof.

Immediate from the equivalences
B(x)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)
.
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a map. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The map
f
is
L^{\prime}
-local.

(ii)

For each
x,y:A
, the map

\mathsf{ap}_{f}:(x=y)\to(f(x)=f(y))

is
L
-local.

(iii)

The diagonal
\delta_{f}:A\to A\times_{B}A
of
f
is
L
-local.

Proof.

First we show that for any
s,t:\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)
there is an equivalence

(s=t)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{ap}_{f}}(\mathsf{pr}_{2}(s)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}\mathsf{pr}_{2}(t)^{-1})

We do this by
\Sigma
-induction on
s
and
t
, and then we calculate using basic manipulations of identifications that

\displaystyle({\mathopen{}(x,p)\mathclose{}}={\mathopen{}(y,q)\mathclose{}})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:x=y)}}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}\mathsf{tr}_{f(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})=b}(r,p)=q

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:x=y)}}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({r}\right)^{-1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}p=q

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:x=y)}}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}{\sum_{(r:x=y)}}\mathsf{ap}_{f}\left({r}\right)=p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{ap}_{f}}(p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q^{-1}).

By these equivalences, it follows that if
\mathsf{ap}_{f}
is
L
-local, then for each
s,t:\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)
the identity type
s=t
is an
L
-local type.

For the converse, note that we have equivalences

\displaystyle\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{ap}_{f}}(p)

\displaystyle\simeq((x,p)=(y,\mathsf{refl}_{f(y)})).

Therefore it follows that if
f
is
L^{\prime}
-local, then the identity type
(x,p)=(y,\mathsf{refl}_{f(y)})
in
\mathsf{fib}_{f}(f(y))
is
L
-local for any
p:f(x)=f(y)
, and therefore
\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{ap}_{f}}(p)
is
L
-local.
∎

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}B(x)\to C(x)
be a fiberwise transformation. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

For each
x:A
the map
f(x)
is
L
-local.

(ii)

The induced map

\mathsf{tot}(f):\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}C(x)\Big)

is
L
-local.

Proof.

This follows directly from
Section

2
.
∎

4
Quasi-left-exactness of
L^{\prime}
-localization

We now explain how
L
and
L^{\prime}
together behave similarly to a
lex modality
,
i.e., a modality that preserves pullbacks.
Theorem 3.1 of
[
28
]
gives 13 equivalent characterizations of a lex modality,
and it turns out that these hold for any reflective subuniverse
if the modal operator is replaced by
L
and
L^{\prime}
in the appropriate way.
The propositions in this section show this for parts (ix), (x), (xii) and (xi)
of Theorem 3.1, respectively.
The proofs use the dependent elimination of
L^{\prime}
in a crucial way,
but do not use the specific construction of
L^{\prime}
-localization, just the existence.

Before proving the next result,
we need a lemma, which follows directly from the dependent elimination of
L^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
P:L^{\prime}X\to\mathcal{U}
be a type family over
L^{\prime}X
.
Then the map

f:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(\eta^{\prime}(x))\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}P(y)\Big)

given by
(x,p)\mapsto(\eta^{\prime}(x),p)
is an
L
-equivalence.

Proof.

For any
L
-local type
Z
we have the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big(\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}{\sum_{(y:L^{\prime}X)}}P(y)\Big)\to Z\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(\eta^{\prime}x)\right)\to Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:L^{\prime}X}(P(y)\to Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}(P(\eta^{\prime}x)\to Z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}f.\,{\lambda}(x,p).\,f(\eta^{\prime}(x),p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}pair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}f.\,{\lambda}x.\,{\lambda}y.\,f(\eta^{\prime}(x),y)}

The bottom map is an equivalence by
Section

2
, using that
Z
and
P(y)\to Z
are
L
-local.
The two vertical maps are also equivalences, so it follows that the top map is an equivalence. The claim now follows from
Section

1
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Consider a commuting cube of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}A\times_{L^{\prime}X}L^{\prime}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}

Then the map
A\times_{X}B\to L^{\prime}A\times_{L^{\prime}X}L^{\prime}B
is an
L
-equivalence.

Proof.

Consider the following commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}f(x)=g(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x^{\prime}:L^{\prime}A)}}}{\sum_{(x^{\prime}:L^{\prime}A)}}{\sum_{(x^{\prime}:L^{\prime}A)}}{\sum_{(x^{\prime}:L^{\prime}A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y^{\prime}:L^{\prime}B)}}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:L^{\prime}B)}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:L^{\prime}B)}}{\sum_{(y^{\prime}:L^{\prime}B)}}L^{\prime}f(x^{\prime})=L^{\prime}g(y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}\eta^{\prime}(f(x))=\eta^{\prime}(g(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}L^{\prime}f(\eta^{\prime}(x))=L^{\prime}g(\eta^{\prime}(y))}

In this square, the downwards morphism on the left is the induced map on total spaces of the map
\mathsf{ap}_{\eta^{\prime}}:(f(x)=g(y))\to(\eta^{\prime}(f(x))=\eta^{\prime}(g(y)))
, which is an
L
-equivalence by
2.14
and
Section

1
. The bottom map is an equivalence, obtained from the naturality squares
\eta^{\prime}\circ f\sim L^{\prime}f\circ\eta^{\prime}
and
\eta^{\prime}\circ g\sim L^{\prime}g\circ\eta^{\prime}
. In particular, it is an
L
-equivalence. The upwards map on the right is an
L
-equivalence by
Section

4
. Therefore, the asserted map is a composite of
L
-equivalences, so it is also an
L
-equivalence.
∎

As a consequence we get a result about the preservation of certain fiber sequences.

Corollary 4.3
.

Given a fiber sequence
F\hookrightarrow E\twoheadrightarrow B
, there is a map of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}p}

in which the left vertical map is an
L
-equivalence.∎

5
Connected maps

Definition 5.1
.

A map
f:A\to B
is said to be
L
-connected
if
L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
is contractible for every
b:B
. In particular, a type
A
is
L
-connected if and only if
LA
is contractible.

In the following proposition we characterize
L^{\prime}
-connected types. We will establish a similar claim for maps in
Section

5

Proposition 5.2
.

A type
A
is
L^{\prime}
-connected if and only if
A
is merely inhabited (i.e.
\|A\|
holds), and the identity types of
A
are
L
-connected.

Proof.

A
is
L^{\prime}
-connected if and only if
A\to\mathbf{1}
is an
L^{\prime}
-localization. By
2.14
this holds if and only if
A\to\mathbf{1}
is surjective, and the maps
(x=y)\to\mathsf{\Omega}(\mathbf{1})
are
L
-localizations — in other words: if and only if
A
is merely inhabited and the identity types of
A
are
L
-connected.
∎

In the following proposition we provide two equivalent conditions to a map being
L
-connected.

Proposition 5.3
.

Consider a map
f:A\to B
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is
L
-connected.

(ii)

For every family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
of
L
-local types, the map

{\lambda}s.\,s\circ f:\Bigl({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P(b)\Bigr)\to\Bigl({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P(f(a))\Bigr).

is an equivalence.

(iii)

For every family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
of
L
-local types, the map

{\lambda}s.\,s\circ f:\Bigl({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P(b)\Bigr)\to\Bigl({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P(f(a))\Bigr).

has a section.

Proof.

First suppose
f
is
L
-connected and let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
. Then the map

{\lambda}p.\,\mathsf{const}_{p}:P(b)\to(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)\to P(b))

is an equivalence for every
b:B
, since
L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
is assumed to be contractible.
Therefore we obtain a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}P(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}P(f(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\big(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)\to P(b)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}{a:A}(f(a)=b)\to P(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{evpair}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev\underline{\ }{}refl}}

in which three out of four maps are known equivalences. The remaining map must therefore also be an equivalence.

Thus,
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
, and clearly
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
.
To show
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
, let

P(b)\vcentcolon\equiv L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
.
Then
(iii)
yields a map
c:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
with

c(f(a))={\eta}{{\mathopen{}(a,\mathsf{refl}_{f(a)})\mathclose{}}}
. To show that each
L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
is contractible, we will show that
c(b)=w
for any
b:B
and
w:L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
.
In other words, we must show that the identity function
L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))\to L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
is equal to the constant function at
c(b)
.
By the universal property of
L(\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b))
, it suffices to show that they become equal when precomposed with
{\eta_{\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)}}
, i.e. we may assume that
w={\eta}{\mathopen{}(a,p)\mathclose{}}
for some
a:A
and
p:f(a)=b
.
But now path induction on
p
reduces our goal to the given
c(f(a))={\eta}{{\mathopen{}(a,\mathsf{refl}_{f(a)})\mathclose{}}}
.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

A type
A
is
L
-connected if and only if the “constant functions” map

B\to(A\to B)

is an equivalence for every modal type
B
.∎

Dually, we will prove in
Section

4
that when
L
is a modality, if this holds for all
L
-connected
A
then
B
is
L
-local.

Corollary 5.5
.

If
f:A\to B
is an
L
-connected map into an
L
-local type
B
, then
f
is an
L
-localization. (The converse only holds when
L
is a modality.)

Proposition 5.6
.

Consider a map
f:A\to B
.

(i)

If
f
is an
L^{\prime}
-equivalence, then
f
is
L
-connected.

(ii)

If
f
is
L
-connected, then
f
is an
L
-equivalence.

Proof.

The first statement is a direct consequence of
Section

4
, since we get for each
b:B
a map of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{L^{\prime}f}(\eta^{\prime}b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}f}

in which the induced map
\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)\to\mathsf{fib}_{L^{\prime}f}(\eta^{\prime}b)
is an
L
-equivalence. Since
L^{\prime}f
is an equivalence, it follows that
\mathsf{fib}_{L^{\prime}f}(\eta^{\prime}b)
is contractible, so we conclude that
\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)
is
L
-connected. It follows that
f
is
L
-connected.

For the second statement, we observe that if
f
is
L
-connected, then by
Section

5
it induces an equivalence

{f}^{\ast}:(B\to X)\to(A\to X)

for every
L
-local type
X
. Therefore we conclude by
Section

1
that
f
is an
L
-equivalence.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Consider a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(i)

If
h
is
L
-connected, then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f
is
L
-connected.

(b)

g
is
L
-connected.

(ii)

If
f
is
L^{\prime}
-connected, then the following are equivalent:

(a)

g
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.

(b)

h
is
L
-connected.

In particular,
g
is
(n+1)
-connected if and only if
h
is
n
-connected, assuming that
f
is
(n+1)
-connected.

Proof.

For the first statement suppose that
h
is
L
-connected.
For any
z:C
we have

\displaystyle L(\mathsf{fib}_{g\circ h}(z))

\displaystyle\simeq L(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}\mathsf{fib}_{h}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(p)))

\displaystyle\simeq L(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}L(\mathsf{fib}_{h}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(p))))

\displaystyle\simeq L(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}\mathbf{1})

\displaystyle\simeq L(\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z)).

using the fact that
h
is
L
-connected.
Thus, one is contractible if and only if the other is. We conclude that
f
is
L
-connected if and only if
g
is.

For the second statement, suppose that
f
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.
If
g
is
L^{\prime}
-connected, then both
g\circ h
and
g
are
L^{\prime}
-equivalences,
and thus
h
is an
L^{\prime}
-equivalence. Then
Section

5

implies that
h
is
L
-connected.

For the converse, notice that taking fibers over each
x:X
reduces the problem
to showing that given an
L
-connected map
h:A\to B

such that
A
is
L^{\prime}
-connected, it follows that
B
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.

Since
L^{\prime}A
is contractible, it follows that
L^{\prime}B
is contractible if and only if
L^{\prime}h:L^{\prime}A\to L^{\prime}B
is an equivalence.
Therefore it suffices to show that the fibers of
L^{\prime}h
are contractible. Moreover, since
\eta^{\prime}:B\to L^{\prime}B
is a surjective map by
2.14
, it is enough to show that
\mathsf{fib}_{L^{\prime}h}(\eta^{\prime}(b))
is contractible for every
b:B
.
First we observe that,
\mathsf{fib}_{L^{\prime}h}(\eta^{\prime}(b))\simeq\mathsf{\Omega}(L^{\prime}B,\eta^{\prime}(b))
since
L^{\prime}A
is contractible.
Now we observe using
Section

4
that we have for every
b:B
a morphism of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{h}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(L^{\prime}B,\eta^{\prime}(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}h}

in which the map on the left is an
L
-equivalence. However, the type
\mathsf{\Omega}(L^{\prime}B,\eta^{\prime}(b))
is
L
-local, so it follows that the map

\mathsf{fib}_{h}(b)\to\mathsf{\Omega}(L^{\prime}B,\eta(b))

is an
L
-localization. Now it follows by our assumption that
h
is
L
-connected that
\mathsf{\Omega}(L^{\prime}B,\eta(b))
is contractible. We conclude that
\mathsf{fib}_{L^{\prime}h}(\eta^{\prime}(b))
is contractible, and therefore that
L^{\prime}B
is contractible.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

In general it is not true that if
g
and
g\circ h
are
{\ocircle}
-connected then
h
is; this is one of the equivalent characterizations of lex modalities (Theorem 3.1 of
[
28
]
).

The above proposition almost gives us a 3-for-2 property that combines
L
and
L^{\prime}
.
However the map
\mathbf{0}\to\mathbf{1}
is
(-2)
-connected, and
\mathbf{1}
is
(-1)
-connected,
whereas
\mathbf{0}
is not
(-1)
-connected. So the remaining implication of the 3-for-2 property
does not hold. One can show the weaker result that the composite of an
L
-connected map followed by
an
L^{\prime}
-connected map is
L
-connected.

Remark 5.9
.

2.14
allows us to give a concrete description of
the extension defined in
Section

2
.
Using an argument similar to the one used in the proof of
Section

4
,
one can show that given an
L^{\prime}
-localization
\eta^{\prime}:X\to L^{\prime}X

and a map
f:Y\to X
with
L
-local fibers,
f
is the pullback of
the fiberwise
L
-localization of
\eta^{\prime}\circ f
.

Definition 5.10
.

A commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is said to be
L
-cartesian
if its gap map is
L
-connected.

Proposition 5.11
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

(i)

Suppose that
g
is surjective, and that the square is
L
-cartesian. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

The map
h:A\to B
is
L
-connected.

(b)

The map
i:X\to Y
is
L
-connected.

(ii)

Suppose that
i:X\to Y
is
L^{\prime}
-connected. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

The map
h:A\to B
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.

(b)

The square is
L
-cartesian.

Proof.

For the first statement we observe that by
Section

5

h
is an
L
-connected if and only if
g^{\ast}i
is an
L
-connected. Since
g
is assumed to be surjective, it follows that
g^{\ast}i
is
L
-connected if and only if
i
is
L
-connected.

For the second statement we observe that, since
i:X\to Y
is assumed to be
L^{\prime}
-connected, the map
g^{\ast}i:X\times_{Y}B\to B
. Now it follows by
Section

5
that the gap map is
L
-connected if and only if
h
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

A map
f:A\to B
is a
L^{\prime}
-connected if and only if
f
is surjective and
\delta_{f}:A\to A\times_{B}A
is a
L
-connected.

Proof.

Since every proposition is
L^{\prime}
-local, it follows that
\|X\|=\|L^{\prime}X\|
for any type
X
. In particular, if
X
is
L^{\prime}
-connected, then
\|X\|=\mathbf{1}
. From this observation it follows that if
f
is
L^{\prime}
-connected, then
f
is surjective. Furthermore, since the identity function
\mathsf{id}_{A}:A\to A
is obviously
L^{\prime}
-connected, it follows that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is
L
-cartesian. In other words:
\delta_{f}:A\to A\times_{B}A
is
L
-connected.

Now suppose that
f
is surjective and that
\delta_{f}
is
L
-connected. Then the fibers of
\delta_{f}
are
L
-connected. Since we have equivalences

((x,p)=(y,q))\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\delta_{f}}(x,y,p\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}q^{-1})

for any
(x,p),(y,q):\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)
it follows by
Section

5
that
\mathsf{fib}_{f}(b)
is
L^{\prime}
-connected for every
b:B
. In other words:
f
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.
∎

A commuting square is said to be a
quasi-pullback
if its gap map is surjective. In other words, quasi-pullback squares are the same as
(-1)
-cartesian squares.

Corollary 5.13
.

A map
f:A\to B
is
n
-connected if and only if the gap map of the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\mathbb{S}^{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\mathbb{S}^{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\mathbb{S}^{k}}}

is a quasi-pullback square, for each
-1\leq k\leq n
. A map is therefore
\infty
-connected if this square is a quasi-pullback for all
k

Corollary 5.14
.

A pointed connected type
A
is
L^{\prime}
-connected if and only if the map
\mathbf{1}\to A
is
L
-connected.

Proof.

If
A
is
L^{\prime}
-connected, then its loop space is
L
-connected. It follows that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

is
L
-cartesian. Since the map
\mathbf{1}\to\mathbf{1}
is obviously
L
-connected and the map
\mathbf{1}\to A
is surjective by the assumption that
A
is connected, it follows that the map
\mathbf{1}\to A
is
L
-connected.

For the converse, suppose that the map
\mathbf{1}\to A
is
L
-connected. Then the identity types of
A
are
L
-connected, since all identity types of
A
are merely equal to
\mathsf{\Omega}(A)
. Therefore it follows by
Section

5
that
A
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.
∎

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
be type families, and let

g:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}P(x)\to Q(f(x))

be a fiberwise transformation over

.

(i)

Suppose that each

is

(a)

If
f
is
L
-connected, then
\mathsf{tot}_{f}(g)
is

-connected.

(b)

If each
Q(x)
is merely inhabited and
\mathsf{tot}_{f}(g)
is

f
is

-connected.

(ii)

Suppose that
f
is
L^{\prime}
-connected. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

Each
g_{x}:P(x)\to Q(f(x))
is
L
-connected.

(b)

The map
\mathsf{tot}_{f}(g)
is
L^{\prime}
-connected.

Proposition 5.16
.

.

6

Modalities

In this section we will introduce the following four notions of modality
and prove that they are all equivalent:

(i)

Higher modalities

(ii)

Uniquely eliminating modalities

(iii)

\Sigma
-closed reflective subuniverses

(iv)

Stable orthogonal factorization systems

After their equivalence has been established, we will call all of them simply
modalities
.

The first three definitions have the following data in common: by a
modal operator
we mean a function
{\ocircle}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
, and by a
modal unit
we mean a family of functions
{\eta}^{\ocircle}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}*{A:\mathcal{U}}A\to{\ocircle}A
.
Given these data, we say a type
X

is modal
if
{\eta_{X}}:X\to{\ocircle}X
is an equivalence, and we write
\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(X:\mathcal{U})}}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}modal}(X)
for the
subuniverse of modal types
.

Definition 6.1
.

A
higher modality
consists of a modal operator and modal unit together with

(i)

for every
A:\mathcal{U}
and every dependent type
P:{\ocircle}A\to\mathcal{U}
, a
function

\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}A}:\big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}{\ocircle}(P(\eta(a)))\big)\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:{\ocircle}A}{\ocircle}(P(z)).

(ii)

An identification

\mathsf{comp}_{{\ocircle}A}(f,x):\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}A}(f)(\eta(x))\mathbin{{=}}f(x)

for each
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A}{\ocircle}(P(\eta(x)))
.

(iii)

For any
x,y:{\ocircle}A
the modal unit
{\eta_{(x\mathbin{{=}}y)}}:x\mathbin{{=}}y\to{\ocircle}(x\mathbin{{=}}y)
is an equivalence.

Definition 6.2
.

A
uniquely eliminating modality
consists of
a modal operator and modal unit such that the function

{\lambda}f.\,f\circ{\eta_{A}}:({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}A}{\ocircle}(P(x)))\to({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}{\ocircle}(P({\eta_{A}}(a))))

is an equivalence for any
A
and any
P:{\ocircle}A\to\mathcal{U}
.

Definition 6.3
.

A reflective subuniverse
L
is said to be
\Sigma
-closed
if
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
is
L
-local for every family
P:X\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
of
L
-local types over an
L
-local type
X
.

Note that unlike
Sections

6
and
6
, in
Section

6
the notion of “modal type” is part of the data.
However, we will show in
Section

1
that
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}modal}(A)
if and only if
{\eta_{A}}
is an equivalence.

Definition 6.4
.

An
orthogonal factorization system
consists of
predicates
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}*{A,B:\mathcal{U}}(A\to B)\to\mathsf{Prop}
such that

(i)

\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
are closed under composition and contain all identities (i.e. they are subcategories of the category of types that contain all the objects), and

(ii)

the type
\mathsf{fact}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)
of factorizations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{R}}}

of
f
, with
f_{\mathcal{L}}
in
\mathcal{L}
and
f_{\mathcal{R}}
in
\mathcal{R}
, is contractible.

More precisely, the type
\mathsf{fact}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)
is defined to
be the type of
tuples

(\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f),(f_{\mathcal{L}},p),(f_{\mathcal{R}},q),h)

consisting of a type
\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)
, a function
f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)
with

p:\mathcal{L}(f_{\mathcal{L}})
, a function
f_{\mathcal{R}}:\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)\to B
with
q:\mathcal{R}(f_{\mathcal{R}})
, and an identification
h:f\mathbin{{=}}f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}}
. The type
\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)
is called
the
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
-image of
f
.

A type
X
is said to be
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
-modal
if
the map
!:X\to\mathbf{1}
is in
\mathcal{R}
(and hence
!_{\mathcal{L}}

is an equivalence).

An orthogonal factorization system is said to be
stable
if the class

\mathcal{L}
is stable under pullbacks (By

Section

4
,
\mathcal{R}
is always stable under pullbacks).

Remark 6.5
.

By univalence, the fact that
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
contain all identities implies that they each contain all equivalences.
Conversely, if
f\in\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{R}
, then
(\mathsf{id},f)
and
(f,\mathsf{id})
are both
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
-factorizations of
f
, and hence equal; which implies that
f
is an equivalence.
Thus,
\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{R}
consists exactly of the equivalences.

We now consider a few examples.
Since we will eventually prove all the definitions to be equivalent, we can use any one of them to describe any particular example.

Example 6.6
.

The prime example is the
n
-truncation modality

\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\|_{n}
as studied in
[
33
, Chapter 7]
.
This can be given as a higher modality, using its induction principle and the fact that
\|A\|_{n}
is an
n
-type and the identity types of an
n
-type are again
n
-types (indeed,
(n-1)
-types).
The corresponding stable orthogonal factorization system, consisting of
n
-connected and
n
-truncated maps, is also constructed in
[
33
, Chapter 7]
; our construction in
6.20
will be a generalization of this.

Example 6.7
.

Let
Q
be a mere proposition.
The
open modality
determined by
Q
is defined by
\mathsf{Op}_{Q}A=(Q\to A)
, with unit
{\eta_{A}}(x)={\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,x:A\to(Q\to A)
.
To show that this is a higher modality, suppose we have
P:(Q\to A)\to\mathcal{U}
and
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}Q\to P({\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,a)
.
Then for any
z:Q\to A
and
q:Q
we have
f(z(q),q):P({\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,z(q))
.
And since
Q
is a mere proposition, we have
z(q)=z(q^{\prime})
for any
q^{\prime}:Q
, hence
e(z,q):({\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,z(q))=z
by function extensionality.
This gives

{\lambda}z.\,{\lambda}q.\,{e(z,q)}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({(f(z(q),q))}\right)\mathclose{}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:Q\to A}Q\to P(z)

For the computation rule, we have

\displaystyle({\lambda}z.\,{\lambda}q.\,{e(z,q)}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({(f(z(q),q))}\right)\mathclose{})({\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,a)

\displaystyle={\lambda}q.\,{e({\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,a,q)}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({(f(a,q))}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle={\lambda}q.\,f(a,q)=f(a)

by function extensionality, since
e({\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,a,q)=\mathsf{refl}
.
Finally, if
x,y:Q\to A
, then
(x=y)\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{q:Q}x(q)=y(q)
, and the map

\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{q:Q}x(q)=y(q)\Big)\to\Big(Q\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{q:Q}x(q)=y(q)\Big)

is (by currying) essentially precomposition with a product projection
Q\times Q\to Q
, and that is an equivalence since
Q
is a mere proposition.

Example 6.8
.

Again, let
Q
be a mere proposition.
The
closed modality
determined by
Q
is defined by
\mathsf{Cl}_{Q}A=Q\ast A
, the
join
of
Q
and
A
(the pushout of
Q
and
A
under
Q\times A
).
We show that this is a
\Sigma
-closed reflective subuniverse.
Define a type
B
to be modal if
Q\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(B)
, and note that it is indeed the case that
Q\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(Q\ast A)
, for any type
A
.
By the universal property of pushouts, a map
Q\ast A\to B
consists of a map
f:A\to B
and a map
g:Q\to B
and for any
a:A
and
q:Q
an identification
p:f(a)=g(q)
.
But if
Q\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(B)
, then
g
and
p
