are uniquely determined, so this is just a map
A\to B
.
Thus
(\mathsf{Cl}_{Q}A\to B)\to(A\to B)
is an equivalence, so we have a reflective subuniverse.
It is
\Sigma
-closed since the dependent sum of a contractible family of types over a contractible base is contractible.

Example 6.9
.

The
double negation modality
is defined by
A\mapsto\neg\neg A
, i.e.
(A\to\mathbf{0})\to\mathbf{0}
, with
{\eta}(a)={\lambda}g.\,g(a)
.
We show that this is a uniquely eliminating modality.
Since the map
{\lambda}f.\,f\circ{\eta_{A}}
that must be an equivalence has mere propositions as domain and codomain, it suffices to give a map in the other direction.
Thus, let
P:\neg\neg A\to\mathcal{U}
and
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}\neg\neg P({\lambda}g.\,g(a))
; given
z:\neg\neg A
we must derive a contradiction from
g:\neg P(z)
.
Since we are proving a contradiction, we can strip the double negation from
z
and assume given an
a:A
.
And since
\neg\neg A
is a mere proposition, we have
z={\lambda}g.\,g(a)
, so that we can transport
f(a)
to get an element of
\neg\neg P(z)
, contradicting
g
.

Example 6.10
.

The
trivial modality
is the identity function on
\mathcal{U}
.
It coincides with
\mathsf{Op}_{\top}
and with
\mathsf{Cl}_{\bot}
.

Dually, the
zero modality
sends all types to
\mathbf{1}
.
It is equivalently the
(-2)
-truncation, and coincides with
\mathsf{Op}_{\bot}
and with
\mathsf{Cl}_{\top}
.

Summary.

In each of
Sections

6
,
6
,
6
and
6

we have defined what it means for a type to be modal. In each case, being
modal is a family of mere propositions indexed by the universe, i.e. a subuniverse.
We will show in
6.12
,
6.15
,
1.8
and
6.31
that each kind of structure is completely determined by this subuniverse.
(
1.8
is more general, not requiring
\Sigma
-closedness.)

It follows that the type of all modalities of each
kind is a subset of the set
\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
of all subuniverses, and in particular is a set.
This makes it easier to establish
the equivalences of the different kinds of modalities.
It suffices
to show that any modality of one kind determines a modality of the next kind
with the same modal types, which we will do as follows:
higher modality

stable factorization system

uniquely eliminating modality

\Sigma
-closed reflective subuniverse

6.13
6.32
6.16
6.20

Before
6.20
we take the opportunity to develop a bit more theory of reflective subuniverses, including closure under identity types (
Section

1
) and dependent products
(
Section

1
), along with several equivalent characterizations of
\Sigma
-closedness (
6.19
).

Of these equivalences, the most surprising is that a stable factorization system is uniquely determined by its underlying reflective subuniverse of types.
This is false for stable factorization systems on arbitrary categories; the reason it holds here is that we are talking
in type theory
about factorization systems
on the category of types
.
An analogous fact is true in classical set-based mathematics for stable factorization systems on the category of sets (although in that case there are much fewer interesting examples).
We
[
28
]
we also observe that when type theory is interpreted in a higher category, the data of a reflective subuniverse or modality has to be interpreted “fiberwise”, giving a richer structure than a single reflective subcategory.

1

Higher modalities

We start by showing that a higher modality is determined by its modal types, and gives rise to a uniquely eliminating modality.

Lemma 6.11
.

If
{\ocircle}
is a higher modality, then any type of the form
{\ocircle}X
is modal.

Proof.

We want to show that the modal unit
{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}:{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X

is an equivalence. By the induction principle and the computation rule for
higher modalities, we find a function
f:{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}X
with
the property that
f\circ{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}\sim\mathsf{id}_{{\ocircle}X}
. We wish to
show that we also have
{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}\circ f\sim\mathsf{id}
. Since identity
types of types of the form
{\ocircle}Y
are declared to be modal, it is
equivalent to find a term of type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}({\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}(f(x))=x).

Now we are in the position to use the induction principle of higher modalities
again, so it suffices to show that
{\eta}(f({\eta}(x)))={\eta}(x)

for any
x:{\ocircle}X
. This follows from the fact that
f\circ{\eta}=\mathsf{id}
.
∎

Theorem 6.12
.

The data of two higher modalities
{\ocircle}
and
{\ocircle}^{\prime}

are identical if and only if they have the same modal types.

Proof.

Another way of stating this, is that the function from the type of
all

modalities on
\mathcal{U}
to the type
\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
of predicates on
\mathcal{U}
, given
by mapping a modality to the predicate
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}modal}
, is an embedding. Thus, we
need to show that for any predicate
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
, we can find at
most one modality for which
\mathcal{M}
is the class of modal types. This
follows, once we demonstrate that,

for any
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
closed under identity types,
and for any type
X
, the type of tuples
(Y,p,\pi,I,C)
—
consisting of a type
Y
with
p
witnessing that
Y

satisfies
\mathcal{M}
, a function
\pi:X\to Y
, a function

I_{P}:({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(\pi(x)))\to({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:Y}P(y))

for every
P:Y\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}
,
which is a right inverse of precomposing with
\pi
, as is witnessed by the
term
C
— is a mere proposition.

We prove this statement in two parts. First, we show that the
type of pairs
(I,C)
, with
I
and
C
of the indicated types, is a mere
proposition for any
(Y,p,\pi)
. After that, we show that the type of triples

(Y,p,\pi)
is also a mere proposition. These two facts combined prove the
statement.

Consider a type
Y
satisfying
\mathcal{M}
, and a function
\pi:X\to Y
, and
let
(I,C)
and
(I^{\prime},C^{\prime})
be two terms witnessing that
Y
satisfies an induction
principle with a computation rule. We want to show that
(I,C)=(I^{\prime},C^{\prime})
, and of
course it suffices to show that
(I(s),C(s))=(I^{\prime}(s),C(s))
for any

P:Y\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
s:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P(\pi(x))
.

To show that
I(s,y)=I^{\prime}(s,y)
for any
y:Y
, we use
the induction principle
(I,C)
. So it suffices to show that

I(s,\pi(x))=I^{\prime}(s,\pi(x))
. Both of these terms are equal to
s(x)
. Thus,
we obtain a proof
J(s,y)
that
I(s,y)=I^{\prime}(s,y)
, with the property that

J(s,\pi(x))=C(s,x)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}{C^{\prime}(s,x)}^{-1}
.
Now we need to show that
{J(s)}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C(s)}\right)\mathclose{}=C^{\prime}(s)
, which is equivalent
to the property we just stated. This finishes the proof that the type of
the induction principle and computation rule is a mere proposition.

It remains to show that
(Y,\pi)=(Y^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
, provided that
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are both
in
\mathcal{M}
, and that both sides satisfy
the induction principle and computation rule. It suffices to find an equivalence

f:Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
f\circ\pi=\pi^{\prime}
.

From the induction principles of
Y
resp.
Y^{\prime}
, we obtain a function

f:Y\to Y^{\prime}
with the property that
f\circ\pi=\pi^{\prime}
, and a function

f^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to Y
with the property that
f^{\prime}\circ\pi^{\prime}=\pi
.
To show that
f^{\prime}\circ f=\mathsf{id}
we use the induction principle
of
Y
. Since the type
f^{\prime}(f(y))=y
is in
\mathcal{M}
, it suffices to show that

f^{\prime}(f(\pi(y)))=\pi(y)
. This readily follows from the defining properties of
f

and
f^{\prime}
. Similarly, we have
f\circ f^{\prime}=\mathsf{id}
.
∎

Theorem 6.13
.

A higher modality is a uniquely eliminating modality, with the
same modal types.

Proof.

Let
{\ocircle}
be a modality with modal units
{\eta_{A}}
. Our goal is to show
that the pre-composition map

{\lambda}s.\,s\circ{\eta_{A}}:({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}A}{\ocircle}(P(x)))\to({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}{\ocircle}(P({\eta_{A}}(a))))

is an equivalence for each
A:\mathcal{U}
and
P:{\ocircle}A\to\mathcal{U}
.
By the given induction principle and computation rule, we obtain a
right inverse
\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}A}
of
\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ{\eta_{A}}
.

To show that it is a left inverse, consider
s:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}A}{\ocircle}(P(x))
.
We need to find a homotopy

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}A}s(x)\mathbin{{=}}\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}A}(s\circ{\eta}_{A})(x).

By assumption we have that
P(x)
is
modal for each
z:{\ocircle}A
and hence it follows that
s(x)\mathbin{{=}}\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}A}(s\circ{\eta}_{A})(x)

is modal for each
x
. Hence it suffices to find a function of type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}s({\eta}_{A}(a))\mathbin{{=}}\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}A}(s\circ{\eta}_{A})({\eta}_{A}(a)).

This follows straight from the computation rule of higher modalities.
∎

2

Uniquely eliminating modalities

Next, we show that a uniquely eliminating modality is determined by its modal types, and gives rise to a
\Sigma
-closed reflective subuniverse.

Lemma 6.14
.

Given a uniquely eliminating modality,
{\ocircle}X
is modal for any type
X
.

Proof.

Using the elimination principle of
{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X
, we find a function

f:{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}X
and an identification
f\circ{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}=\mathsf{id}_{{\ocircle}X}
.
By the uniqueness property, the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(g:{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(g:{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(g:{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X)}}g\circ{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}={\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}

is contractible. Since both
\mathsf{id}_{{\ocircle}{\ocircle}X}
and
{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}\circ f

are in this type (with suitable identifications), we find that
f
is also the
right inverse of
{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}
. This shows that
{\eta_{{\ocircle}X}}
is an
equivalence, so
{\ocircle}X
is modal.
∎

Theorem 6.15
.

The data of two uniquely eliminating modalities
{\ocircle}
and
{\ocircle}^{\prime}
are equivalent if and only if both have the same modal types.

Proof.

We need to show that the type of uniquely eliminating modalities
with a given class
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
of modal types
is a mere proposition. Since the types of the form
{\ocircle}X
are modal,
it suffices to show for any class
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
and any type
X
, that

the type of tuples
(Y,p,\pi,H)
— consisting of a type
Y

with
p
witnessing that
Y
is in
\mathcal{M}
, a function
\pi:X\to Y
, and
for each
P:Y\to\mathcal{U}
a term
H_{P}
witnessing that the function

{\lambda}s.\,s\circ\pi:({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{y:Y}{\ocircle}(P(y)))\to({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}{\ocircle}(P(\pi(x))))

is an equivalence — is a mere proposition.

Let
(Y,p,\pi,H)
and
(Y^{\prime},p^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},H^{\prime})
be such tuples. To show that they are
equal, it suffices to show that
(Y,\pi)=(Y^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
because the other things
in the list are terms of mere propositions. Furthermore, showing that

(Y,\pi)=(Y^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is equivalent to finding an equivalence
f:Y\simeq Y^{\prime}
with
the property that
f\circ\pi=\pi^{\prime}
. By
H
, there is such a function, and by

H^{\prime}
there is a function
f^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to Y
such that
f^{\prime}\circ\pi^{\prime}=\pi
. Now the
uniqueness gives that
f^{\prime}\circ f
is the only function from
Y
to
Y
such
that
f^{\prime}\circ f\circ\pi=\pi
and of course
\mathsf{id}_{Y}
is another such function.
Therefore it follows that
f^{\prime}\circ f=\mathsf{id}
, and similarly it follows that

f\circ f^{\prime}=\mathsf{id}
.
∎

Theorem 6.16
.

Any uniquely eliminating modality determines a
\Sigma
-closed reflective
subuniverse with the same modal types.

Proof.

It is immediate from the definition of uniquely eliminating modalities
that every map
f:A\to B
into a modal type
B
has a homotopy unique extension to
{\ocircle}A

along the modal unit:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

Since the types of the form
{\ocircle}X
are modal, we obtain a reflective subuniverse.
It remains to verify that the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x))
is modal for
any type
X
and
P:X\to\mathcal{U}
. We have the function

\varphi\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}m.\,{\mathopen{}(f(m),g(m))\mathclose{}}:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)),

where

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}}({\lambda}x.\,{\lambda}u.\,x)

\displaystyle:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))\to{\ocircle}X

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{ind}_{{\ocircle}}({\lambda}x.\,{\lambda}u.\,u)

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{w:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}{\ocircle}(P(f(w)))

Our goal is to show that
\varphi
is an inverse to the modal unit.

Note that

\varphi({\eta}(x,y))\equiv{\mathopen{}(f({\eta}(x,y)),g({\eta}(x,y)))\mathclose{}}\equiv{\mathopen{}(x,y)\mathclose{}},

so we see immediately that
\varphi
is a left inverse of
{\eta}
.

To show that
\varphi
is a right inverse of
{\eta}
, note that the type
of functions
h
fitting in a commuting square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}

is contractible, and it contains the identity function. Therefore, it suffices
to show that
({\eta}\circ\varphi)\circ{\eta}={\eta}
, but this follows
from the fact that
\varphi
is a left inverse of the modal unit.
∎

3

\Sigma
-closed reflective subuniverses

Now we study reflective subuniverses in a bit more detail, and end by
showing that
\Sigma
-closed ones give rise to stable factorization
systems.
\Sigma
-closure is used in
6.20
to
show that left maps and right maps are closed under composition.

\Sigma
-closed reflective subuniverses

Definition 6.17
.

Let
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{Prop}
be a reflective subuniverse with modal
operator
{\ocircle}
. We say
that a type
X
is
{\ocircle}
-connected
if
{\ocircle}X
is contractible,
and we say that a function
f:X\to Y
is
{\ocircle}
-connected
if each
of its fibers is. Similarly, we say that
f
is
modal
if each of its
fibers is.

Note that a type
X
is modal or
{\ocircle}
-connected just when the map
X\to\mathbf{1}
is.

Example 6.18
.

Recall from
Section

6
that the open modality associated to a proposition
Q
is defined by
\mathsf{Op}_{Q}(A)\vcentcolon\equiv(Q\to A)
.
We claim that
A
is
\mathsf{Op}_{Q}
-connected if and only if
Q\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)
.
In other words,
(Q\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A))\simeq\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(Q\to A)
.
For on the one hand, if
Q\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)
, then
Q\to A
; while any two
f,g:Q\to A
can be shown equal by function extensionality, since if
Q
then
A
is contractible.
But on the other hand, if
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(Q\to A)
and
Q
, then
(Q\to A)\simeq A
, hence
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)
.

Note that
Q\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)
is also the defining condition for the
\mathsf{Cl}_{Q}
-modal types from
Section

6
.
That is, the
\mathsf{Op}_{Q}
-connected types coincide with the
\mathsf{Cl}_{Q}
-modal types.

The following theorem combines Lemma 7.5.7 and Theorem 7.7.4 of
[
33
]
.

Theorem 6.19
.

Given a reflective universe with modal operator
{\ocircle}
,
the following are equivalent:

(i)

It is
\Sigma
-closed.

(ii)

It is uniquely eliminating.

(iii)

The modal units are
{\ocircle}
-connected.

Proof.

Suppose first that
{\ocircle}
is
\Sigma
-closed, let
X
be a type and let

P:{\ocircle}X\to\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle}
, i.e.
P(x)
is modal for each
x:{\ocircle}X
.
To show that
{\ocircle}
is uniquely eliminating, we want

{\lambda}s.\,s\circ{\eta_{X}}:({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}P(x))\to({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}P({\eta}(x)))

to be an equivalence. Since the type
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}B(a)
is equivalent to the type
of functions
f:A\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}B(a)
such that
\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ f=\mathsf{id}_{A}
, we
get the desired equivalence if the pre-composition map
{\lambda}j.\,j\circ{\eta}

gives an equivalence from diagonal fillers of the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

to the type of maps
X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}P(x)
such that the indicated square
commutes. But this is true by the universal property of
{\eta}
, since
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}P(x)
is modal by
\Sigma
-closedness.

Now suppose that
{\ocircle}
is uniquely eliminating.
To show that the modal units are connected, we want a term of type

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}({\ocircle}(\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}(x))).

Using the dependent eliminators, it is easy to find a term

s:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}(x))
with the property that

s\circ{\eta}(x)={\eta}(x,\mathsf{refl}_{{\eta}(x)})
. Now we need to show
that

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{w:{\ocircle}(\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}(x))}w=s(x).

Since the type
w=s(x)
is modal, this is equivalent to

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{x^{\prime}:X}{p:{\eta}(x^{\prime})=x}{\eta}(x^{\prime},p)=s(x).

Moreover, the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\eta}(x^{\prime})=x
is contractible, so this
is equivalent to

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x^{\prime}:X}{\eta}(x^{\prime},\mathsf{refl}_{{\eta}(x^{\prime})})=s({\eta}(x^{\prime})),

of which we have a term by the defining property of
s
.

Finally, suppose that all the modal units are
{\ocircle}
-connected, let
X
be modal and let
P:X\to\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle}
.
To show that
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
is modal, we show that

{\eta}:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))\to{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))
is an equivalence.
Since
X
is modal, we can extend
\mathsf{pr}_{1}
along
{\eta}
as indicated
in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

The type of maps

f:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)

such that
\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ f=p
is equivalent to the type
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))}P(p(z))
.
Using the assumption that
{\eta}
is connected, we calculate

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))}P(p(z))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))}{\ocircle}(\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}(z))\to P(p(z))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))}\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}(z)\to P(p(z))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{{\mathopen{}(x,y)\mathclose{}}:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}P(x)

We have the second projection
\mathsf{pr}_{2}
of the latter type. We obtain a term

q:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{z:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))}P(p(z))

such that
q(\eta(x,y))=y
. Therefore, we get the map
{\mathopen{}(p,q)\mathclose{}}:{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
for which the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathopen{}(p,q)\mathclose{}}}

commutes. In particular,
{\mathopen{}(p,q)\mathclose{}}
is a left inverse of the modal unit.
To see that it is also a right inverse, note that
{\eta}\circ{\mathopen{}(p,q)\mathclose{}}\circ{\eta}={\eta}=\mathsf{id}\circ{\eta}
; thus
{\eta}\circ{\mathopen{}(p,q)\mathclose{}}=\mathsf{id}
follows by uniqueness.
Hence
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)
is modal.
∎

Theorem 6.20
.

A
\Sigma
-closed reflective subuniverse determines a stable orthogonal factorization system with the same
modal types.

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{L}
to be the class of
{\ocircle}
-connected
maps and
\mathcal{R}
to be the class of modal maps.
We first show that both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
are closed under
composition. Recall that for
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
, one has

\mathsf{fib}_{g\circ f}(z)=\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}{\sum_{(p:\mathsf{fib}_{g}(z))}}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(p))
.
Thus, by
\Sigma
-closedness, if
f
and
g
are both in
\mathcal{R}
then so is
g\circ f
, so
\mathcal{R}
is closed under composition; while
Section

5
implies that
\mathcal{L}
is closed under composition.
And since the fibers of an identity map are contractible, and contractible types are both modal and
{\ocircle}
-connected, both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
contain all identities.

To obtain a factorization system,
it remains to show that the type of

(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
-factorizations of any function
f
is contractible.
Since
{\mathopen{}(X,f)\mathclose{}}={\mathopen{}(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}\mathsf{fib}_{f}(y),\mathsf{pr}_{1})\mathclose{}}
, it is sufficient to
show that
\mathsf{fact}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{pr}_{1})
is contractible for any

\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}P(y)\to Y
. But
\mathsf{pr}_{1}
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}P(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\ocircle}(P(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{R}}}

where
p_{\mathcal{L}}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{tot}({\eta_{P(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})}})
and
p_{\mathcal{R}}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{pr}_{1}
.
The fibers of
p_{\mathcal{R}}
are
{\ocircle}(P(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}))
, so it follows
immediately that
p_{\mathcal{R}}
is in
\mathcal{R}
.
Moreover, since

\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{tot}({\eta})}({\mathopen{}(y,u)\mathclose{}})\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta_{P(y)}}}(u)
and each
{\eta}
is
{\ocircle}
-connected, it follows that
p_{\mathcal{L}}
is in

\mathcal{L}
.

Now consider any other factorization
(g,h,H)
of
\mathsf{pr}_{1}
into
an
\mathcal{L}
-map
g:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}P(y))\to I
followed by an
\mathcal{R}
-map
h:I\to Y
. Since

I=\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y)
, we have a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}P(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:Y)}}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}{\sum_{(y:Y)}}\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

in which
\gamma(y,u)\vcentcolon\equiv{\mathopen{}(g(y,u),H(y,u))\mathclose{}}
.
It follows that
(g,h,H)=(\mathsf{tot}(\gamma),\mathsf{pr}_{1},\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt})
.
Thus suffices to show that there is a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(P(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{y}}

We will do this using
Section

1
, by showing that
\gamma_{y}
has the same universal property as
{\eta_{P(y)}}
.
This follows from the following calculation:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y)\to Z)

\displaystyle\simeq((\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}}{\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}{\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}{\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}{\ocircle}(\mathsf{fib}_{g}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(w))))\to Z)

\displaystyle\simeq((\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}}{\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}{\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}{\sum_{(w:\mathsf{fib}_{h}(y))}}\mathsf{fib}_{g}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(w)))\to Z)

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathsf{fib}_{h\circ g}(y)\to Z)

\displaystyle\simeq(P(y)\to Z).

which we can verify is given by precomposition with
\gamma_{y}
.

It remains to show that our orthogonal factorization system is stable. Consider a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in which
l
is in
\mathcal{L}
. By the pasting lemma for pullbacks, it
follows that
\mathsf{fib}_{k}(b)=\mathsf{fib}_{l}(g(b))
for each
b:B^{\prime}
. Thus, it follows that

k
is in
\mathcal{L}
.
∎

4

Stable orthogonal factorization systems

To complete
Section

6
, we will show that stable orthogonal factorization systems are also determined by their modal types, and give rise to higher modalities.

Orthogonal factorization systems

In classical category theory, orthogonal factorization systems are equivalently characterized by a unique lifting property.
We begin with the analogue of this in our context.

Definition 6.21
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
be an orthogonal factorization system, and
consider a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(i.e. paths
S:r\circ f=g\circ l
)
for which
l
is in
\mathcal{L}
and
r
is in
\mathcal{R}
. We define

\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)
to be the type of
diagonal fillers

of the above diagram, i.e. the type of tuples
(j,H_{f},H_{g},K)
consisting of

j:B\to X
,
H_{f}:j\circ l=f
and
H_{g}:r\circ j=g
and an equality
K:r\circ H_{f}=S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l)
.

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
be an orthogonal factorization system, and
consider a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

for which
l
is in
\mathcal{L}
and
r
is in
\mathcal{R}
. Then the type

\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)
of diagonal fillers is contractible.

Proof.

By the fact that every morphism factors uniquely as a left map followed by a
right map, we may factorize
f
and
g
in
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
as
H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}}
and
H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}
, obtaining the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\mathcal{R}}}

Now both
(r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}})\circ f_{\mathcal{L}}
and

g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ(g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)
are factorizations
of the same function
r\circ f:A\to Y
.
Since
\mathsf{fact}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(r\circ f)
is contractible, so is its identity type

(\im(f),f_{\mathcal{L}},r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}},r\circ H_{f})=(\im(g),g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l,g_{\mathcal{R}},S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l)).

This identity type is equivalent to

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}}{\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}{\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}{\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}\\
((r\circ H_{f})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})=S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ H_{\mathcal{L}}))

Now since
\mathsf{fact}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)
and
\mathsf{fact}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(g)
are also contractible, we can sum over them to get that the following type is contractible:

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\im(f):\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(\im(f):\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(\im(f):\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(\im(f):\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to\im(f))}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to\im(f))}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to\im(f))}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to\im(f))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(f)\to X)}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(f)\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(f)\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(f)\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}\\
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to\im(g))}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to\im(g))}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to\im(g))}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to\im(g))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(g)\to Y)}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(g)\to Y)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(g)\to Y)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:\im(g)\to Y)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}\\
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}}{\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}{\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}{\sum_{(e:\im(f)\simeq\im(g))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=e\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ e)}}\\
((r\circ H_{f})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})=S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ H_{\mathcal{L}}))

(omitting the hypotheses that
f_{\mathcal{L}},g_{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathcal{L}
and
f_{\mathcal{R}},g_{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{R}
).
Reassociating and removing the contractible type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(\im(g):\mathcal{U})}}(\im(f)\simeq\im(g))
, and renaming
\im(f)
as simply
I
, this is equivalent to

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})}}\\
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=f_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=f_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{L}}:g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=f_{\mathcal{L}})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}})}}{\sum_{(H_{\mathcal{R}}:r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}})}}\\
((r\circ H_{f})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{\mathcal{L}})=S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(g_{\mathcal{R}}\circ H_{\mathcal{L}}))

Removing the contractible
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{L}}:A\to I)}}(g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l=f_{\mathcal{L}})
and
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to Y)}}(r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=g_{\mathcal{R}})
, this becomes

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}\\
(r\circ H_{f}=S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l))

Inserting a contractible
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}(f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)
, and reassociating more, we get

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}}{\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}{\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}{\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}\\
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}\circ l)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}})}}(r\circ H_{f}=S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l))

But now
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(I:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}{\sum_{(f_{\mathcal{R}}:I\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}{\sum_{(g_{\mathcal{L}}:B\to I)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}}{\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}{\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}{\sum_{(H_{j}:f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ g_{\mathcal{L}}=j)}}
is just
\mathsf{fact}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(j)
, hence contractible.
Removing it, we get

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{f}:f=j\circ l)}}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=j\circ l)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=j\circ l)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:f=j\circ l)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ j)}}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ j)}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ j)}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:g=r\circ j)}}(r\circ H_{f}=S\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l))

which is just
\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)
.
Therefore, this is also contractible.
∎

Definition 6.23
.

For any class
\mathcal{C}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}*{A,B:\mathcal{U}}(A\to B)\to\mathsf{Prop}
of maps, we define

(i)

{}^{\bot}\mathcal{C}
to be the class of maps with
(unique) left lifting
property
with respect to all maps in
\mathcal{C}
: the mere proposition

{}^{\bot}\mathcal{C}(l)
asserts that for every commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with
r
in
\mathcal{C}
, the type
\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)
of diagonal fillers is contractible.

(ii)

\mathcal{C}^{\bot}
to be the class of maps with the dual
(unique) right lifting
property
with respect to all maps in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 6.24
.

In an orthogonal factorization system
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
, one has

\mathcal{L}={{}^{\bot}\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\bot}=\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathcal{L}={{}^{\bot}\mathcal{R}}
, i.e. we show that

\mathcal{L}(f)\leftrightarrow{{}^{\bot}\mathcal{R}}(f)
for any map
f
. Note
that the implication
\mathcal{L}(f)\to{{}^{\bot}\mathcal{R}}(f)
follows from

Section

4
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a map in
{{}^{\bot}\mathcal{R}}
.
We wish to show that
\mathcal{L}(f)
. Consider the factorization

(f_{\mathcal{L}},f_{\mathcal{R}})
of
f
. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{im}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}}

commutes. Since
f
has the left lifting property, the type of diagonal fillers
of this square is contractible. Thus we have a section
j
of
f_{\mathcal{R}}
.
The map
j\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}
is then a diagonal filler of the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{im}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{im}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{R}}}

Of course, the identity map
\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{im}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f)}

is also a diagonal filler for this square, so the fact that the type of
such diagonal fillers is contractible implies that
j\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}=\mathsf{id}
.
Thus,
j
and
f_{\mathcal{R}}
are inverse equivalences, and so the pair
(B,f)
is equal to the pair
(\mathsf{im}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(f),f_{\mathcal{L}})
.
Hence
f
, like
f_{\mathcal{L}}
, is in
\mathcal{L}
.

Similarly,
Section

4
also implies that
\mathcal{R}(f)\to\mathcal{L}^{\bot}(f)

for any map
f
, while we can prove
\mathcal{L}^{\bot}(f)\to\mathcal{R}(f)
analogously to
{{}^{\bot}\mathcal{R}}(f)\to\mathcal{L}(f)
.
∎

Corollary 6.25
.

The data of two orthogonal factorization systems
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
and

(\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
are identical if and only if

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

“Only if” is obvious.
Conversely, if
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, then by
Section

4
we have
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
, and the remaining data of an orthogonal factorization system is a mere proposition.
∎

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
be an orthogonal factorization system. Then
the class
\mathcal{R}
is stable under pullbacks.

Proof.

Consider a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
h:X\to Y
is assumed to be in
\mathcal{R}
, and let
k=k_{\mathcal{R}}\circ k_{\mathcal{L}}
be a factorization of
h
.
Then the outer rectangle in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathcal{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes, so by the universal property of pullbacks we obtain a unique map
j:\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(k)\to A
such that
j\circ k_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathsf{id}
and
k\circ j=k_{\mathcal{R}}
.
It suffices to show that
k_{\mathcal{L}}
is an equivalence, and since we already have that
j\circ k_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathsf{id}
we only need to show that
k_{\mathcal{L}}\circ j=\mathsf{id}
.

We do this using the contractibility of the type of diagonal fillers. Consider the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathcal{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathcal{R}}}

for which
\mathsf{id}:\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(k)\to\im_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(k)
(with the trivial homotopies) is a diagonal filler. However, we also have the homotopies
k_{\mathcal{L}}\circ j\circ k_{\mathcal{L}}\sim k_{\mathcal{L}}
and
k_{\mathcal{R}}\circ k_{\mathcal{L}}\circ j\sim k\circ j\sim k_{\mathcal{R}}
. This shows that we have a second diagonal filler, of which the underlying map is
k_{\mathcal{L}}\circ j
. Since the type of diagonal fillers is contractible, it follows that
k_{\mathcal{L}}\circ j=\mathsf{id}
, as desired.
∎

Stable orthogonal factorization systems

Lemma 6.27
.

Given
l,r,f,g
and a homotopy
S:r\circ f=g\circ l
, consider as
b:B
varies all the diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and write
S_{b}:r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!}
for the induced commutative square.
(Note that the square on the left commutes judgmentally.)
Then the map

\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)\to{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b}),

defined by precomposition with
b
, is an equivalence.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the map on total spaces

\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b})\Big)

(1)

is an equivalence.
The domain of (
1
) can be computed as

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{f}:j\circ l=f)}}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:j\circ l=f)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:j\circ l=f)}}{\sum_{(H_{f}:j\circ l=f)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H_{g}:r\circ j=g)}}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:r\circ j=g)}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:r\circ j=g)}}{\sum_{(H_{g}:r\circ j=g)}}(r\circ H_{f})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(H_{g}\circ l)^{-1}=S

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}(j\circ l=f)\times(r\circ j=g)

by contracting a based path space.
On the other hand, note that

\displaystyle(r\circ f=g\circ l)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}r(f(a))=g(l(a))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}{b:B}{l(a)=b}r(f(a))=g(l(a))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}{u:\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)}r(f(i_{b}(a)))=g(l(i_{b}(a)))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}{u:\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)}r(f(i_{b}(a)))=g(b)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}(r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!})

That is, to give
S
is the same as to give each
S_{b}
.
Thus the codomain of (
1
) can be computed as

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\sum_{(S:r\circ f=g\circ l)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(S:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}(r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!}))}}}{\sum_{(S:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}(r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!}))}}{\sum_{(S:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}(r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!}))}}{\sum_{(S:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}(r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!}))}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b})

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(S_{b}:r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!})}}}{\sum_{(S_{b}:r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!})}}{\sum_{(S_{b}:r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!})}}{\sum_{(S_{b}:r\circ(f\circ i_{b})=(g\circ b)\circ\mathord{!})}}\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b})

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}(j_{b}=f\circ i_{b})\times(r\circ j_{b}=g(b))

using the same argument as above for
S
.
Now we can compute

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:\mathbf{1}\to X)}}(j_{b}=f\circ i_{b})\times(r\circ j_{b}=g\circ b)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j_{b}:X)}}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:X)}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:X)}}{\sum_{(j_{b}:X)}}({\lambda}x.\,j_{b}=f\circ i_{b})\times(r(j_{b})=g(b))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}({\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,j(b)=f\circ i_{b})\times(r(j(b))=g(b))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}{\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,j(b)=f\circ i_{b})\times({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}r(j(b))=g(b))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}{\lambda}\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\hskip 4.30554pt}\hskip 1.0pt}.\,j(b)=f\circ i_{b})\times(r\circ j=g)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}{p:l(a)=b}j(b)=f(a))\times(r\circ j=g)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}j(l(a))=f(a))\times(r\circ j=g)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(j:B\to X)}}(j\circ l=f)\times(r\circ j=g)

which is what we computed as the domain of (
1
) above.
∎

Corollary 6.28
.

In any orthogonal factorization system

(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
, if

l:A\to B
is a map such that
\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)\to\mathbf{1}
is in
\mathcal{L}
for each
b:B
, then also
l
itself is in
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

By
Section

4
,
l
is in
\mathcal{L}
iff
\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)
is contractible for each
r\in\mathcal{R}
and
S
as in
Section

4
, while similarly
\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)\to\mathbf{1}
is in
\mathcal{L}
iff
\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b})
is contractible.
But the product of contractible types is contractible.
∎

Corollary 6.29
.

In any stable orthogonal factorization system, if
l\perp r
for all maps
l\in\mathcal{L}
of the form
l:A\to\mathbf{1}
, then
r\in\mathcal{R}
.
In particular, for any modality
{\ocircle}
, if
X\to(A\to X)
is an equivalence for all
{\ocircle}
-connected types
A
, then
X
is modal.

Proof.

By
Section

4
, for any
l\in\mathcal{L}
and commutative square
S
from
l
to
r
, we have
\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)\simeq{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b})
.
Since
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
is stable, each map
\mathord{!}_{b}:\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)\to\mathbf{1}
is also in
\mathcal{L}
, so that
\mathord{!}_{b}\perp r
by assumption.
Thus
\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S_{b})
is contractible for all
b
, hence so is
\mathsf{fill}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}}(S)
.

For the second statement, the type
f:A\to X
is equivalent to the type of commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and the type of fillers for such a square is equivalent to the type of
x:X
such that
f(a)=x
for all
a:A
, i.e. the fiber of
X\to(A\to X)
over
f
.
Thus, the assumption ensures that all such types of fillers are contractible, i.e.
l\perp r
for all
{\ocircle}
-connected maps of the form
l:A\to\mathbf{1}
, so the first statement applies.
∎

Lemma 6.30
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
be a stable orthogonal factorization system.
Then a map
r:X\to Y
is in
\mathcal{R}
if and only if
\mathsf{fib}_{r}(y)

is
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
-modal for each
y:Y
.

Proof.

The class of right maps is stable under pullbacks by
Section

4
,
so it suffices to show that any map with modal fibers is in
\mathcal{R}
.

Let
r:X\to Y
be a map with modal fibers. Our goal is to show that

r
is in
\mathcal{R}
. By
Section

4
it suffices to show that

r
has the right lifting property with respect to the left maps.
Consider a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in which
l
is a map in
\mathcal{L}
.
We wish to show that the type of diagonal fillers is contractible.
By
Section

4
, the type of diagonal fillers of the above diagram
is equivalent to the dependent product of the types of fillers of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ i_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(b)}

indexed by
b:B
. Thus, it suffices that the type of diagonal fillers for this
square is contractible for each
b:B
. Since any filler factors uniquely through
the pullback
\mathbf{1}\times_{Y}X
, which is
\mathsf{fib}_{r}(g(b))
, the type of diagonal
fillers of the above square is equivalent to the type of diagonal fillers of the
square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{l}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{r}(g(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}

where the dotted map, is the unique map into the pullback
\mathsf{fib}_{r}(g(b))
. In
this square, the left map is in
\mathcal{L}
because
\mathcal{L}
is assumed
to be stable under pullbacks, and the right map is in
\mathcal{R}
by assumption,
so the type of diagonal fillers is contractible.
∎

Theorem 6.31
.

Any two stable orthogonal factorization systems with the same modal types are
equal.

Proof.

By
Section

4
it follows that any orthogonal factorization system
is completely determined by the class of right maps.
By
Section

4
it follows that in a stable orthogonal factorization
system, the class of right maps is completely determined by the modal types.
∎

Theorem 6.32
.

Any stable orthogonal factorization system determines a higher modality with
the same modal types.

Proof.

For every type
X
we have the
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
-factorization
X\to{\ocircle}X\to\mathbf{1}
of the
unique map
X\to\mathbf{1}
. This determines the modal unit

{\eta}:X\to{\ocircle}X
which is in
\mathcal{L}
, and the
unique map
{\ocircle}X\to\mathbf{1}
is in
\mathcal{R}
, i.e.
{\ocircle}X
is
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
-modal.

To show the induction principle, let
P:{\ocircle}X\to\mathcal{U}
and
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}{\ocircle}(P(\eta(x)))
.
Then we have a (judgmentally) commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

Note that by
Section

4
,
the projection
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))\to{\ocircle}X
is in
\mathcal{R}

because its fibers are modal. Also, the modal unit

{\eta}:X\to{\ocircle}X
is in
\mathcal{L}
.
Thus, by
Section

4
, the type of fillers of this square is contractible.
Such a filler consists of a function
s
and homotopies filling the two triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

whose composite is reflexivity, i.e. the type

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:{\ocircle}X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}}{\sum_{(s:{\ocircle}X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}{\sum_{(s:{\ocircle}X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}{\sum_{(s:{\ocircle}X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(x:{\ocircle}X)}}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s(x))=x)}}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s(x))=x)}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s(x))=x)}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s(x))=x)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(K:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s({\eta}(x))=f(x))}}}{\sum_{(K:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s({\eta}(x))=f(x))}}{\sum_{(K:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s({\eta}(x))=f(x))}}{\sum_{(K:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s({\eta}(x))=f(x))}}\\
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(K(x))=H({\eta}(x)).

If we decompose
s
,
f
, and
K
by their components, we get

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s_{1}:{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(s_{1}:{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(s_{1}:{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(s_{1}:{\ocircle}X\to{\ocircle}X)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(s_{1}(x))))}}}{\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(s_{1}(x))))}}{\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(s_{1}(x))))}}{\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(s_{1}(x))))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}s_{1}(x)=x)}}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}s_{1}(x)=x)}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}s_{1}(x)=x)}}{\sum_{(H:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}s_{1}(x)=x)}}\\
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(K_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{1}({\eta}(x))=f_{1}(x))}}}{\sum_{(K_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{1}({\eta}(x))=f_{1}(x))}}{\sum_{(K_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{1}({\eta}(x))=f_{1}(x))}}{\sum_{(K_{1}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{1}({\eta}(x))=f_{1}(x))}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(K_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{2}({\eta}(x))=_{K_{1}(x)}f_{2}(x))}}}{\sum_{(K_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{2}({\eta}(x))=_{K_{1}(x)}f_{2}(x))}}{\sum_{(K_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{2}({\eta}(x))=_{K_{1}(x)}f_{2}(x))}}{\sum_{(K_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{2}({\eta}(x))=_{K_{1}(x)}f_{2}(x))}}\\
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}K_{1}(x)=H({\eta}(x)).

Now we can contract
s_{1}
and
H
, and also
K_{1}
with the final unnamed homotopy, to get

\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}}{\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}{\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}{\sum_{(s_{2}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{\ocircle}X}{\ocircle}(P(x)))}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}s_{2}({\eta}(x))=_{K_{1}(x)}f_{2}(x).

But this is just the type of extensions of
f
along
{\eta}
, i.e. the fiber of precomposition by
{\eta}
.
Thus, precomposition by
{\eta}
is an equivalence, so in fact that we have a uniquely eliminating modality.
By
Section

1
, the identity types of
{\ocircle}X
are modal, so we have a higher modality as well.
∎

7

Accessible reflective subuniverses

Definition 7.1
.

Given a family
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}A_{i}\to B_{i}
of maps, a type
X
is said to be
f
-local
if the precomposition map

{f_{i}}^{\ast}:(B_{i}\to X)\to(A_{i}\to X)

is an equivalence, for each
i:I
. The family
f
is said to be a
presentation
of a reflective subuniverse
L
if the subuniverses of
f
-local types and
L
-local types coincide. A reflective subuniverse is said to be
accessible
if there exists a presentation for it.

In
[
28
]
it is shown that the subuniverse of
f
-local types is always a reflective subuniverse, provided that sufficiently many higher inductive types are available. However, it is not clear whether their construction is possible in our current setting, where the only higher inductive types that are assumed to exist are homotopy pushouts. In this section we will establish general properties of accessible reflective subuniverses. We will show in
Chapter

7
that for any family
f
of maps between
compact
types, the subuniverse of
f
-local types is indeed reflective.

Remark 7.2
.

Note that being accessible is structure; different families can present the same reflective subuniverse or modality.
As a trivial example, note that localizing at the empty
type, and localizing at the type family on
\mathbf{2}
defined by

{0_{\mathbf{2}}}\mapsto\mathbf{0}
and
{1_{\mathbf{2}}}\mapsto\mathbf{1}
both map all types to contractible types.

However, we are usually only interested in properties of presentations insofar as they determine properties of subuniverses.
For instance, by
Section

7
, a reflective subuniverse is a modality exactly when it has a presentation in which each
C(a)=\mathbf{1}
.

Example 7.3
.

The trivial modality
\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\|_{(-2)}
is presented by
\mathbf{0}
, while the propositional truncation modality
\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\|_{(-1)}
is presented by
\mathbf{2}
. More generally, the

n
-truncation modality
\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\|_{n}
is presented by the
(n+1)
-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n+1}
.

Example 7.4
.

For every mere proposition
P
, the open modality
\mathsf{Op}_{P}(X)\vcentcolon\equiv(P\to X)
from
Section

6
is
presented by the singleton type family
P
.
To see this, note that
{\eta_{X}}:X\to(P\to X)
is the same as the map in the definition of locality, so that
X
is modal for the open modality on
P
if and only if it is
P
-local.
(If
P
is not a mere proposition, however, then
X\mapsto(P\to X)
is not a modality, and in particular does not coincide with localization at
P
.)

Example 7.5
.

The closed modality
\mathsf{Cl}_{P}
from
Section

6
associated to a mere proposition
P
is presented by the type family
{\lambda}x.\,\mathbf{0}:P\to\mathcal{U}
.
For by definition,
A
is null for this family if and only if for any
p:P
the map
A\to(\mathbf{0}\to A)
is an equivalence.
But
\mathbf{0}\to P
is contractible, so this says that
P\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}contr}(A)
, which was the definition of
\mathsf{Cl}_{P}
-modal types from
Section

6
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
f:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}A_{i}\to B_{i}
be a family of maps. Denote the family consisting of the suspensions
of the functions by
\mathsf{\Sigma}{f}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}\mathsf{\Sigma}{A_{i}}\to\mathsf{\Sigma}{B_{i}}
.
A type
X
is
\mathsf{\Sigma}f
-local if and only if for every
x,y:X
, the type

x=_{X}y
is
f
-local.
In other words,
L_{\mathsf{\Sigma}{f}}=(L_{f})^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By the induction principle for suspension and naturality, we obtain for each
i:I
a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{\Sigma}{B_{i}}\to X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:X)}}}{\sum_{(x,y:X)}}{\sum_{(x,y:X)}}{\sum_{(x,y:X)}}(B_{i}\to x=y)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{\Sigma}{A_{i}}\to X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x,y:X)}}}{\sum_{(x,y:X)}}{\sum_{(x,y:X)}}{\sum_{(x,y:X)}}(A_{i}\to x=y)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

in which the horizontal maps are equivalences.
So
X
is
\mathsf{\Sigma}f
-local if and only if the right vertical map is an equivalence
for every
i:I
, if and only if for each
x,y:X
, the type
x=y
is
f_{i}
-local
for every
i:I
.
∎

A general localization is only a reflective subuniverse, but there is a convenient sufficient condition for it to be a modality: if each
C(a)=\mathbf{1}
.
A localization modality of this sort is called
nullification
.

Theorem 7.7
.

If
F:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}B(a)\to C(a)
is such that each
C(a)=\mathbf{1}
, then localization at
F
is a modality, called
nullification at
B
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for any
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
, the
B
-null types are
\Sigma
-closed.
Thus, let
X:\mathcal{U}
and
Y:X\to\mathcal{U}
be such that
X
and each
Y(x)
are
B
-null.
Then

\displaystyle(B\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Y(x))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:B\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:B\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:B\to X)}}{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{b:B}Y(g(b))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}B\to Y(x)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Y(x)

with the inverse equivalence being given by constant maps.
Thus,
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Y(x)
is
B
-null.
∎

Of course, it might happen that
\mathcal{L}_{F}
is a modality even if
F
doesn’t satisfy the condition of
7.7
.
For instance, if
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
has a section
s:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}B(a)
, then localizing at the family
s^{\prime}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}\mathbf{1}\to B(a)
is equivalent to nullifying at
B
, since in a section-retraction pair the section is an equivalence if and only if the retraction is.
However, we can say the following.

Lemma 7.8
.

If
F:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a:A}B(a)\to C(a)
is such that
\mathcal{L}_{F}
is a modality, then there exists a family
E:D\to\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{F}
coincides with nullification at
E
.

Proof.

Write
{\ocircle}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathcal{L}_{F}
and
{\eta}
for its modal unit.
Define
D=\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a:A)}}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}{\sum_{(a:A)}}({\ocircle}(B(a))+{\ocircle}(C(a)))
, and
E:D\to\mathcal{U}
by

\displaystyle E(a,{\mathsf{inl}}(b))

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta_{B(a)}}}(b)

\displaystyle E(a,{\mathsf{inr}}(c))

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta_{C(a)}}}(c).

Then since
{\eta}
is
{\ocircle}
-connected, each
E(d)
is
{\ocircle}
-connected, and hence every
F
-local type is
E
-null.

On the other hand, suppose
X
is an
E
-null type.
Each
{\eta_{B(a)}}
and
{\eta_{C(a)}}
is
\mathcal{L}_{E}
-connected, since their fibers are
\mathcal{L}_{E}
-connected (by definition); thus
X
is also
{\eta_{B(a)}}
-local and
{\eta_{C(a)}}
-local.
But we have the following commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(B(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(C(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{B(a)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(F(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{C(a)}}}

and
{{\ocircle}(F(a))}
is an equivalence; thus
X
is also
F(a)
-local.
So the
F
-local types coincide with the
E
-null types.
∎

This shows that the following definition of accessible modality is consistent with our terminology of accessible reflective subuniverse.

Definition 7.9
.

A modality
{\ocircle}
on
\mathcal{U}
is said to be
accessible
if it is the nullification at a family of types in
\mathcal{U}
, indexed by a type in
\mathcal{U}
.
A
presentation
of a modality
{\ocircle}
consists of a family of types
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
, where
A:\mathcal{U}
, such that the subuniverse of modal types coincides with the subuniverse of
B
-null types.

Examples 7.10
.

Our characterizations of the truncation and open and closed modalities in
Sections

7
,
7
and
7
made no reference to the ambient universe.

Example 7.11
.

By contrast, the double-negation modality
\neg\neg

is
defined in a polymorphic way on all universes, but in general there seems no reason for it to be accessible on any of them.
However, if propositional resizing holds, then it is the nullification at
\mathbf{2}
together with all propositions
P
such that
\neg\neg P
holds, and hence accessible.

Whether or not any inaccessible modalities remain after imposing propositional resizing may depend on large-cardinal principles.
It is shown in
[
10
]
that this is the case for the analogous question about reflective sub-
(\infty,1)
-categories of the
(\infty,1)
-category of
\infty
-groupoids.

Remark 7.12
.

It is tempting to think that
any
reflective subuniverse
{\ocircle}
on
\mathcal{U}
could be extended to an accessible one on
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
by localizing at the family of
all
functions in
\mathcal{U}
that are inverted by
{\ocircle}
(or nullifying at the family of all
{\ocircle}
-connected types in
\mathcal{U}
, in the case of modalities), which is a
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
-small family though not a
\mathcal{U}
-small one.
This does produce an accessible reflective subuniverse
{\ocircle}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
such that the
{\ocircle}^{\prime}
-modal types in
\mathcal{U}
coincide with the
{\ocircle}
-modal ones, but there seems no reason why the modal
operators

{\ocircle}^{\prime}
and
{\ocircle}
should agree on types in
\mathcal{U}
.

Chapter 6

The equifibrant replacement operation

We begin this chapter with a generalization of the descent theorem for reflexive coequalizers
4.11
: the
modal descent theorem
.
Any modality
{\ocircle}
gives rise to a class of maps
f:A\to B
satisfying the condition, due to Wellen
[
38
]
, that the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}f}

is a pullback square. Following
[
38
]
we call such maps
{\ocircle}
-étale maps. The modal descent theorem asserts that a
{\ocircle}
-étale map into
A
is equivalently described as a modal map into
{\ocircle}A
. Every
{\ocircle}
-étale map is certainly modal, but the condition of being
{\ocircle}
-étale is slightly stronger than the condition of being modal.

The difference between the notions of
{\ocircle}
-étale maps and modal maps becomes perhaps most visible when we look at the left orthogonal classes of the
{\ocircle}
-étale maps and modal maps. The
{\ocircle}
-connected maps are left orthogonal to the modal maps, whereas a map is left orthogonal to the
{\ocircle}
-étale maps if and only if it is a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence (i.e. a map
f
such that
{\ocircle}f
is an equivalence). In the case of the
n
-truncation there is a clear difference: a map
f:X\to Y
is an
n
-equivalence if and only if it induces an isomorphism of homotopy groups
\pi_{i}(X)\to\pi_{i}(Y)
for any
i\leq n
