, whereas an
n
-connected map is an
n
-equivalence satisfying the further condition that
\pi_{n+1}(X)\to\pi_{n+1}(Y)
is surjective.

In
2.11
we show that the
{\ocircle}
-equivalences and the
{\ocircle}
-étale maps form an orthogonal factorization system. We call this factorization system the
reflective
factorization system of a modality. The reflective factorization system is not stable, so it does not form a new modality. What does follow is that for the unique factorization
f=f_{r}\circ f_{l}
of
f:A\to X
as a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence
f_{l}
followed by a
{\ocircle}
-étale map
f_{r}
, the canonical map

\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f_{r},e)\to\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,e)

is an equivalence for any
{\ocircle}
-étale map
e
into
X
.

We then proceed to apply these ideas to the case of reflexive graphs and the reflexive coequalizer. The class of
\Delta
-étale maps is introduces as the class of morphisms
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
of reflexive graphs satisfying the condition that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{constr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{constr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(f))}

is a pullback square, and we show that the class of
\Delta
-étale morphisms is precisely the class of fibrations of reflexive graphs, as defined in
Section

4
.

In the case of sequential colimits, another way of obtaining a cartesian transformation from an arbitrary one is to take the sequential colimit fiberwise. We establish this result in
4.3
. It has many important consequences. First of all, we show in
Section

4
that sequential colimits commute with identity types. Second, we show in
Section

4
that the sequential colimit of a fiber sequence is again a fiber sequence. Third, we show in
Section

4
that truncation levels are closed under sequential colimits, and that sequential colimits commute with truncations. Finally, we show in
4.11
that sequential colimits commute with homotopy groups.

1

Modal descent

1

{\ocircle}
-étale maps

Definition 1.1

(Definition 4.4.1 of
[
38
]
)
.

We say that a map
f:A\to B
is
{\ocircle}
-étale
if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

is a pullback square. We will write

\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}etale}(f)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}pullback}(\eta,f,\mathsf{nat\underline{\ }{}unit}_{\ocircle}(f)).

It is immediate from the definition that any equivalence is
{\ocircle}
-étale, and that the
{\ocircle}
-étale maps are closed under composition, and that every equivalence is
{\ocircle}
-étale.

Example 1.2
.

We claim that a map
f:A\to B
is
\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\|
-étale if and only if
A\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
. Examples of maps that satisfy this condition include equivalences, maps between propositions, and any map of the form
\mathbf{0}\to B
.

To see that if
f:A\to B
is
{\ocircle}
-étale, then
A\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
, consider the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|A\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|B\|,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|f\|}

and let
a:A
. Then both
\|A\|
and
\|B\|
are contractible, so
\|f\|:\|A\|\to\|B\|
is an equivalence. Since equivalences are stable under pullback it follows that
f
is an equivalence.

Now suppose that
A\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
. Since
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
is a proposition, we also have
\|A\|\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
. To see that the gap map

A\to B\times_{\|B\|}\|A\|

is an equivalence, we will show that its fibers are contractible. Let
b:B
,
x:\|A\|
and
p:\mathopen{}\left|b\right|\mathclose{}=\|f\|(x)
. Since
\|A\|\to\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}equiv}(f)
, it follows that
f
is an equivalence. Then
\|f\|
is also an equivalence, from which it follows that the naturality square is a pullback square. We conclude that the fibers of the gap map are contractible.

Lemma 1.3
.

Any map between
{\ocircle}
-modal types is
{\ocircle}
-étale.

Proof.

Suppose
f:X\to Y
is a map between
{\ocircle}
-modal types. Then the top and bottom maps in the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}Y}

are equivalences. Therefore this square is a pullback square, so
f
is
{\ocircle}
-étale.
∎

For the following lemma, recall that for a modality all propositions are modal if and only if all units
\eta:X\to{\ocircle}X
are surjective.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
{\ocircle}
be a modality for which all propositions are modal, and consider a map
f:A\to B
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is
{\ocircle}
-étale.

(ii)

The commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{{\ocircle}A}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times_{{\ocircle}B}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times_{{\ocircle}f}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square.

Remark 1.5
.

In the special case of
(-1)
-truncation, the characterization of
Section

1
asserts that a map
f:A\to B
is
(-1)
-étale if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square.

Proof.

Suppose first that
f
is
{\ocircle}
-étale, and consider the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{{\ocircle}A}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times_{{\ocircle}B}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B}

Since the top, bottom, and both front squares are pullback squares, it follows that both back squares are pullback. This proves that (i) implies (ii).

Now suppose that (ii) holds. Then the map

\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}({\eta}(a))\to\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}({\eta}(f(a)))

is an equivalence for every
a:A
. Since all propositions are assumed to be modal, it follows that

\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}(t)\to\mathsf{fib}_{{\eta}}({\ocircle}f(t))

is an equivalence for every
t:{\ocircle}A
. Thus it follows that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}

is a pullback square.
∎

Corollary 1.6
.

If
f:A\to B
is
{\ocircle}
-étale, then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{{\ocircle}A}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times_{{\ocircle}B}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{{\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{{\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times_{{\ocircle}f}f}

is a pullback square.

Proof.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{{\ocircle}A}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times_{{\ocircle}B}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{{\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{{\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times_{{\ocircle}f}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The bottom square is a pullback square by
Section

1
, and the outer rectangle is a pullback since both vertical composites are homotopic to the respective identity functions. Therefore the top square is a pullback.
∎

Theorem 1.7
.

A map
f:A\to B
is
0
-étale if and only if for each
a:A
the restriction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\|a=x\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}\|f(a)=y\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

of
f
to the connected component at
a
of
A
is an equivalence.

Proof.

By
Section

1
and the fact that
(\mathopen{}\left|a\right|_{0}\mathclose{}=\mathopen{}\left|x\right|_{0}\mathclose{})\simeq\|a=x\|
, it follows that
f
is
0
-étale if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(a,x:A)}}}{\sum_{(a,x:A)}}{\sum_{(a,x:A)}}{\sum_{(a,x:A)}}\|a=x\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b,y:B)}}}{\sum_{(b,y:B)}}{\sum_{(b,y:B)}}{\sum_{(b,y:B)}}\|b=y\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{tot}(\|\mathsf{ap}_{f}\|)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square. Furthermore, this square is a pullback if and only if the induced map

\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\|a=x\|\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}\|f(a)=y\|\Big)

is an equivalence, for each
a:A
.
∎

2

Modal descent

The following theorem can be seen as a ‘modal flattening lemma’, since it is analogous to
4.11
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p^{\prime}\sim p\circ g
, where
E
and
B
are modal types. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

is a pullback square, where
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{g}
are the unique extensions of
f
and
g
along the modal units of
B^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
, respectively.

Proof.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B^{\prime}\times_{B}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{gap}(p^{\prime},g,H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

In this diagram, the square on the right is a pullback by definition, and the outer rectangle is a pullback by assumption, so the square on the left is also a pullback. Therefore the gap map
E^{\prime}\to{\ocircle}B^{\prime}\times_{B}E
is
{\ocircle}
-connected. Moreover, since the modal types are closed under pullbacks it follows that
{\ocircle}B^{\prime}\times_{B}E
is modal, and therefore it follows that
\pi_{2}:{\ocircle}B^{\prime}\times_{B}E\to E
is a modal map. Therefore the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B^{\prime}\times_{B}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{gap}(p^{\prime},g,H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

factors
g
as a
{\ocircle}
-connected map followed by a
{\ocircle}
-modal map. Of course, another such factorization is the composite
g\sim\tilde{g}\circ{\eta}
. Since factorizations are unique, the claim follows.
∎

Using modal flattening we establish partial left exactness of the modality.

Corollary 1.9
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
f
is assumed to be
{\ocircle}
-étale. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}f}

is again a pullback square.

Proof.

Since
f
is assumed to be
{\ocircle}
-étale, the square on the right in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}f}

is a pullback square. Therefore the outer rectangle is a pullback square by the pullback pasting lemma. Now the claim follows from modal flattening
1.8
, using the outer rectangle.
∎

Corollary 1.10
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and suppose that
p:E\to B
is
{\ocircle}
-étale. Then
p^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
is
{\ocircle}
-étale.

Proof.

Consider the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B.}

The vertical squares on the back right and front right are pullback squares by assumption.
Then it follows from
Section

2
that the vertical square on the front left is a pullback square.
Therefore the square on the back left is a pullback square by the pullback pasting property.
∎

Definition 1.11
.

Let
X
be a type. We will define an operation

\mathsf{etale\underline{\ }{}map}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}}{\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}A\to{\ocircle}X\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}etale}(g)\Big)

Construction.

Given a map
f:A\to{\ocircle}X
we take the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{{\ocircle}X}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}

Then the map
\pi_{1}:X\times_{{\ocircle}X}A\to X
is
{\ocircle}
-étale by
Sections

1
and
2
.
∎

The following is a descent theorem for
{\ocircle}
-étale maps.

Theorem 1.12

(Modal descent)
.

For any modality
{\ocircle}
, and any type
X
, the operation

\mathsf{etale\underline{\ }{}map}:\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}}{\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle})}}A\to{\ocircle}X\Big)\to\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}{\sum_{(Y:\mathcal{U})}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:Y\to X)}}\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}etale}(g)\Big)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

If
g:Y\to X
is
{\ocircle}
-étale, then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}

is a pullback square. Therefore
g:Y\to X
is in the fiber of
\mathsf{etale\underline{\ }{}map}
at
{\ocircle}g:{\ocircle}Y\to{\ocircle}X
.

It remains to show that for any map
f:A\to{\ocircle}X
with modal domain, there is an equivalence
A\simeq{\ocircle}(X\times_{{\ocircle}X}A)
such that the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(X\times_{{\ocircle}X}A)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}(\mathsf{etale\underline{\ }{}map}(f))}

commutes. To see this, note that both
f\circ\pi_{2}
and
{\ocircle}(\mathsf{etale\underline{\ }{}map}(f))\circ{\eta}
factor the same map as a
{\ocircle}
-connected map followed by a modal map, so the claim follows from uniqueness of factorizations.
∎

Corollary 1.13
.

Suppose
P:X\to\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle}
is a family of modal types such that the projection map
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)\big)\to X
is
{\ocircle}
-étale. Then
P
has a unique extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{\ocircle}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{P}}

It follows that the square commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}P(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(t:{\ocircle}X)}}}{\sum_{(t:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(t:{\ocircle}X)}}{\sum_{(t:{\ocircle}X)}}\tilde{P}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}

is a pullback square. In particular the top map is
{\ocircle}
-connected, so this square is in fact a
{\ocircle}
-naturality square.

We conclude with a slightly more economic rephrasing of
1.8
, which is an easy corollary of the results in this section.

Theorem 1.14
.

Consider a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
H:f\circ p^{\prime}\sim p\circ g
, where
E
and
B
are modal types, where
p^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
is
{\ocircle}
-étale. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The square is a pullback square.

(ii)

The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

is a pullback square, where
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{g}
are the unique extensions of
f
and
g
along the modal units of
B^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
, respectively.

3

The reflective factorization system of a modality

In this subsection we investigate the reflective factorization system associated to a modality, of which the right class is the class of
{\ocircle}
-étale maps. The left class is the class of
{\ocircle}
-equivalences
.

Definition 1.15
.

We say that a map
f:A\to B
is an
{\ocircle}
-equivalence
if
Lf:{\ocircle}A\to{\ocircle}B
is an equivalence.

Remark 1.16
.

The difference between the notions of
{\ocircle}
-equivalences and
{\ocircle}
-connected maps is best explained by an example. In the case of
n
-truncation, the
n
-equivalences are precisely the maps that induce isomorphisms on the first
n
homotopy groups. The
n
-connected maps are the maps that induce isomorphisms on the first
n
homotopy groups, and moreover induce an epimorphism on the
(n+1)
-st homotopy group.

We also note that the
n
-equivalences are not stable under pullbacks, whereas the
n
-connected maps are. Consider for instance the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{\Omega}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{n+1}}

Here the map on the right is an
n
-equivalence, since
\mathbb{S}^{n+1}
is
n
-connected. However, the map on the left is not an
n
-equivalence, since the
n
-th homotopy group of
\mathsf{\Omega}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1})
is not trivial: it is the
(n+1)
-st homotopy group of
\mathbb{S}^{n+1}
, which is
\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 1.17
.

The
reflective factorization system
associated to a modality
{\ocircle}
consists of the
{\ocircle}
-equivalences as the left class, and the
{\ocircle}
-étale maps as the right class.

Our goal in this section is to show that the reflective factorization system associated to a modality is an orthogonal factorization system.

Lemma 1.18
.

The
{\ocircle}
-equivalences satisfy the 3-for-2 property: given a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

if any two of
f
,
g
, and
h
are
{\ocircle}
-equivalences, then so is the third.

Proof.

Apply
{\ocircle}
to the commuting triangle, and use the 3-for-2 property of equivalences.
∎

Lemma 1.19
.

For a map
f:A\to B
the following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is an
{\ocircle}
-equivalence.

(ii)

For any modal type
X
, the precomposition map

{f}^{\ast}:(B\to X)\to(A\to X)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Suppose first that
f
is an
{\ocircle}
-equivalence, and let
X
be
{\ocircle}
-modal. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{{\ocircle}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{{\ocircle}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\ocircle}f}^{\ast}}

commutes. In this square the two vertical maps are equivalences by the universal property of modalization, and the bottom map is an equivalence since
{\ocircle}f
is an equivalence. Therefore the map
{f}^{\ast}:X^{B}\to X^{A}
is an equivalence, as desired.

Conversely, assume that
{f}^{\ast}:X^{B}\to X^{A}
is an equivalence for every
{\ocircle}
-modal type
X
. By the square above it follows that
{{\ocircle}f}^{\ast}:X^{{\ocircle}B}\to X^{{\ocircle}A}
is an equivalence for every
{\ocircle}
-modal type
X
. The fiber of
{\ocircle}A^{{\ocircle}B}\to{\ocircle}A^{{\ocircle}A}
at
\mathsf{id}:{\ocircle}A\to{\ocircle}A
is contractible, so we obtain a retraction
g
of
{\ocircle}f
. To see that
g
is also a section observe that the fiber of
{\ocircle}B^{{\ocircle}B}\to{\ocircle}B^{{\ocircle}A}
at
{\ocircle}f
is contractible. This fiber contains
(\mathsf{id}_{{\ocircle}B},\mathsf{refl}_{{\ocircle}f})
. However, we also have an identification
p:{{\ocircle}f}^{\ast}({\ocircle}f\circ g)={\ocircle}f
, since

{{\ocircle}f}^{\ast}({\ocircle}f\circ g)\equiv({\ocircle}f\circ g)\circ{\ocircle}f\equiv{\ocircle}f\circ(g\circ{\ocircle}f)={\ocircle}f.

Therefore
({\ocircle}f\circ g,p)
is in the fiber of
{{\ocircle}f}^{\ast}:{\ocircle}B^{{\ocircle}B}\to{\ocircle}B^{{\ocircle}A}
at
{\ocircle}f
. By the contractibility of the fibers it follows that
({\ocircle}f\circ g,p)=(\mathsf{id}_{{\ocircle}B},\mathsf{refl}_{{\ocircle}f})
, so it follows that
{\ocircle}f\circ g=\mathsf{id}_{{\ocircle}B}
. In other words,
g
is both a retraction and a section of
{\ocircle}f
, so
{\ocircle}f
is an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 1.20
.

Every
{\ocircle}
-connected map is a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence.

Definition 1.21
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a map. We define

\displaystyle[A]_{f}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv B\times_{{\ocircle}B}{\ocircle}A

and we define the maps

\displaystyle\mathsf{et}(f)

\displaystyle:[A]_{f}\to B

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}:A\to[A]_{f}

by the universal property of pullbacks, as indicated in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A]_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{et}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ocircle}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta}}

Lemma 1.22
.

For every map
f:A\to B
, the map
\bar{\eta}:A\to[A]
is a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence, and the map
\mathsf{et}(f)
is
{\ocircle}
-étale.

Proof.

The map
\mathsf{et}(f)
is a pullback of a map between modal types, so it is
{\ocircle}
-étale by
Section

2
. Furthermore, the map
\pi_{2}:[A]\to{\ocircle}A
is a pullback of a
{\ocircle}
-connected map, so it is
{\ocircle}
-connected. It follows from
Section

3
that it is a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence. Since the modal unit
{\eta}:A\to{\ocircle}A
is also
{\ocircle}
-connected, and therefore a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence, we obtain by the 3-for-2 property of
{\ocircle}
-equivalences established in
Section

3
that the gap map is also a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence.
∎

Lemma 1.23
.

The class of
{\ocircle}
-equivalences is left orthogonal to the class of
{\ocircle}
-étale maps.

Proof.

We have to show that for every
{\ocircle}
-equivalence
i:A\to B
, and every
{\ocircle}
-étale map
f:X\to Y
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{A}}

is a pullback square. Consider the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\ocircle}X)^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\ocircle}X)^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\ocircle}Y)^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\ocircle}Y)^{A}}

In this cube the top and bottom squares are pullback by the assumption that
f
is
{\ocircle}
-étale and the fact that exponents of pullback squares are again pullback squares. Furthermore, the square in the front left is pullback, because the two vertical maps are equivalences by the assumption that
i:A\to B
is a
{\ocircle}
-equivalence. Therefore we conclude that the square in the back right is also a pullback square, as desired.
∎

Corollary 1.24
.

For any map
f:X\to Y
, the type of factorizations into a
{\ocircle}
-connected map followed by a
{\ocircle}
-étale map is contractible.

The class of
{\ocircle}
-étale morphisms into a given type
A
, thought of as objects of the slice category
\mathcal{U}/A
, form a reflective subuniverse in the following sense.

Theorem 1.25
.

Let
f:A\to X
be a map. Then the pre-composition function

\mathrm{hom}_{X}(\mathsf{et}(f),e)\to\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,e)

is an equivalence for every étale map
e:B\to X
.

Proof.

Let
e:B\to X
be a
{\ocircle}
-étale map. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{[A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{[A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\eta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\eta_{f}}

is a pullback square by
Sections

3
and
3
. Therefore we have a fiberwise equivalence

{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:[A]\to X}\mathsf{fib}_{e\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}(i)\to\mathsf{fib}_{e\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}(i\circ\eta_{f})

by
Section

3
. Now the claim follows, since we have a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{e\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fib}_{e\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}(i\circ\eta_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{hom}_{X}(\mathsf{et}(f),e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{hom}_{X}(f,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

with equivalences on both sides, for each
i:[A]\to X
.
∎

2

The reflective factorization system for the reflexive coequalizer

1

\Delta
-étale maps

Definition 2.1
.

Let
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be a morphism of reflexive graphs. We say that
f
is
\Delta
-étale
if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(f))}

is a pullback square of reflexive graphs. We write
\mathsf{is\underline{\ }{}etale}_{\Delta}(f)
for the proposition that
f
is
\Delta
-étale, and we also write
\mathcal{R}^{\Delta}
for the class of
\Delta
-étale morphisms of reflexive graphs.

Theorem 2.2
.

Consider a morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
of reflexive graphs. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The morphism
f
is a fibration in the sense of
Section

4
.

(ii)

The morphism
f
is
\Delta
-étale.

Proof.

Consider the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{A}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{A}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{B}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{A}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{B}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{B}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})}

If
f
is
\Delta
-étale, then the three parallel vertical squares are pullback squares, hence so are the two squares on the back left side. This shows that (ii) implies (i).

Now suppose that
f
is a fibration, or equivalently, that
f
is cartesian. Then the map
\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)
is the unique map such that the naturality squares are pullback squares. In particular,
f
is a
\Delta
-étale map.
∎

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a family of reflexive graphs over
\mathcal{A}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The family
\mathcal{B}
is equifibered.

(ii)

The morphism
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),\mathcal{A})
is
\Delta
-étale.

The following proposition is analogous to
Section

2
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

of reflexive graphs, and suppose that
f
is a fibration. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}({f^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)}

is again a pullback square.

Proof.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{constr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{constr}}

of reflexive graphs. Since
f
is assumed to be a fibration, we obtain by
2.2
that the square on the right is a pullback square. Furthermore, since the left square is a pullback square by assumption, it follows that the outer rectangle is again a pullback square. Hence the assertion follows from
4.11
.
∎

The following proposition is analogous to
Section

2
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

of reflexive graphs, and suppose that
f
is a fibration. Then
f^{\prime}
is a fibration.

Proof.

Consider the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})).}

Then the two squares in the front and the square in the back right are pullback squares, so it follows that the square in the back left is a pullback square.
∎

2

The reflective factorization system of discrete graphs

Definition 2.6
.

Let
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
be a morphism of reflexive graphs. We say that
f
is a
\Delta
-equivalence
if the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(f):\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})\to\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}

is an equivalence. We also write
\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}
for the class of
\Delta
-equivalences of reflexive graphs.

Lemma 2.7
.

The
\Delta
-equivalences satisfy the 3-for-2 property.
\square

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
be a morphism of reflexive graphs. The following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is a
\Delta
-equivalence.

(ii)

For any type
X
, the map

\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta(X))\to\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\Delta(X))

is an equivalence.

Proof.

We have a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}),X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}),X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},\Delta(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\Delta(X))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ f}

in which the two vertical maps are equivalences. Therefore it follows that if
\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)
is an equivalence, then so is the bottom map in the square. Conversely, if the bottom map in the square is an equivalence for every type
X
, then
\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)
is an equivalence for any type
X
, which implies by
Section

3
that
\mathsf{rcoeq}(f)
is an equivalence.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

Let
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
be a morphism of reflexive graphs. We define the morphisms

\displaystyle\mathsf{et}_{\Delta}(f)

\displaystyle:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B}^{\simeq},\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\mathsf{\eta}_{f}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\simeq})

by the universal property of pullbacks, as indicated in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{et}_{\Delta}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{constr}}

The morphism
\mathsf{et}_{\Delta}(f)
is called the
étale factor
of
f
, and the morphism
\eta_{f}
is called the
unit
of the étale factor.

Lemma 2.10
.

For any morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
, the unit
\eta_{f}
of the étale factor of
f
is a
\Delta
-equivalence.

Proof.

By
4.11
, it follows that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}^{\simeq})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})))}

is a pullback square. Since the bottom map is an equivalence of reflexive graphs, it follows that the top map is an equivalence of reflexive graphs. In other words, we have shown that the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}))}

is a
\Delta
-equivalence. Of course, the morphism
\mathsf{constr}:\mathcal{B}\to\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{B}))
is also a
\Delta
-equivalence, so the claim follows by the 3-for-2 property of
\Delta
-equivalences.
∎

Theorem 2.11
.

The pair
(\mathcal{L}^{\Delta},\mathcal{R}^{\Delta})
forms an orthogonal factorization system of reflexive graphs.

Proof.

Since any pullback of a morphism between discrete reflexive graphs is a fibration of reflexive graphs, it follows from
Section

2
that every morphism of reflexive graphs factors as a
\Delta
-equivalence followed by a
\Delta
-étale map. Therefore it remains to show that the class of
\Delta
-equivalences is left orthogonal to the class of
\Delta
-étale maps.

We have to show that for every
\Delta
-equivalence
i:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, and every
\Delta
-étale morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}}

is a pullback square. Consider the commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{X}))^{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{X}))^{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{Y}))^{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{Y}))^{\mathcal{A}}}

In this cube the top and bottom squares are pullback by the assumption that
f
is
\Delta
-étale and the fact that exponents of pullback squares are again pullback squares. Furthermore, the square in the front left is pullback, because the two vertical maps are equivalences by the assumption that
i:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a
\Delta
-equivalence. Therefore we conclude that the square in the back right is also a pullback square, as desired.
∎

Theorem 2.12
.

For any morphism
f:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
, the pre-composition map

\mathsf{rGph}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{et}_{\Delta}(f),e)\to\mathrm{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(f,e)

is an equivalence for every
\Delta
-étale map
e:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Analogous to
1.25
.
∎

3

Equifibrant replacement

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
be families of reflexive graphs over
\mathcal{A}
. We define the type

\mathsf{rGph}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})

of morphisms of reflexive graphs
over

\mathcal{A}
to consist of triples
({f}_{0},{f}_{1},\mathsf{rfx}_{f})
consisting of

\displaystyle{f}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle:{B}_{0}(x)\to{C}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle{f}_{1}(e)

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{u:{B}_{0}(x)}{v:{B}_{0}(y)}{B}_{1}(e,u,v)\to{C}_{1}(e,{f}_{0}(u),{f}_{0}(v))

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{f}(x)

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{u:{B}_{0}(x)}{f}_{1}(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,u))=\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{C}}(x,{f}_{0}(u))

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
be families of reflexive graphs over
\mathcal{A}
. Then there is an equivalence

\mathsf{rGph}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})))}}}{\sum_{(f:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})))}}{\sum_{(f:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})))}}{\sum_{(f:\mathsf{rGph}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})))}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}=\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ f.

Note that, given a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
, any family
B:\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{U}
determines an equifibered family
\mathsf{equifib\underline{\ }{}fam}(B)
over
\mathcal{A}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{equifib\underline{\ }{}fam}(B)}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv B(\mathsf{constr}_{0}(x))

\displaystyle{\mathsf{equifib\underline{\ }{}fam}(B)}_{1}(e)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{tr}_{B}(\mathsf{constr}_{1}(e))

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathsf{equifib\underline{\ }{}fam}(B)}(x,y)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{htpy\underline{\ }{}eq}(\mathsf{ap}_{\mathsf{tr}_{B}}(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathsf{constr}}(x)),y).

In other words,
\mathsf{equifib\underline{\ }{}fam}
is an operation that takes a family
B:\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{U}
to an equifibered family over
\mathcal{A}
. Moreover, it is not hard to see that

\mathsf{equifib\underline{\ }{}fam}:(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{U})\to\mathsf{equifib}(\mathcal{A})

is in fact an equivalence, where
\mathsf{equifib}(\mathcal{A})
is the type of all (small) equifibered families over
\mathcal{A}
. In the following definition we observe that any family over
\mathcal{A}
induces an equifibered family over
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a family of reflexive graphs over
\mathcal{A}
. We define the
equifibrant replacement

\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B})
of
\mathcal{B}
by

\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B})\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{equifib\underline{\ }{}fam}(\mathsf{fib}_{\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathsf{pr}_{1})}),

and we define the morphism
\eta_{\mathcal{B}}:\mathsf{rGph}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B}))
.

Theorem 2.16
.

Consider a family
\mathcal{B}
of reflexive graphs over
\mathcal{A}
, and let
\mathcal{E}
be an equifibered family over
\mathcal{A}
. Then the pre-composition operation

\mathsf{rGph}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B}),\mathcal{E})\to\mathsf{rGph}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E})

is an equivalence for every equifibered family
\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

First we observe that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathsf{pr}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{constr}}

is a pullback square of reflexive graphs. Thus, the map
p
is the étale factor of the morphism
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{A}
. Now the claim follows by
2.12
.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

By the universal property of the equifibrant replacement it follows that the family
{\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B})}_{0}
of vertices of the equifibrant replacement of
\mathcal{B}
can be seen as a ‘recursive’ higher inductive family of types. It comes equipped with

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\to{\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B})}_{0}(x)\simeq{\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B})}_{0}(y)

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{A}_{0}}\zeta(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(x))\sim\mathsf{id}_{{\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B})}_{0}(x)}

\displaystyle{\eta}_{0}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{A}_{0}}{B}_{0}(x)\to{\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B})}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle{\eta}_{1}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x,y:{A}_{0}}{e:{A}_{1}(x,y)}{u:{B}_{0}(x)}{v:{B}_{0}(y)}{B}_{1}(e,u,v)\to\zeta(e,u)=v

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\zeta}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:{A}_{0}}{u:{B}_{0}(x)}{\eta}_{1}(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(x),\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{B}}(u))=\epsilon(x,u).

In other words, the equifibrant replacement is the homotopy initial family of types over
{A}_{0}
with equivalences over the edges of
\mathcal{A}
(coherent with reflexivity) and a morphism
\eta:\mathsf{rGph}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},\mathsf{EqF}(\mathcal{B}))
.

4

Identity types of reflexive coequalizers

Definition 2.18
.

Consider a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
with a base point
a:A_{0}
. We define the
universal
\Delta
-bundle

\mathcal{E}(a)
over
\mathcal{A}
at
a
to be the equifibered family over
\mathcal{A}
corresponding to the equifibrant replacement of the morphism
a:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathbf{1},\mathcal{A})
corresponding to
a:A_{0}
.

Proposition 2.19
.

Consider a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
with a base point
a:A_{0}
. Then there are equivalences

{\mathcal{E}(a)}_{0}(x)\simeq(\mathsf{constr}_{0}(a)=\mathsf{constr}_{0}(x))

In particular, we have an equivalence
{\mathcal{E}(a)}_{0}(a)\simeq\mathsf{\Omega}(\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A}))
.

Proof.

Immediate from the definition of the equifibrant replacement.
∎

In the following theorem we establish the universal property of the identity type of
\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})
as the initial reflexive relation
R
on
{A}_{0}
with certain extra structure.

Theorem 2.20
.

Consider a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
, and let

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle:A_{0}\to A_{0}\to\mathcal{U},

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:A_{0}}R(x,x)

be a reflexive relation on
A_{0}
equipped with a ‘composition’ operation

\mu:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a,x,y:{A}_{0}}{A}_{1}(x,y)\to(R(a,x)\simeq R(a,y)).

satisfying the unit law

\displaystyle\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}_{\mu}

\displaystyle:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{a,x:{A}_{0}}{r:R(a,x)}\mu(\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{A}}(x),r)=r.

Then there is a unique extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A_{0},R,\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(\mathsf{constr}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mu}}

of the morphism
\bar{\mu}:\mathsf{rGph}(\mathcal{A},(A_{0},R,\rho))
consisting of

\displaystyle{\bar{\mu}}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{constr}_{0}

\displaystyle{\bar{\mu}}_{1}(e)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mu(e,\rho(x))

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\bar{\mu}}(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}_{\mu}(\rho(x)),

where
k(\mathsf{constr}_{0})
is the pre-kernel of the function
\mathsf{constr}_{0}:A_{0}\to\mathsf{rcoeq}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

The data
(R,\mu,\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}_{\mu})
equivalently gives for every
a:A
an equifibered family
\mathcal{B}(a)
over
\mathcal{A}
consisting of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}(a)}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv R(a,x)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}(a)}_{1}(e)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mu(e)

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfx}_{\mathcal{B}(a)}(x,r)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{left\underline{\ }{}unit}_{\mu}(r),

and the reflexivity term
\rho
provides for every
a:{A}_{0}
a term of type
{\mathcal{B}(a)}_{0}
. Thus we have by
2.16
for every
a:{A}_{0}
a unique morphism from the equifibrant replacement of
a:\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
over
\mathcal{A}
. Equivalently, we have a unique extension of
\bar{\mu}
along
\mathcal{A}\to k(\mathsf{constr}_{0})
as asserted.
∎

Corollary 2.21
.

The loop space of the suspension
\mathsf{\Sigma}X
of a pointed type
X
is the initial pointed type
Y
equipped with a pointed map

X\to_{\ast}(Y\simeq Y).

In particular, the loop space of the
(n+1)
-sphere is the initial pointed type
Y
equipped with a pointed map
\mathbb{S}^{n}\to(Y\simeq Y)
, or equivalently, an
(n+1)
-loop
\mathsf{\Omega}^{n+1}(\mathrm{BAut}(Y))
. Even more in particular, the loop space of the
2
-sphere is the initial pointed type
Y
equipped with a homotopy
\mathsf{id}_{Y}\sim\mathsf{id}_{Y}
.

Corollary 2.22
.

The type
\mathbb{Z}
if integers is the initial pointed type equipped with an automorphism.

3

Equifibrant replacement for other homotopy colimits

1

Equifibrant replacement for diagrams over graphs

Recall that the colimit of a diagram
\mathcal{D}
over a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
is simply the reflexive coequalizer of the reflexive graph
\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\tau:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
be a natural transformation of diagrams over a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
.

(i)

We say that
\tau
is a
weak equivalence
if it induces an equivalence

\mathsf{colim}(\tau):\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{D}).

We write
\mathcal{W}
for the class of weak equivalences.

(ii)

We say that
\tau
is
étale
if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{D}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\tau)}

is a pullback square of reflexive graphs. We write
\mathcal{R}
for the class of étale maps.

Theorem 3.2
.

A natural transformation
\tau:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
is étale if and only if it is cartesian.

Proof.

This follows directly by
2.2
and
5
.
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

The pair
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{R})
forms an orthogonal factorization system.

Proof.

This follows from
2.11
.
∎

4

Equifibrant replacement for sequential colimits

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a sequential family over
\mathcal{A}
, and consider
x:A_{n}
. We write
\mathcal{B}_{n}[x]
for the type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}(f_{n}(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+2}(f_{n+1}(f_{n}(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a sequential family over
\mathcal{A}
. We will define an equifibered sequential family
\square\mathcal{B}
over
\mathcal{A}
equipped with a morphism

\mathrm{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},\square\mathcal{B})

Construction.

We define

\square B:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to\mathcal{U}

by
\square B_{n}(x)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{colim}\mathcal{B}_{n}[x]
. Next, we have to construct a fiberwise equivalence

g_{\infty}:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{n:\mathbb{N}}{x:A_{n}}\square B_{n}(x)\simeq\square B_{n+1}(f_{n}(x))

Note that we have the natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{k+n}(f^{k}(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{(k+1)+n}(f^{k+1}(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{k+(n+1)}(f^{k}(f(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{(k+1)+(n+1)}(f^{k+1}(f(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Now we observe that there are identifications

(k+(n+1),f^{k}(f(x)))=((k+1)+n,f^{k+1}(x)),

which we may use to construct equivalences going up diagonally in each naturality square. An induction argument reveals that each square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{k+(n+1)}(f^{k}(f(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{(k+1)+(n+1)}(f^{k+1}(f(x)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{(k+1)+n}(f^{k+1}(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{(k+2)+n}(f^{k+2}(x))}

commutes, from which we obtain the desired equivalence.
1
1

1

A formalization of this argument appears in
https://github.com/cmu-phil/Spectral/blob/master/colimit/seq_colim.hlean

∎

Theorem 4.3
.

The equifibered family
\square\mathcal{B}
over
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the universal property that

\mathrm{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\square\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E})

is an equivalence for any equifibered family
\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

This fact is formalized in
[
14
]
. An ‘informalized’ proof is work in progress.
∎

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a sequential family over
\mathcal{A}
. Then we have a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}\Big(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A_{\infty})}}}{\sum_{(x:A_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(x:A_{\infty})}}B_{\infty}(x)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathsf{pr}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

in which the top map is an equivalence.

Proof.

Since
\square\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{EqF}\mathcal{B}
both satisfy the universal property of the equifibrant replacement of
\mathcal{B}
, they are the same sequences over
\mathcal{A}
. It follows that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\square\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{colim}(\Sigma(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}}

is a pullback square of type sequences. We conclude that the top arrow is a colimiting cocone, so the result follows.
∎

Corollary 4.5
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}

Then the canonical map

\mathsf{colim}(f^{n}(x)=f^{n}(y))\to(\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}_{0}(x)=\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}in}_{0}(y))

is an equivalence for every
x,y:A_{0}
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Consider a sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

of pullback squares. Then the sequential colimit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\infty}}

is again a pullback square.

Proof.

Since sequential colimits commute with
\Sigma
and identity types, they commute with pullbacks.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Consider a sequence of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

in which all maps and homotopies are assumed to be pointed. Then the colimit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\infty}\hookrightarrow E_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow B_{\infty}}

is again a fiber sequence.

Corollary 4.8
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

of pointed types (and pointed maps between them). Then the canonical map

\mathsf{colim}(\mathsf{\Omega}(A_{n}))\to\mathsf{\Omega}(A_{\infty})

is an equivalence.

Proposition 4.9
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

If each
A_{n}
is
k
-truncated, then so is the sequential colimit
A_{\infty}
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
k\geq-2
. The base case is trivial, and the inductive step follows since sequential colimits commute with identity types.
∎

Theorem 4.10
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Then the canonical map

\mathsf{colim}_{n}\|A_{n}\|_{k}\to\|A_{\infty}\|_{k}

is an equivalence.

Theorem 4.11
.

Consider a type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Then the canonical map

\mathsf{colim}_{n}(\pi_{k}(A_{n}))\to\pi_{k}(A_{\infty})

is a group isomorphism, for any
k\geq 1
.

Chapter 7

Compact types

In this chapter we introduce the notion of compact type, and we show that the compact types are closed under finite coproducts,
\Sigma
-types, finite products, pushouts, reflexive coequalizers, and retracts. In particular it follows that the spheres are compact.

In
Section

2
we show that if
P
and
Q
are families of compact types, then the subuniverse of
F
-local types is reflective. Of course, one might impose stronger assumptions on the type theory so that the subuniverse of
F
-local types is reflective for any family
F
of maps (see for instance
[
28
]
). The setting of this dissertation is that of univalent type theory in which the universes are closed under homotopy pushouts, so we have to find a different way to define localizations.

The idea is to approximate the
F
-localization of a type
X
by iterated ‘quasi
F
-local extensions’ of
X
, by which we mean a map
l:X\to Y
equipped with a diagonal filler for the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{P_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{P_{i}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}

for each
i:I
. We show in
Section

2
that, provided that
F
is a family of maps between compact types, the sequential colimit of type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{2}}

is
F
-local if each
l_{n}
has the structure of a quasi
F
-local extension. We show that there is an initial quasi
F
-local extension
l:X\to QL_{F}X
, for every type
X
, and in
2.10
we show that the sequential colimit
X\to QL_{F}^{\infty}
is an
F
-localization. We establish in
2.10
that if
F
is a family of maps between compact types, then the subuniverse of
F
-local types is a reflective subuniverse. Furthermore, if
A
is a family of compact types, then the subuniverse of
A
-null types is a modality.

Dan Christensen pointed out that this construction of
F
-localization already appears in section 1.B of
[
18
]
, where arbitrary localizations are constructed by iterating the initial quasi
F
-local extension transfinitely many times. We stick to the case
\omega
since larger ordinals are not yet as well understood in homotopy type theory. A minor note is that in Theorem B.5 Dror Farjoun only establishes that the transfinite colimit
QL_{F}^{\kappa}X
localizes the homotopy groups. Our proofs are therefore different than Dror Farjoun’s.

1

Compact types

Definition 1.1
.

Consider a type sequence
\mathcal{A}\equiv(A,f)
, and let
X
be a type. We define the type sequence
\mathcal{A}^{X}
to consist of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}

Furthermore, given a natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
of type sequences, we define the natural transformation
\tau^{X}
to consist of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{0}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{2}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}

where the naturality squares commute by whiskering the naturality squares of
\tau
.

In particular, if
\tau:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{A},\Delta Y)
is a cocone with vertex
Y
, then
\tau^{X}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{A}^{X},(\Delta Y)^{X})
is a cocone with vertex
Y^{X}
, since
(\Delta Y)^{X}\equiv\Delta(Y^{X})
.

Definition 1.2
.

We call a type
X

(sequentially) compact
if for any type sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

the map

\mathsf{seq\underline{\ }{}ind}(\mathsf{constr}(\mathcal{A})^{X}):\mathsf{colim}(X\to A_{n})\to(X\to A_{\infty})

is an equivalence.

Example 1.3
.

The empty type is compact, simply because
X^{\mathbf{0}}
is contractible for any type
X
, and a sequential colimit of contractible types is contractible.

Example 1.4
.

The unit type is compact because
{\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{const}_{x}:X\to X^{\mathbf{1}}
is an equivalence for any type
X
. Thus we obtain a natural equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}^{\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}^{\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}

Thus we obtain a commuting triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(A_{n}^{\mathbf{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{\mathbf{1}}.}

where two out of three maps are equivalences. Therefore the unit type is compact by the 3-for-2 property.

We will show in the remainder of this section that the compact types are closed under finite coproducts,
\Sigma
-types (and therefore also finite products), pushouts, reflexive coequalizers, and retracts. In the following example we note that compact types are not closed under pullbacks, and are not closed under exponentiation either.

Remark 1.5
.

The compact types are not closed under identity types, because
\mathbb{Z}
is not compact
and
\mathbb{Z}\simeq\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. To see that
\mathbb{Z}
is not compact, consider
the sequence
A_{n}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbb{N}_{-n}
, where
\mathbb{N}_{-n}
is a version of the natural
numbers of starting at
-n
and
A_{n}\to A_{n+1}
is the inclusion map. The colimit
of
A_{n}
is
\mathbb{Z}
and the image of the map
\mathsf{colim}(\mathbb{Z}\to A_{n})\to(\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z})
is
the set of maps that are bounded from below. Thus, it is not surjective.

Also, the compact types are not closed under exponentiation. We have
the equivalence
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{S}^{1})\simeq\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}
, which is not
compact.

Lemma 1.6
.

Finite coproducts of compact types are compact.

Proof.

We have already seen that the empty type is compact.
Thus it remains to show that compact types are closed under disjoint
sums.

Suppose that
X
and
Y
are compact. Then we have a commuting diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{X+Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{X}\times\mathcal{A}^{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{X})\times\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{X+Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{X}\times A_{\infty}^{Y}}

where all but one of the maps are equivalences. Thus the remaining map is an equivalence too.
∎

Definition 1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}:X\to\mathsf{Seq}
be an indexed type sequence. Then we define the type sequence
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathcal{A}(x)
to consist of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}A_{0}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}A_{1}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}h.\,{\lambda}x.\,f_{0}(x,h(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}h.\,{\lambda}x.\,f_{1}(x,h(x))}

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}:X\to\mathsf{Seq}
be an indexed type sequence, indexed by a compact type
X
.
Then the canonical map

\mathsf{colim}\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}A_{n}(x)\Big)\to\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}A_{\infty}(x)\Big)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Consider the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathcal{A}(x)\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}A_{\infty}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}\Big(X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}A_{n}(x)\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big(X\to\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}A_{\infty}(x)\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\to X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{const}_{\mathsf{id}_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}_{1}\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}

The square on the right and the outer rectangle are pullback squares. Hence the square on the left is a pullback square. Now observe that the type
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}A_{\infty}(X)
is the sequential colimit of the type sequence
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}A_{n}(x)
, by
Section

4
. Therefore it follows by the assumption that
X
is compact, that the bottom map in the left square is an equivalence. We conclude that the top map in the left square is an equivalence, proving the claim.
∎

Corollary 1.9
.

Suppose
X
is compact, and
Y(x)
is compact for each
x:X
. Then the total space
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Y(x)
is also compact.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a type sequence. Then we have the following commuting pentagon

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}\Big(\mathcal{A}^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Y(x)}\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Y(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}\Big({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathcal{A}^{Y(x)}\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}A_{\infty}^{Y(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{x:X}\mathsf{colim}\Big(\mathcal{A}^{Y(x)}\Big)}

in which all but the top map are known to be equivalences. Hence it follows that the top map is an equivalence, which shows that
\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}Y(x)
is compact.
∎

Corollary 1.10
.

Compact types are closed under finite products.

Proposition 1.11
.

Compact types are closed under pushouts.

Proof.

Consider a span
\mathcal{S}\equiv(S,f,g)
from
X
to
Y
, where
X
,
Y
, and
S
are assumed to be compact, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a type sequence.
Then we have the commuting pentagon

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}\Big(\mathcal{A}^{X\sqcup^{S}Y}\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{X\sqcup^{S}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}\Big(\mathcal{A}^{X}\times_{\mathcal{A}^{S}}\mathcal{A}^{Y}\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{X}\times_{A_{\infty}^{S}}A_{\infty}^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{X})\times_{\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{S})}\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{Y})}

In this diagram, the downwards maps from the top left and from the top right are equivalences by the universal property of pushouts. The downwards map to the left side to the bottom is an equivalence since sequential colimits preserve pullbacks by
4.6
. The map from the bottom to the right side is an equivalence by the assumption that
X
,
Y
, and
S
are all compact types. Therefore we conclude that the top map is an equivalence, which proves the claim.
∎

Corollary 1.12
.

Compact types are closed under joins, suspensions, and wedges. Furthermore, the cofiber of a map between compact types is again compact. In particular, all the spheres and the finite dimensional real and complex projective spaces are compact, and the smash product of compact pointed types is again compact.

Definition 1.13
.

A reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
is said to be compact its types of vertices and (the total space of) edges are compact.

Corollary 1.14
.

The reflexive coequalizer of a compact reflexive graph is again compact.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Sections

1
and
3
.
∎

Proposition 1.15
.

Compact types are closed under retracts.

Proof.

Suppose that
Y
is a retract of a compact type
X
, i.e. we have
i:Y\to X
and
r:X\to Y
such that
r\circ i=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
.
Note that for any type
Z
, the type
Z^{Y}
is a retract of
Z^{X}
, since we have the section-retraction pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{Y}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ i}

It follows that for any type sequence
\mathcal{A}
we have a section-retraction pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A}^{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty}^{Y}}

of morphisms. Note that the downward morphism in the middle is an equivalence by the assumption that
X
is compact. Recall from Theorem 4.7.4 of
[
33
]
that a retract of an equivalence is again an equivalence, so the claim follows.
∎

2

Localizing at maps between compact types

In this section, we assume to have
P,Q:I\to\mathcal{U}
and a family of maps
F:{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ D}{i:I}P(i)\to Q(i)
.

Recall type of diagonal fillers for a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

with
H:j\circ f\sim g\circ i
is defined to be the fiber of the gap map

\mathsf{gap}:B^{X}\to B^{A}\times_{Y^{A}}Y^{X}

at the point
(i,j,\mathsf{eq\underline{\ }{}htpy}(H))
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
quasi
F
-local extension
of
X
consists of a map
l:X\to Y
equipped with a diagonal filler for the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{P_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{P_{i}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}

for each
i:I
.

Lemma 2.2
.

For any map
l:X\to Y
into an
F
-local type
Y
, the type of diagonal fillers for the commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{P_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{P_{i}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\circ\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}}

is contractible. In particular, any such map is a quasi
F
-local extension.

Proof.

If
Y
is
F
-local, then the map
F_{i}^{\ast}:Y^{Q_{i}}\to Y^{P_{i}}
is an equivalence, for any
i:I
. Since equivalences are right orthogonal to any map, the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
h:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a natural transformation of type sequences equipped with the structure of a diagonal filler for each naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}

Then the map
h_{\infty}:A_{\infty}\to B_{\infty}
is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let us write
j_{n}:B_{n}\to A_{n+1}
for the diagonal fillers, which come equipped with homotopies

\displaystyle J_{n}

\displaystyle:f_{n}\sim j_{n}\circ h_{n}

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle:h_{n+1}\circ j_{n}\sim g_{n}

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle:(h_{n+1}\cdot J_{n})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(K_{n}\cdot h_{n})\sim H_{n}

Note that
j
is a natural transformation from
\mathcal{B}
to the shifted sequence
\mathsf{shift}(\mathcal{A})
, with the homotopies
(f_{n+1}\cdot K_{n})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(J_{n+1}\cdot g_{n}):f_{n+1}\circ j_{n}\sim j_{n+1}\circ g_{n}
filling the naturality squares.

We claim that the composite
j\circ h
is just the natural transformation
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{shift}(\mathcal{A})
. Indeed, we have the homotopy
J_{n}:j_{n}\circ h_{n}\sim f_{n}
. Moreover, the type of
j_{n+1}\cdot L_{n}

(j_{n+1}\cdot h_{n+1}\cdot J_{n})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(j_{n+1}\cdot K_{n}\cdot h_{n})\sim j_{n+1}\cdot H_{n}

is equivalent to the type of homotopies

\displaystyle(J_{n+1}\cdot f_{n})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(j_{n+1}\cdot h_{n+1}\cdot J_{n})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(j_{n+1}\cdot K_{n}\cdot h_{n})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(j_{n+1}\cdot H_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sim\mathsf{htpy\underline{\ }{}refl}_{(f_{n+1}\circ f_{n})}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\displaystyle\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\centerdot$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\scriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}{\mathbin{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\,\centerdot\,$}}}}(J_{n+1}\cdot f_{n}),

filling the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+2}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n+1}}

Therefore we obtain the coherence of the naturality squares needed to conclude that
f=j\circ h
as natural transformations.

Likewise, the composite
\mathsf{shift}(h)\circ j
is just the natural transformation
g:\mathcal{B}\to\mathsf{shift}(\mathcal{B})
. Therefore we obtain a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathsf{shift}(\mathcal{A}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathsf{shift}(\mathcal{B}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(\mathsf{shift}(h))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{colim}(j)}

Since both
\mathsf{colim}(f)
and
\mathsf{colim}(g)
are equivalences, it follows by the 6-for-2 property of equivalences (see Exercise 4.5 of
[
33
]
), that the remaining maps are equivalences. In particular
\mathsf{colim}(h)
is an equivalence.
∎

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
F
is a family of maps between compact types, and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

be a sequence of types in which each
X_{n+1}
is a quasi
F
-local extension of
X_{n}
. Then the sequential colimit
X_{\infty}
is
F
-local.

Proof.

We have a natural transformation
F_{i}^{\ast}:\mathsf{Seq}(\mathcal{X}^{Q_{i}},\mathcal{X}^{P_{i}})
for which each naturality square comes equipped with a diagonal filler

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}^{Q_{i}}}
